                                                              PRIME LABELINGS OF TREES
                                                                     Ho Dang Dung
                                                                              HSE University
                                                                             dkho@edu.hse.ru




arXiv:2610.02271v2 [math.CO] 5 Oct 2026
                                                                             30 September 2026

                                                  Abstract. A prime labeling of a graph labels its vertices bijectively
                                                  with the integers from one to the number of vertices, so that adjacent
                                                  vertices receive coprime labels. We prove the Entringer–Tout conjecture:
                                                  every tree has a prime labeling. Haxell, Pikhurko, and Taraz proved this
                                                  for all sufficiently large trees; our proof covers every order. The proof is
                                                  computer-assisted. Trees with at most 1359 vertices are handled by finite
                                                  certificates whose rules are proved sound. For larger trees, primes above
                                                  half the order label a small separator, the even labels are assigned to one
                                                  side of the remaining forest with prescribed numbers in each divisibility
                                                  class, and the odd labels are placed by a matching obtained from Hall’s
                                                  theorem. For these trees, the inequalities needed for this matching are
                                                  verified by exact rational computation when the natural logarithm of the
                                                  order is at most 256, and by an analytic argument otherwise. Most of
                                                  the proof development, computational verification, and drafting of this
                                                  manuscript were carried out by generative AI systems, principally GPT
                                                  Astra and Claude Opus, under the author’s direction; see the section on
                                                  use of generative AI for details.

                                           3
                               1. Introduction
  For a positive integer n, write [n] = {1, . . . , n}. A prime labeling of a
graph G of order n is a bijection f : V (G) → [n] such that
                gcd(f (u), f (v)) = 1       whenever uv ∈ E(G).
A graph with a prime labeling is called prime. Entringer conjectured around
1980 that every tree is prime. Haxell, Pikhurko, and Taraz [3, Section 1]
trace the conjecture to that time, find its earliest written statement in a 1982
paper of Tout, Dabboucy, and Howalla [9], and call it the Entringer–Tout
conjecture. They proved it for all sufficiently large trees [3, Theorem 1]. We
prove it for every order.
Theorem 1.1. For every integer n ≥ 1 and every tree T on n vertices, there
is a bijection f : V (T ) → [n] such that gcd(f (u), f (v)) = 1 for every edge uv
of T .
   The proof is computer-assisted. The structural reductions, the matching
criteria, and the soundness of every certificate check are proved in the text.
The finite certificate conditions are verified by exact integer and rational
computation. Section 10 completes the proof of Theorem 1.1, and Section 11
lists the computed conditions, the programs that verify them, and the
commands that reproduce the verification.

1.1. Main ideas and contributions
   The theorem of Haxell, Pikhurko, and Taraz is stated for all sufficiently
large trees, without an explicit threshold. They report an estimate of Spiess,
                                                                           100
based on an earlier version of their argument, that the threshold 1010 is a
suitable choice in their Theorem 1, and they note that closing the remaining
gap is beyond any small-order approach [3, Section 1]. The published finite
verifications reach orders 15, 34, and 50 [2, 5, 6]. Direct order-by-order
verification is not a viable way to close the gap between these ranges. The
intermediate orders are therefore treated here by arguments that are uniform
over long ranges of orders and whose constants are explicit enough to be
checked.
   We keep the special labels of Haxell, Pikhurko, and Taraz: primes above
n/2 label a small separator, and the labels 2p with n/3 < p ≤ n/2 are
coprime to all odd labels except p, so a final exchange removes the few pairs
p, 2p (Section 3.2). The quantitative work is organized differently, in four
ways.
    (1) Explicit preparation. Under explicit counting conditions on the order,
         a tree is reduced to a bipartite residual forest, a set of even labels for
         one side, a set of odd labels for the other, and a reserve of positions
         that accept every remaining odd label (Proposition 3.4.3). Special
         even labels are placed on vertices of largest degree, which bounds the
         residual degree. The prime counts in these conditions are computed
                                        4
        exactly for the orders through 1982759 and bounded by Dusart’s
        explicit estimates [1] for log n ≥ 29/2.
    (2) Exact quotas. Labels are grouped by their divisibility by small primes.
        The even labels are assigned to the vertices of the residual forest with
        exactly prescribed class sizes, and the resulting errors in degree sums
        and position tags are bounded deterministically (Proposition 4.3.5 and
        Theorem 7.1.4). Probabilistic arguments are confined to permutations
        within exact classes. Larger prime divisors are controlled by first-
        moment or exponential bounds for random permutations, and, from
        the fifth logarithmic bridge on and in the unbounded range, by sorting
        labels against degrees within these classes (Theorem 7.3.2).
    (3) Hall criteria with finitely many rational conditions. The odd labels
        are placed by a perfect matching. Several sufficient conditions for
        Hall’s condition are proved, each depending on a small number of
        normalized parameters (Theorems 4.4.7, 4.5.6, 7.2.2, and 9.3.3, and
        Lemma 9.6.2).
    (4) Certificates for ranges of orders. Orders through 1359 are covered by
        finite proof graphs for coprime labelings by general label sets, whose
        soundness is proved (Theorem 5.1.11). Integer intervals through order
        1982759 are covered by bounds that hold uniformly on each interval
        (Theorem 6.6.6 and its variants). The orders with 29/2 ≤ log n ≤ 256
        are covered by finitely many closed cells in the logarithm of the order
        and, for most bridges, a normalized degree, and the orders with
        log n ≥ 256 by an analytic argument (Theorem 7.7.2).
   Some tools may be of use elsewhere: the rounding of fractional type assign-
ments in forests with exact class sizes and bounded degree error (Section 4.3),
Hall criteria for type-compatibility graphs with a reserve of universal vertices
(Sections 4.5, 7.2, and 9.3), the certificate rules for coprime labelings by
arbitrary finite label sets (Section 5.1), and the directed rational evaluation
of probability bounds (Section 6.5).

1.2. Related work
  We recall the results most directly connected with the two parts of our
argument: reductions for finite label sets and embeddings for large orders.
For results on particular families and on other graph classes we refer to the
account in [3, Section 1].
1.2.1. Finite orders and particular families. Fu and Huang [2, Theorem 2.8]
proved that every tree of order at most 15 is prime. Pikhurko extended the
verified range to 34 vertices [5] and subsequently to 50 vertices [6, Theorem 5].
The latter two proofs proceed by explicit reductions that can be checked by
hand. Haxell, Pikhurko, and Taraz [3, Section 1] also mention the bound
206, claimed in an unpublished manuscript of Kuo and Fu. We have not
seen that manuscript, and the present proof does not use it. Removing a
suitable part of a tree may leave a nonconsecutive label set, so the induction
                                       5
must allow general finite sets of integers. This is also the reason for using
the predicate P(L) here.
  Results for particular families follow a complementary approach. Robert-
son and Small [7] extended coprime mapping results and used them to label
palm trees, banana trees, binomial trees, and certain spider colonies. Such
constructions exploit the structure of the chosen family; the reductions below
must instead apply to an arbitrary input tree.
1.2.2. Asymptotic embeddings. Let m(n) be the least integer m such that
every tree of order n has an injective coprime labeling using labels in [m].
Pikhurko proved that m(n) = (1 + o(1))n [6, Theorem 6]. Allowing unused
labels removes part of the difficulty of a spanning embedding. The prime
labeling conjecture requires the exact equality m(n) = n.
   Haxell, Pikhurko, and Taraz [3, Theorems 1 and 2] proved that all suffi-
ciently large trees are prime, as a consequence of a more general theorem for
bipartite degenerate graphs with suitable separators. Their proof separates a
structural partition, an arithmetic partition, and an embedding step. Primes
above n/2 and the labels 2p with n/3 < p ≤ n/2 provide the exceptional
labels needed at the separators; a final exchange removes the possible conflicts
between p and 2p.
1.2.3. Relation to the present argument. Our finite reductions continue the
approach of keeping track of the entire remaining label set. The large-order
construction retains the special-label and separator method just described. Its
quantitative requirements are expressed through exact type counts, prescribed
class sizes, degree sums, and matching inequalities. These quantities must
be controlled for every tree in each claimed order range.
   For rounding fractional type assignments, we use the sorted-slot matching
construction of Shmoys and Tardos [8]. Its application here preserves exact
class sizes and controls the change in degree sums; Section 4.3 gives the
required form with proof. Our local sampling bounds use Hoeffding’s convex
comparison between sampling with and without replacement [4, Theorem 4].
Section 6.1 includes a proof and derives the finite-population and forest-degree
forms used here.
   The arithmetic and structural statements proved below are the general
ingredients for that task. To deduce the all-orders statement from them,
we supply the finite reduction certificates through order 1359, verify the
parameter ranges for every larger order with log n ≤ 256, and prove the
estimates used in the unbounded range log n ≥ 256. Section 2 summarizes
these dependencies, and Sections 10 and 11 make them explicit.

1.3. Organization
   Section 2 describes the proof and lists the ranges of orders with the
results that cover them. Sections 3 and 4 contain the general structural,
arithmetic, and matching results. Sections 5, 6, 8, and 9 cover the orders

                                       6
with log n ≤ 256 in increasing order: certificates through order 1359, integer
intervals through 34999, integer bridges through 1982759, and logarithmic
bridges from log n = 29/2 to 256. Section 7 contains uniform estimates
and the argument for log n ≥ 256; it is placed before the bridges because
several logarithmic bridges use its tools. Section 10 proves Theorem 1.1, and
Section 11 describes the computations and their reproduction. Appendix A
lists the notation, and Appendix B contains the degree covers of the retained-
position certificates.

1.4. Notation
   All graphs are finite and simple. The order of a graph is its number of
vertices. We write V (G) and E(G) for its vertex and edge sets, e(G) = |E(G)|,
NG (v) for the S neighborhood of v, and dG (v) = |NG (v)|. For a vertex set X,
put NG (X) = x∈X NG (x). The notation G[X] means the induced subgraph
on X, and G − X = G[V (G) \ X]. A forest may have isolated vertices or be
empty. Set ∆(∅) = 0 and, for X ⊆ V (G), ∆G (X) = maxx∈X dG (x), with
value zero when X is empty. A graph is C4 -free if it contains no cycle of
length four, whether induced or not.
   For a finite set L of positive integers, a coprime labeling by L is a bijection
onto L with coprime labels on every edge. Write P(L) if every forest on
|L| vertices admits such a labeling, and Pd (L) if this holds for every such
forest of maximum degree at most d. In particular P(∅) and Pd (∅) hold.
A label q ∈ L is universal in L if it is coprime to every element of L \ {q}.
Universality always refers to the specified label set.
   We use log for the natural logarithm and log2 for the logarithm to base
two. For real x ≥ 0, π(x) denotes the number of primes at most x. All label
sets and vertex sets in disjoint unions are understood to be pairwise disjoint.
Further notation is introduced where it is first used; Appendix A lists the
symbols used in more than one section.

                        2. Overview of the proof
  The proof uses one reduction at every order, several ways of solving the
reduced problem, and a chain of ranges of orders, each covered by one of
these ways. This section describes the steps in the order of the argument
and lists the ranges.

2.1. The residual problem
   Since ⌊n/2⌋ labels are even, the vertices receiving them must form an
independent set. We remove a set K of at most b(n) = ⌊log2 (n − 2)⌋ + 2
vertices and split the rest of the tree into independent sets S and Z with
|S| = ⌊n/2⌋ (Proposition 3.3.2). If n ≥ 8 and (n/2, n] contains at least b(n)
primes, the vertices of K receive such primes (Corollary 3.3.3); each of them
is coprime to every other label in [n].

                                        7
   Two further families of labels have few conflicts (Lemma 3.2.3). The
special even labels are the powers of 2 and the labels 2p with p prime and
n/3 < p ≤ n/2; each is coprime to every odd label except possibly p. The
special odd labels are 1 and the primes above n/3 not used on K; each is
coprime to every even label except possibly its double. The pairs p, 2p are
allowed during the construction and removed at the end by an exchange
(Lemma 3.2.1 and Corollary 3.2.2).
   The special even labels are placed first, on a set U ⊆ S. It contains the
neighbors of a reserve W ⊆ Z of positions, so that every position of W
accepts every odd label, and it may contain vertices of largest degree, which
bounds the degrees that remain (Lemma 3.3.4 and Proposition 3.4.3). What
remains is a bipartite residual forest G = (S0 , Z0 ). Its even side must receive
the remaining even labels bijectively, and its positions must receive distinct
odd labels, coprime along the edges of G. Once the even labels are fixed, the
odd labels are placed by a matching, and the reserve reduces Hall’s condition
by the number of reserved positions (Corollary 3.4.4).

2.2. Types, quotas, and matchings
   Fix a set P of small odd primes. The type of an odd label is the set of
primes of P dividing it, and the type of an even label is that of its half. The
numbers N of labels of each type in an interval are compared with the product
law µ = p∈P Bernoulli(1/p) (Section 4.1). The even labels are assigned to
the vertices of S0 type by type, with exactly prescribed numbers of vertices
of each type. Every position of Z0 receives a tag, a set of primes containing
every prime of P that divides a neighboring even label; in some constructions
a position is instead marked, and it later receives a special odd label. The
assignment controls the distribution of the tags and the degree sums of the
classes (Sections 4.3 and 7.1).
   A label of type I can be placed at a position with tag J only if I ∩ J = ∅;
primes outside P may delete further pairs. Within each exact class the
even labels are then permuted, either at random or by sorting them against
the degrees of their vertices. The number of deleted pairs is bounded in
both cases (Sections 4.4 and 7.3). Hall criteria reduce the existence of the
matching to lower bounds on degrees in the compatibility graph and an upper
bound on the number of deleted pairs (Sections 4.4, 4.5, 7.2, 8.1, and 9.3).

2.3. How the ranges are covered
  Orders through 1359. These orders are small enough for finite certificates.
The assertion P([n]) is proved by finite proof graphs whose nodes are state-
ments P(L) about general label sets L. The rules of these graphs are proved
sound in Theorem 5.1.11, and a program checks that every rule is applied
correctly and every case list is complete. Branch peeling (Theorem 5.2.7)
and certified rooted blocks reduce the number of cases.

                                        8
   Integer intervals from 1360 through 1982759. For an interval [ℓ, u] of
orders, the preparation constants are bounded uniformly, and the largest
residual degree D takes one of finitely many values (Lemma 6.6.2). Each
value of D has a row specifying a construction and its parameters. The class
permutations are random. A finite family of events covers every failure of a
local degree condition, and the deleted pairs are bounded by a first-moment
or an exponential estimate. If the sum of the failure probabilities is less than
one, some permutation succeeds (Theorem 6.6.6, its variants with the Hall
estimates of Proposition 6.10.1 and Lemma 6.11.1, and Theorem 6.12.1). All
bounds are evaluated by directed rational arithmetic (Section 6.5). From
35000 the same scheme is used with a reserve on the label side (Section 8.1).
   Logarithmic bridges for 29/2 ≤ log n ≤ 256. Here the order enters only
through log n and, in the first fifteen bridges, a normalized residual degree.
All quantities are bounded monotonically on closed cells: rectangles in these
two coordinates for log n ≤ 48, and unit intervals of log n beyond. Each cell
carries a finite list of rational inequalities that imply the Hall conditions,
and the cells cover 29/2 ≤ log n ≤ 256. The nineteen bridges differ in
the preparation, the type assignment, the Hall criterion, or the arithmetic
bounds; Table 30 lists them.
   The unbounded range log n ≥ 256. The cutoff for the small primes grows
with log n, and every estimate is proved analytically, using monotonicity and
explicit comparisons at finitely many rational points, mostly at log n = 256
(Theorem 7.7.2).

2.4. The chain of ranges
   Table 1 lists the ranges. The direct result of a range treats the orders of
that range: it is a theorem, or a certificate proposition which, together with
its criterion, proves the assertion for these orders. A cumulative result states
the assertion for all orders up to an endpoint; its proof applies the direct
result to the new orders and the preceding cumulative result to the others.
For the logarithmic range, Theorem 9.21.1 instead collects the nineteen
bridges. Adjacent integer ranges share no order and omit none; the passage
to the logarithmic range uses log 1982759 < 29/2 < log 1982760.
   The cumulative results are joined in Section 10. Except for Theorems 5.8.4
and 7.7.2, each direct result above either is a certificate proposition, which
states that the finite conditions of a criterion hold, or combines such a
proposition with the criterion. The computations behind the certificate
propositions are described in Section 11.

            3. Structural reductions and special labels
   This part reduces the construction of a prime labeling to a matching
problem. Section 3.1 collects local reductions for coprime labelings by general
finite label sets; the finite certificates of Section 5 use them. Section 3.2
introduces the special labels and the exchange that removes the pairs p, 2p

                      estimates

(Lemma 3.2.1). Sections 3.3 and 3.4 choose the balanced independent set, the
separator, and the reserve, and state the residual problem (Proposition 3.4.3)
with its Hall condition (Corollary 3.4.4).

3.1. Local reductions for finite label sets
   Removing vertices from a tree usually leaves a forest. We therefore state
the extension rules for forests and record first why this does not enlarge the
terminal family that needs to be checked.
Lemma 3.1.1. Every nonempty forest can be extended to a tree on the same
vertex set by adding edges. If its maximum degree is at most d, where d ≥ 2,
the extension can be chosen to have maximum degree at most d.
Proof. Order the components as F1 , . . . , Fj . In each nontrivial component,
choose two distinct leaves, one as a left attachment vertex and one as a
right attachment vertex. In an isolated-vertex component use its sole vertex
for both roles. For i < j, join the right attachment vertex of Fi to the left
attachment vertex of Fi+1 .
   The resulting graph is connected and has one fewer edge than vertices, so
it is a tree. A selected leaf gains at most one edge, and an isolated vertex
gains at most two. All other degrees are unchanged. This proves both
assertions.                                                                 □
   Consequently, for a nonempty label set, P(L) is equivalent to the corre-
sponding assertion for trees. The same equivalence holds for Pd (L) when
d ≥ 2. A degree-restricted assertion is nevertheless distinct from P(L): a
labeling table omitting high-degree trees does not establish the latter.
                                      10
3.1.1. Deleting a leaf and its neighbor. The next lemma makes a two-vertex
deletion useful even when the available label set supports only a restricted
degree bound.
Lemma 3.1.2. Let T be a tree on m ≥ 4 vertices. There is a leaf x with
neighbor y such that
                                              m−1
                                                
                 ∆(T − {x, y}) ≤ min ∆(T ),          .
                                               2
Proof. Put h = ⌊(m − 1)/2⌋. If ∆(T ) ≤ h, any leaf and its neighbor satisfy
the conclusion. Otherwise choose a vertex y of degree s > h.
   We first show that y has a leaf neighbor. Suppose otherwise. Each of its
s neighbors has a further neighbor distinct from y. Since T is a tree, these
further vertices are distinct and lie outside {y} ∪ NT (y). Thus T has at least
1 + 2s > m vertices, a contradiction. Choose a leaf x adjacent to y.
   Let v ∈ V (T ) \ {x, y}. If v is adjacent to y, their neighborhoods are
disjoint: a common neighbor would form a triangle. Hence dT (v) + s ≤ m,
and deleting y gives
                           dT −{x,y} (v) ≤ m − 1 − s.
If v is not adjacent to y, both neighborhoods avoid v and y and intersect in
at most one vertex, since two common neighbors would form a four-cycle.
Thus dT (v) + s ≤ m − 1, giving the same bound.
   Finally, s ≥ h + 1 implies m − 1 − s ≤ m − 2 − h ≤ h. Deletion also
preserves the bound by ∆(T ).                                                □
Corollary 3.1.3. Let L have m ≥ 4 elements, and let q, a ∈ L be distinct,
with q universal in L. Let d ≥ 2 and let d′ be a nonnegative integer such that
                                         m−1
                                              
                            ′
                           d ≥ min d,              .
                                            2
Then Pd′ (L \ {q, a}) implies Pd (L).
Proof. Complete the given forest to a tree of maximum degree at most d by
Lemma 3.1.1, and choose x, y by Lemma 3.1.2. Label x by a and y by q. The
remaining forest has maximum degree at most d′ , so the hypothesis labels it
by L \ {q, a}. Every edge incident with y has coprime labels, and x has no
other neighbor. Restricting to the original forest proves the assertion.  □
3.1.2. Removing a block through one vertex. A larger deletion must control
both the size and the internal degree of the removed forest. For an integer
b ≥ 3, define
                   Kb = {b} ∪ {k ∈ 2Z : b < k ≤ 2b − 2}.
The even sizes in this set will allow arithmetic blocks to be assembled from
pairs of consecutive integers.
Lemma 3.1.4. Let b ≥ 3, and let T be a tree of order n ≥ b + 2. There are
a vertex u and a union A of components of T − u such that
                       |A| ∈ Kb ,    ∆(T [A]) ≤ b − 1.
                                      11
Proof. Root T at a leaf. Choose a vertex v of greatest depth whose rooted
subtree has at least b + 1 vertices. The root is not such a deepest vertex,
since its unique child has a subtree of order n − 1 ≥ b + 1. Thus v has a
parent, and every child subtree of v has at most b vertices.
   If some union of these child subtrees has order in Kb , take it as A and
put u = v. Each component of T [A] then has at most b vertices, giving the
degree bound.
   Suppose no such union exists. No child subtree has order b. Pair the
child subtrees of odd order, leaving at most one unpaired, and treat each
even-order child subtree as a separate group. Every group has even total
order at most 2b − 2. Its order must be less than b, or it would be an allowed
union. If the groups together had total order at least b, accumulating them
until first reaching b would give an even total between b and 2b − 2, again an
allowed union. Hence the total order g of all groups satisfies g ≤ b − 1.
   The child subtrees together have at least b vertices. There must therefore
be an unpaired odd-order subtree, of order a ≤ b − 1. The odd integer g + a
lies between b and 2b − 2, and so is at most 2b − 3. It follows that the whole
rooted subtree of v has even order g + a + 1 ∈ Kb . Take this subtree as A
and let u be the parent of v.
   It remains to check the degree at v. If r is its number of children, the
grouped subtrees contain r − 1 children, so r − 1 ≤ g ≤ b − 1. Equality
r = b would force all grouped child subtrees to be singletons. The unpaired
subtree, together with b − a of these b − 1 singletons, would then have order
b, a contradiction. Thus r ≤ b − 1. Every other vertex lies in a child subtree
of order at most b − 1; even its attachment vertex has degree at most b − 1
after adding the edge to v. This completes the proof.                        □
Proposition 3.1.5 (Extension through a universal label). Let |L| = n ≥ b+2,
where b ≥ 3. For every k ∈ Kb with k ≤ n − 1, suppose there is a partition
                     L = {qk } ⊔ Bk ⊔ Ck ,      |Bk | = k,
such that qk is universal in L, Pb−1 (Bk ) holds, and P(Ck ) holds. Then P(L)
holds.
Proof. By Lemma 3.1.1, it suffices to label a tree T of order n. Apply
Lemma 3.1.4 and put k = |A|. Give u the label qk , label T [A] by Bk , and
label T − (A ∪ {u}) by Ck . These assignments use every label exactly once.
Since A is a union of components of T − u, every edge between one of the
two forests and its exterior is incident with u. Universality of qk checks all
such edges.                                                                 □
   The partitions may depend on k. Thus a finite certificate needs a valid
arithmetic partition for each permitted size, while the structural lemma
applies to every input tree. No enumeration of all trees of order n is required
at this step.


                                      12
3.2. Exceptional pairs and special labels
  We temporarily relax coprimality for a small family of pairs. The next
lemma shows that a labeling obtained under this relaxation can be repaired
without changing which vertices carry even labels. It is a finite C4 -free
variant of the switching argument in [3, Section 4].
  For a finite set L of distinct positive integers, define
                            q ≥ 3 is odd, 2q ∈ L,
                                                                     
         Q(L) = q ∈ L :                                                   .
                            gcd(q, t) = 1 for every t ∈ L \ {q, 2q}
Given a bijection f : V (G) → L, call an edge uv bad if gcd(f (u), f (v)) > 1.
Lemma 3.2.1. Let G be a C4 -free graph, and let L be a set of |V (G)| distinct
positive integers containing 1 and two distinct powers of 2 greater than 1.
Suppose that f : V (G) → L is a bijection and that every bad edge has labels
q, 2q for some q ∈ Q(L). Then G has a bijective labeling by L in which every
edge has coprime labels. Moreover, the parity of the label at each vertex can
be preserved.
Proof. We show how to remove one bad edge without creating another.
Repeating this operation will prove the lemma.
   First observe that the vertices carrying even labels form an independent
set. An edge between two such vertices would be bad and would not have
the allowed form. This remains true after any exchanges that preserve parity
at each vertex.
   Fix a bad edge xy, with f (x) = q and f (y) = 2q. Let u be the vertex
carrying 1, and let vr , vs carry two distinct powers of 2 greater than 1,
denoted by r and s. These five vertices are distinct. By the definition of
Q(L), the only other label in L that is not coprime to q is 2q. Among odd
labels, the only one that is not coprime to 2q is q.
   Case 1: x is not adjacent to at least one of vr , vs . Suppose that xvr ∈
                                                                           / E(G).
Exchange the labels 2q and r. All neighbors of y and vr carry odd labels.
The new label at y is therefore coprime to every neighboring label. The new
label at vr can conflict only with q, which is at the nonadjacent vertex x.
Thus every affected edge is good.
   Case 2: x is adjacent to both vr and vs . The vertex u cannot be adjacent
to both: otherwise
                                  x, vr , u, vs , x
would be a cycle of length four. After interchanging the names r, s if necessary,
assume that uvr ∈ / E(G). Exchange q with 1, and 2q with r. The new labels
at x and y are 1 and a power of 2, respectively, so their incident edges are
good. The only possible conflict involving the new label q at u is with the
new label 2q at vr ; these vertices are not adjacent. Conversely, all neighbors
of vr have odd labels, so q is its only possible conflicting label. All affected
edges are again good.
   In both cases the exchanges preserve the label set and the parity at every
vertex. The chosen bad edge disappears, and no new bad edge is introduced.
                                       13
                  x             u                     x                 u
                  q             1                     1                 q



           2q     s      r                     r      s        2q
           y      vs     vr                    y      vs       vr

          Figure 1. The second case of Lemma 3.2.1. Vertex names
          are unchanged; the entries inside vertices are labels. Solid
          segments are edges required in this case, and the dashed
          segment marks the chosen nonedge uvr . Other adjacencies
          are not specified. The exchanges move q and 2q to nonadjacent
          vertices.

The number of bad edges is a nonnegative integer and strictly decreases. The
procedure therefore terminates with the required labeling.                □

Corollary 3.2.2. Under the assumptions on G and L in Lemma 3.2.1,
suppose that every bad edge has labels p, 2p, where p is an odd prime and
3p > max L. Then the labeling can be repaired while preserving parity at
every vertex.
Proof. For each such pair, the only multiples of p in L are p and 2p. Since p
is prime, it follows that p ∈ Q(L). Apply Lemma 3.2.1.                     □
3.2.1. The arithmetic families. Fix n ≥ 8 and put
                En = {2, 4, . . . , 2⌊n/2⌋}, On = [n] \ En ,
                  Pn+ = {p : n/2 < p ≤ n, p prime},
                  Pn0 = {p : n/3 < p ≤ n/2, p prime}.
For distinct a, b ∈ [n], write a ∼n b if either gcd(a, b) = 1 or {a, b} = {p, 2p}
for some p ∈ Pn0 . We call this relation relaxed compatibility. Corollary 3.2.2
shows that a bijective labeling of a tree by [n] satisfying this relation on
every edge can be repaired to a prime labeling. The labels 1, 2, 4 required
for the repair are present.
Lemma 3.2.3. Let n ≥ 8, let Psep ⊆ Pn+ , and put O = On \ Psep . Define
(3.2.1)         E∗ = {2j : j ≥ 1, 2j ≤ n} ∪ {2p : p ∈ Pn0 },
                                                                    
(3.2.2)         O∗ = {1} ∪ {p : n/3 < p ≤ n, p prime} \ Psep .
Then the following hold.
   (1) Every label in Psep is universal in [n].
   (2) Every label in E∗ is relaxed-compatible with every label in O.
   (3) Every label in O∗ is relaxed-compatible with every label in En .

                                        14
Moreover, the two families in (3.2.1) are disjoint, and
(3.2.3)           w := |E∗ | = ⌊log2 n⌋ + π(n/2) − π(n/3),
(3.2.4)           u := |O∗ | = 1 + π(n) − π(n/3) − |Psep |.
Proof. A prime p > n/2 has no other positive multiple in [n], proving the
first assertion. A power of two is coprime to every odd number. If p ∈ Pn0 ,
the only multiples of p in [n] are p and 2p; thus p is the only odd label
that can conflict with 2p, and this pair is allowed. This proves the second
assertion. The same observation, together with the universality of 1 and of
primes greater than n/2, proves the third.
   Since n ≥ 8, every prime greater than n/3 is odd. Therefore none of
the labels 2p in (3.2.1) is a power of two, and all labels in (3.2.2) are odd.
Counting the indicated primes gives (3.2.3) and (3.2.4).                    □
   The even family is universal only against odd labels, and only for the
relaxed relation. We will assign it to an independent vertex set and invoke
the repair lemma after every label has been placed.

3.3. Balanced independent sets and a reserve
   We need an independent set of exactly ⌊n/2⌋ vertices, with an independent
complement after deleting a small separator. A centroid ordering lets us
change a two-coloring one vertex at a time while keeping all conflicts inside
a set of logarithmic size.
3.3.1. An ordering with a small boundary. For an ordering v1 , . . . , vN of a
graph G, define its boundary after position j by
              ∂j = {vi : i ≤ j, NG (vi ) ∩ {vj+1 , . . . , vN } ̸= ∅}.
In particular ∂0 = ∂N = ∅.
Lemma 3.3.1. Every forest on N ≥ 1 vertices has an ordering for which
                      |∂j | ≤ ⌊log2 N ⌋        (0 ≤ j ≤ N ).
Proof. First consider a tree. We recall why it has a vertex whose deletion
leaves components of order at most N/2. Starting at any vertex, move to
the neighbor in a component of order greater than N/2, if such a component
exists. After crossing an edge in this way, the component on the side just left
has order less than N/2, so that edge cannot be crossed back. A walk in a tree
that revisits a vertex must backtrack along an edge. The procedure therefore
terminates, at a vertex with the required property. Call it a centroid.
   Put the centroid first, and then list the components of its deletion con-
secutively, applying the same rule within each component. We prove the
bound by induction on N . For N = 1 it is immediate. During the processing
of a component C, only the centroid can contribute to the boundary from
outside C. Since |C| ≤ ⌊N/2⌋, the inductive bound is at most
                         1 + ⌊log2 ⌊N/2⌋⌋ ≤ ⌊log2 N ⌋.
                                          15
Cuts between components have boundary of size at most one. Finally, for a
forest process its components consecutively. Completed components have no
neighbors in those still unprocessed, so the same bound applies.       □
Proposition 3.3.2 (Prescribing an independent side). Let T be a tree of
order n ≥ 3, let r be a specified leaf, and let a, n − a be the sizes of its
bipartition classes. For every integer t between a and n − a, inclusive, there
is a partition
                              V (T ) = S ⊔ Z ⊔ K
such that S and Z are independent, r ∈ S, |S| = t, and
(3.3.1)              1 ≤ |K| ≤ b(n) := ⌊log2 (n − 2)⌋ + 2.
The neighbor of r belongs to K.
Proof. Let A, B be the bipartition classes with r ∈ A, and write |A| = a.
Let u be the neighbor of r. Keep r in S and u in K throughout. Initially
put S = A, Z = B \ {u}, and K = {u}.
   Order the vertices of F = T − {r, u} by Lemma 3.3.1. Process them in
this order. To process a vertex, first move it from its current class to K.
Then, one at a time, release every processed vertex having no unprocessed
neighbor in F , placing it in the class opposite its original class. A released
vertex is not moved again.
   At each stage, unprocessed vertices retain their original classes and released
vertices have exchanged classes. There is no edge between these two sets: such
an edge would have prevented the processed endpoint from being released.
Edges within either set remain properly two-colored, and the other vertices
are in K. Edges incident with u cause no problem, and r has no neighbor
other than u. Thus S and Z remain independent throughout.
   After all releases at a given position, K \ {u} is exactly the processed
boundary in F . Immediately after adding the next vertex, its size has
increased by at most one. Lemma 3.3.1 therefore gives (3.3.1) at every
intermediate stage.
   Initially |S| = a. At the end every vertex of F has changed class, so
                    S = {r} ∪ (B \ {u}),       |S| = n − a.
Each individual move changes |S| by at most one. Hence it attains every
integer between these endpoints. Stop when it first attains t.       □
Corollary 3.3.3. Let n ≥ 8 and suppose
(3.3.2)                     π(n) − π(n/2) ≥ b(n).
Every tree T of order n, with a specified leaf r, admits a partition V (T ) =
S ⊔ Z ⊔ K satisfying Proposition 3.3.2 with |S| = m := ⌊n/2⌋. The vertices
of K can be labeled bijectively by a set Psep ⊆ Pn+ so that every incident edge
will have coprime labels, whatever distinct remaining labels are subsequently


                                       16
assigned. The remaining label sets are En for S and O = On \ Psep for Z,
and
(3.3.3)        |Z| = q = ⌈n/2⌉ − |K|,        m − b(n) ≤ q ≤ m.
Moreover, r is isolated in T − K.
Proof. The integer m lies between the two bipartition sizes, so apply Propo-
sition 3.3.2 with t = m. Condition (3.3.2) supplies |K| distinct primes in
Pn+ . They are universal by Lemma 3.2.3. They are also odd, so all m even
labels remain available for S. Counting the unused odd labels gives (3.3.3),
using 1 ≤ |K| ≤ b(n). The final assertion follows because the neighbor of r
lies in K.                                                                □
3.3.2. Choosing positions with few neighbors. The same ordering gives more
than a separator. It also finds many positions whose combined neighborhood
is small. This is more economical than bounding the degree of each position
separately.
Lemma 3.3.4 (Cyclic neighborhood bound). Let G be a bipartite graph
with parts A, B, where |A| = M and |B| = Q > 0. Suppose G has a vertex
ordering with |∂j | ≤ t at every cut, where t is a nonnegative integer. For
every integer h with 1 ≤ h ≤ Q − t, there is a set W ⊆ B such that
                                             M (h + t)
                                                      
(3.3.4)          |W | = h,     |NG (W )| ≤               + t.
                                                 Q
Proof. Read the given ordering cyclically. Divide it into Q blocks, each
ending at a vertex of B and containing all vertices after the preceding vertex
of B. Each block contains exactly one vertex of B; the blocks together
contain all M vertices of A.
   Put ℓ = h + t. Among the Q cyclic intervals consisting of ℓ consecutive
blocks, each vertex of A occurs exactly ℓ times. Thus some interval I satisfies
                                               Mℓ
                                                 
                                  |A ∩ I| ≤        .
                                               Q
Let a, b denote its initial and final cuts in the original linear ordering. Remove
from B ∩ I every vertex in ∂b . Since |B ∩ I| = ℓ and |∂b | ≤ t, at least h
vertices remain; choose any h of them as W .
   We claim that
(3.3.5)                  NG (W ) ⊆ (A ∩ I) ∪ (A ∩ ∂a ).
For an interval not crossing the end of the ordering, an external neighbor
before I lies in ∂a . An external neighbor after I would put its endpoint in
W into ∂b , contrary to the choice of W .
  If I crosses the end, it consists of a terminal segment and an initial segment.
A vertex of W in the initial segment cannot have a neighbor in the intervening
complement, because it was excluded from ∂b . An external neighbor of a
vertex in the terminal segment belongs to ∂a . Finally, if ℓ = Q, the interval

                                        17
is the whole vertex set and has no external neighbors. This proves (3.3.5) in
every case, and hence (3.3.4).                                             □
  For a forest one may take t = ⌊log2 (M + Q)⌋ by Lemma 3.3.1. When
M ≤ Q, the bound becomes |NG (W )| ≤ h + 2t. Thus reserving h positions
costs only an additional logarithmic number of vertices on the other side.

3.4. Reduction to a matching problem
   We now combine the structural and arithmetic preparations. The con-
clusion will be conditional only on labeling a smaller bipartite forest. In
particular, all edges outside that forest and every unused label will be
accounted for explicitly.
3.4.1. The graph of available positions. Suppose a partial labeling of a graph
leaves an independent set X of unlabeled vertices and a set L of unused
labels, with |X| = |L| = q. Assume every edge with both endpoints already
labeled satisfies the required compatibility relation. Form a bipartite graph
H with parts X, L by putting xλ ∈ E(H) precisely when λ is compatible
with the label of every neighbor of x. A perfect matching in H is exactly
a completion of the partial labeling. There are no edges inside X, so no
additional conditions are needed.
   We use the following form of the matching criterion, including its proof to
make the subsequent reduction self-contained.
Lemma 3.4.1 (Hall’s criterion). A bipartite graph H with parts X, L of the
same finite size has a perfect matching if and only if
(3.4.1)              |NH (Q)| ≥ |Q|         for every Q ⊆ L.
Proof. Necessity follows because the matched partners of Q are distinct
members of NH (Q). For sufficiency, let M be a matching of maximum size.
Suppose some label λ ∈ L is unmatched. Starting at λ, follow nonmatching
edges from L to X and matching edges from X to L. If an unmatched
vertex of X is reached, a shortest such directed path alternates between
nonmatching and matching edges. Exchanging the two kinds of edge along
the path increases |M |, which is impossible.
   Let Q ⊆ L and Y ⊆ X be the reachable sets. Every vertex of Y is matched,
and its matched partner belongs to Q \ {λ}. Conversely, every vertex of
Q \ {λ} is reached along its matching edge. These edges give a bijection
between Y and Q \ {λ}. Every neighbor of Q lies in Y : nonmatching edges
can be followed, and the matching neighbor of a reachable label was already
reached. Thus NH (Q) = Y and |NH (Q)| = |Q| − 1, contradicting (3.4.1). □
   For later use, a Hall obstruction can equivalently be written as a pair Q ⊆ L,
Y ⊆ X with no edges between them and |Q| + |Y | > q. Indeed, a deficient Q
gives Y = X \ NH (Q); conversely, any such Y forces |NH (Q)| ≤ q − |Y | < |Q|.
We will refer to the absent pairs Q × Y as an empty rectangle.

                                       18
Lemma 3.4.2 (Matching with a reserve). Let H have parts X, L with
|X| = |L| = q. Suppose W ⊆ X and R ⊆ L, of sizes h and u, satisfy
                      W × L ⊆ E(H),            X × R ⊆ E(H).
Put H0 = H[X \ W, L \ R]. The following are equivalent:
   (1) H has a perfect matching;
   (2) H0 has a matching of size at least max{0, q − h − u};
   (3) |NH0 (Q)| ≥ |Q| − h for every Q ⊆ L \ R.
Proof. A perfect matching has at most h + u edges incident with W ∪ R,
which proves the second assertion from the first.
   Conversely, let a matching in H0 have size v ≥ q − h − u. It leaves
q − h − v ≤ u unmatched vertices of X \ W ; match them to distinct labels
in R. It also leaves q − u − v ≤ h unmatched labels of L \ R; match them
to distinct vertices of W . The numbers still unused in W and R are both
h + u + v − q, and they can be matched arbitrarily. This produces a perfect
matching in H.
   Finally, any set of labels meeting R has neighborhood X and satisfies
Hall’s condition automatically. For nonempty Q ⊆ L \ R, its neighborhood
in H is the disjoint union of W and NH0 (Q). Lemma 3.4.1 therefore proves
the equivalence of the first and third assertions. The empty set satisfies the
third assertion as well.                                                    □
3.4.2. Preparing the residual forest. Use the partition and labels supplied by
Corollary 3.3.3, and let F = T − K, with parts S, Z. Retain the notation
                |S| = m,      |Z| = q,   E = En ,   O = On \ Psep ,
and let E∗ , O∗ , w, u be as in Lemma 3.2.3. For this construction q > 0:
indeed Z has as many vertices as there are odd labels outside Psep , and the
label 1 is outside Psep .
Proposition 3.4.3 (Residual labeling problem). Choose integers a, h such
that 0 ≤ a ≤ w, h ≥ 1, and, on putting t = ⌊log2 (m − a + q)⌋,
                               (m − a)(h + t)
                                                   
(3.4.2)          h + t ≤ q,                    + t ≤ w − a.
                                     q
Choose also an integer d with 0 ≤ d ≤ min{u, q − h}. There are sets U ⊆ S
and disjoint sets W, D ⊆ Z, and a set R ⊆ O∗ , with
          |U | = w,    |W | = h,         |D| = |R| = d,     NF (W ) ⊆ U,
such that, on setting
              S0 = S \ U,  Z0 = Z \ (W ∪ D),
(3.4.3)                                                   G = F [S0 ∪ Z0 ],
             E0 = E \ E∗ , O0 = O \ R,




                                          19
one has
(3.4.4)         |E0 | = |S0 | = m − w,         |O0 | = |Z0 | + h = q − d,
                          m+q−1                          m+q−1
                                                                 
(3.4.5)     ∆G (S0 ) ≤               ,    ∆G (Z0 ) ≤                  .
                             a+1                            d+1
Every bijection f0 : S0 → E0 and injection g0 : Z0 → O0 satisfying
(3.4.6)         gcd(f0 (s), g0 (z)) = 1        for every sz ∈ E(G)
can be converted into a prime labeling of T .
Proof. Step 1: Reserve the positions and the even labels. Choose A ⊆ S
consisting of a vertices of largest degree in F , breaking ties arbitrarily. Apply
Lemmas 3.3.1 and 3.3.4 to F − A, whose parts have sizes m − a and q.
Condition (3.4.2) gives a set W ⊆ Z with |W | = h and |NF −A (W )| ≤ w − a.
Thus A ∪ NF (W ) has at most w vertices. Enlarge it to a set U ⊆ S of size
w, and assign the labels E∗ bijectively to U .
  Step 2: Place some of the universal odd labels. Among the vertices of
Z \ W , choose d of largest degree in F and call the resulting set D. Choose
any d labels R ⊆ O∗ , and label D bijectively by R. The imposed bounds on
d make both choices possible. This construction is illustrated in Figure 2.
  Step 3: Check the residual parameters. The cardinalities in (3.4.4) follow
by subtraction. For s ∈ S0 , the vertices of A all have degree at least dF (s).
Since s ∈/ A and each edge of F has exactly one endpoint in S,
                                 X
               (a + 1)dF (s) ≤         dF (v) = e(F ) ≤ m + q − 1.
                                 v∈S
Likewise, for z ∈ Z0 , the d vertices of D all have degree at least dF (z), and
                                 X
               (d + 1)dF (z) ≤         dF (v) = e(F ) ≤ m + q − 1.
                                 v∈Z
Restricting to G cannot increase degrees. These inequalities prove (3.4.5); if
a side is empty, its maximum degree is zero by convention.
   Step 4: Restore all labels and edges. Suppose f0 , g0 satisfy (3.4.6). Exactly
h labels of O0 remain unused; assign them bijectively to W . Together with
the labels on K, U, D, this uses every element of [n] exactly once.
   Edges incident with K have coprime labels by Corollary 3.3.3. In F , edges
incident with U have relaxed-compatible labels by Lemma 3.2.3(2), and edges
incident with D have relaxed-compatible labels by part (3) of that lemma.
Every edge incident with W has its other endpoint in U . All remaining
edges lie in G and satisfy (3.4.6). Thus every edge of T satisfies the relaxed
relation. Corollary 3.2.2 repairs the labeling, proving the assertion.         □

Corollary 3.4.4. In Proposition 3.4.3, fix a bijection f0 : S0 → E0 . Form
the bipartite graph H0 with parts Z0 , O0 , where zλ is an edge if
                   gcd(f0 (s), λ) = 1     for every s ∈ NG (z).


                                          20

                U ←→ E∗                         W : h reserved positions

          Figure 2. The construction in Proposition 3.4.3. The boxes
          partition the two sides of F = T − K. Lines indicate possible
          classes of edges, not complete bipartite graphs. There are
          no edges from S0 to W . Only the edges between S0 and Z0

There is an injection g0 satisfying (3.4.6) if and only if
(3.4.7)            |NH0 (Q)| ≥ |Q| − h        for every Q ⊆ O0 .
Whenever this condition holds, T has a prime labeling.
Proof. Add h new vertices to the Z0 side of H0 , each adjacent to all labels in
O0 . The resulting graph has equal side sizes by (3.4.4). An injection labeling
all of Z0 extends to a perfect matching by assigning the h unused labels to
the new vertices; conversely, a perfect matching restricts to such an injection.
For a nonempty set Q of labels, its neighborhood in the enlarged graph has
size h + |NH0 (Q)|. Lemma 3.4.1 therefore gives (3.4.7). The last assertion
follows from Proposition 3.4.3.                                               □
  This identifies the remaining task for the matching part of the proof:
choose the even-label bijection so that every set of odd labels satisfies (3.4.7).
The prime supply (3.3.2), the reserve budget (3.4.2), and these simultaneous
matching inequalities are separate obligations. In later sections they must
be established for the parameter ranges in which this reduction is used.

                4. Arithmetic types and Hall estimates
  This part develops the arithmetic and matching tools used for all orders
beyond the finite certificates. Sections 4.1 and 4.2 define the arithmetic types
over a set of small primes, compare the type counts of an interval with the
product law, and bound the compatible type laws. Section 4.3 assigns the
even labels to the residual forest with exact class sizes (Proposition 4.3.5).
Sections 4.4 and 4.5 give Hall criteria for the type-restricted matching
(Theorems 4.4.7 and 4.5.6).



                                         21
4.1. Arithmetic types and their distributions
  The matching graph is controlled in two stages. We first exclude common
small prime divisors by assigning types to vertices. Common larger prime
divisors are then counted as deletions from that initial compatibility graph.
This section supplies the arithmetic information for both stages.
4.1.1. Exact counts and the product law. Let P be a finite set of odd primes.

   For completeness, the residues modulo the distinct primes of P vary
independently over a full period. Indeed the map from residues modulo Π
to their tuple of residues modulo the primes is injective: if two residues
have the same tuple, their difference is divisible by each prime and hence by
Π. Domain and codomain both have Π elements, so the map is a bijection.
There is one zero residue and p − 1 nonzero residues modulo p, giving (4.1.1).
The map x 7→ 2x − 1 is also a permutation modulo Π. This proves the same
period count for the odd sequence.                                           □

4.1.3. Removing special labels. Deleting labels changes the reference law.
The following exact identity keeps this change separate from arithmetic
discrepancy.
Lemma 4.1.5 (Depletion of the empty type). Let a population of M objects
have type law η, and remove w < M objects, all of empty type. Put ξ = w/M .

                            η − ξδ∅            µ − ξδ∅

                                          ∥η − µ∥TV

Proof. The removed mass is ξδ∅ in each numerator. Subtracting the two laws
leaves (η − µ)/(1 − ξ); now take the sum of absolute values. Both displayed
laws are probability measures by the assumptions on the removed objects
and on α.                                                                □
Corollary 4.1.6 (Exact residual quotas). Use the construction of Proposi-
tion 3.4.3. Let 3 ≤ z ≤ n/3, and let P in the present section be the set of
odd primes at most z, partitioned into blocks as above. To distinguish it from
the separator label set, denote that label set here by Psep and put k0 = |Psep |.
   Writing an even label as 2j, the numbers of available even half-labels and
odd labels of block type I are respectively
(4.1.11)            aI = C̄I (m) − w1I=∅ ,
(4.1.12)            oI = C̄I (n) − C̄I (m) − (k0 + d)1I=∅ ,

Proof. A special even half-label is a power of two or a prime greater than
n/3. Neither has an odd prime divisor at most z, so each has empty type.
Subtracting them from [m] proves (4.1.11).
  Among [n], the even labels have exactly the type counts of [m]. Hence
the initial odd counts are C̄I (n) − C̄I (m). The separator labels are primes
greater than n/2, and the d other placed special odd labels belong to O∗ :
each is 1 or a prime greater than n/3. They are distinct and all have empty
type. Their removal proves (4.1.12) and the two total counts.              □
  For a positive residual even population, its empirical law is aI /(m −
w). Lemma 4.1.5 compares it to (µ̄ − ξδ∅ )/(1 − ξ) when ξ = w/m ≤ α.
Alternatively, the exact quotas can be used directly, avoiding a product-law
approximation. An empty population requires no such normalization.

4.2. Compatible type laws and rectangle bounds
  The arithmetic estimates compare the label populations with a product
law. We now show how that law supplies compatible pairs. Two distinct
tools will be useful: a coupling supported on disjoint types, and an exact
bound using only the compatibility degrees. Both concern weighted type
populations and are independent of the forest construction.
4.2.1. A coupling supported on disjoint types. In this subsection let µ denote
a product law on 2[s] with coordinate probabilities q1 , . . . , qs , where s ≥ 1
and 0 < qj < 1/2. This is the block law of (4.1.7), with the bar suppressed.

                                        30
4.2.2. Using an upper bound on depletion. A numerical interval may use a
common upper bound ω on the actual removed proportion ξ. Replacing ξ by
ω is justified by monotonicity of compatibility, without adding a discrepancy
term for this replacement.
Lemma 4.2.3. Let 0 ≤ ξ ≤ ω < α. For every submeasure g ≤ νξ there is a
submeasure ge ≤ νω of the same mass such that, for every nonnegative vector
f,
(4.2.10)                                C(f, ge) ≤ C(f, g).
Proof. Put t = (ω − ξ)/(1 − ξ). Direct substitution gives
                                   νξ = (1 − t)νω + tδ∅ .
Thus a type of law νω , independently replaced by the empty type with
probability t, has law νξ . Let K(I, J) be this transition probability. It
vanishes unless J = I or J = ∅. Put hJ = gJ /νξ (J), which belongs to [0, 1],
and define                            X
                         geI = νω (I)   K(I, J)hJ .
                                                     J
                                                                        P           P
This is bounded by νω , and summing over I gives I geI = J gJ .
   Whenever K(I, J) > 0, disjointness from I implies disjointness from
J, since J ⊆ I. Multiply this indicator inequality by the nonnegative
quantities fA νω (I)K(I, J)hJ and sum over A, I, J. The resulting inequality
is (4.2.10).                                                              □
   This lemma transfers a lower bound proved for the more depleted model
to every less depleted model. If a correlation bound is used, its additional
hypothesis ω < γ must still be checked.

that lie in [τ − V, 1/2 − V ], with 1 ≤ i ≤ r + 1 for Pi and 1 ≤ i ≤ r for

Proof. For fixed s, the coefficients (ai − s − 2V )+ are nondecreasing in i. If
a feasible vector assigns positive mass to a later type while an earlier type
is not full, moving the smaller of the available amounts to the earlier type
cannot increase the objective. Repeating this operation produces a minimizer
                                          32
that fills the types in order, with at most one partially filled type. Thus,
when Pi ≤ s ≤ Pi+1 ,
                     X
          FL (s) =         µj (aj − s − 2V )+ + (s − Pi )(ai − s − 2V )+ .
                     j<i

Between consecutive points in (4.2.14), both the partial index and the signs
inside the positive parts are fixed. Every full-type term is affine or zero. The
partial term is either zero or a quadratic with coefficient −1 on s2 . Therefore
FL is concave on each such interval, and its minimum there occurs at an
endpoint. The formula is continuous across the endpoints. This proves the
claim, including ties among the ai and the case when the prescribed interval
consists of one point.                                                        □
   To apply this to the block model, take the label law to be µ and the position
law to be νω , with ω < α. The degrees are then aI = (c(I) − ω)/(1 − ω) and
bI = c(I) by (4.2.9). Lemma 4.2.3 justifies a common depletion upper bound.
If the coupling estimate is also applicable, both lower bounds are available
and the larger may be used.
   For a matching graph with ℓ labels and ℓ positions, normalize type counts
by ℓ. Then ℓ2 C(f, g) is the number of compatible pairs in the selected
rectangle before any large-prime deletions. In particular, a rectangle covered
by Proposition 4.2.4 cannot become empty if the number of deleted pairs is
strictly less than ℓ2 W (V, τ ). This addresses the range in which both selected
sides have mass at least τ . Smaller sides require the separate degree estimates
in the later matching argument.

4.3. Assigning types to a forest
   The arithmetic counts prescribe how many even half-labels belong to each
type. We must place these types on the even side of the residual forest and
control which odd labels are allowed at the opposite vertices. We give a
construction in which every separating vertex lies on the opposite side. This
preserves the even quotas exactly during separation. Two additive quantities
suffice: the number of even positions and the sum of their degrees.
4.3.1. Completion and fractional assignments. It is sometimes useful to add
edges before constructing the assignment. The following criterion records
the cost of making the forest connected.
Lemma 4.3.1 (Bipartite completion). Let G = (A, B) be a bipartite forest
with both parts nonempty, and let D ≥ 1 be an integer. If ∆G (A) ≤ D and
                                D|A| ≥ |A| + |B| − 1,
then G is contained in a tree H on the same vertices with the same bipartition
and ∆H (A) ≤ D.
Proof. Join each isolated vertex of A to any vertex of B. These edges create
no cycle and respect the degree bound. Every resulting component contains
                                           33
a vertex of B. If there are c > 1 components, their total number of edges is
|A| + |B| − c. The unused degree capacity on A is therefore at least c − 1 > 0.
Choose an A-vertex with unused capacity and join it to a B-vertex in a
different component. This reduces both the unused capacity and c − 1 by
one. Repetition joins all components without a cycle or a violation of the
degree bound.                                                               □
   We will temporarily give a connected piece several colors with fractional
weights. A piece is pure if one color has weight one, and mixed otherwise.
The next elementary reduction bounds the number of pieces that still need
to be split.
Lemma 4.3.2 (Fractional pruning). Let d ≥ 0 and g ≥ 1 be integers, and let
finitely
P
         many objects have vectors vj ∈ Rd . Suppose wji ≥ 0, 1 ≤ i ≤ g, and
   i wji = 1 for each object j. The weights can be changed so as to preserve
every vector sum j wji vj and leave at most d(g − 1) mixed objects. Objects
                   P

that are already pure may be kept fixed. If the data are rational, the changed
weights may be taken rational.
Proof. Freeze the pure objects and suppose that f > d(g − 1) mixed objects
remain. Each has at least two positive weights. Vary only these positive
entries, subject to preserving the f row sums and the d coordinates of the
vector sum for each of the first g − 1 colors. There are more than f + d(g − 1)
variables and at most that many homogeneous equations, so a nonzero
direction exists. The vector sum of the last color is also preserved, because
all row sums are fixed.
   A nonzero direction preserving row sums has a negative coordinate. Move
in that direction until the first positive weight becomes zero. All weights
remain nonnegative, and their row sums remain one. No previously zero
entry becomes positive. Freeze any newly pure objects and repeat if more
than d(g − 1) mixed objects remain. Each step strictly reduces the number of
positive entries, so the process ends. For rational data, Gaussian elimination
supplies a rational direction, and the step to the first zero is rational as
well.                                                                        □
4.3.2. Rounding the last assignments. Once each mixed piece contains only
one even position, its color can be rounded. Exact class sizes alone do not
control the sums of degrees; we use the ordering of those degrees to obtain
that control. The construction uses the sorted slots and bipartite matching
of Shmoys and Tardos [8, Section 2]. We give the exact-quota form and the
two-sided error estimate needed here.

   To obtain the last assertion, choose a perfect matching minimizing the
sum of dj assigned to slots of color i∗ . Its cost is at most the fractional cost.
Here is a direct justification. Starting with y, choose a perfect matching in
its positive support and subtract the least entry on that matching from all its
edges. The row and column sums decrease by the same amount, and at least
one positive entry vanishes. If the common sum is still positive, the same
mass argument gives another perfect matching. Repetition ends after finitely
many steps and expresses y as a convex combination of perfect matching
matrices. A minimum-cost matching has cost at most their weighted average.
Thus ϵi∗ ≤ 0, while the already proved bounds remain valid. The empty case
is immediate.                                                                  □
4.3.3. Separating from one side. We measure the size of a piece by its number
of A-vertices. A piece with just one such vertex is already suitable for the
preceding rounding lemma, regardless of the number of B-vertices it contains.
                                            35
Lemma 4.3.4 (A weighted one-sided cut). Let U = (AU , BU ) be a bipartite
tree with m = |AU | ≥ 2 and ∆U (AU ) ≤ D, where D ≥ 2. Put ρ = (D − 1)/D.
There is a vertex b ∈ BU such that every component V of U − b satisfies
(4.3.2)                         |V ∩ AU | − 1 ≤ ρ(m − 1).
Proof. Choose a vertex c minimizing a∈AU distU (c, a). Each branch of U −c
                                                P

contains at most m/2 vertices of AU : otherwise a move into a branch with
s > m/2 such vertices changes the sum by m − 2s < 0. If c ∈ BU , take b = c.
Every resulting component then satisfies |V ∩ AU | − 1 ≤ m/2 − 1 ≤ ρ(m − 1).
  Suppose instead that c ∈ AU . Its branches have total AU -count m − 1 and
there are at most D of them. Let s be the largest branch count, and let b
be the neighbor of c in such a branch. Then (m − 1)/D ≤ s ≤ m/2. The
component of U − b containing c has AU -count m − s, so its count minus
one is at most ρ(m − 1). Every other component lies in the chosen branch
and has count minus one at most s − 1 ≤ m/2 − 1. This proves (4.3.2) in
both cases.                                                               □
   For integers M ≥ 1, d ≥ 0, and 0 < ρ < 1, define

tions at different component sizes.

a set C ⊆ B such that
    (1) exactly ai vertices have color i;
    (2) every vertex of B \ C has neighbors of a single color;
    (3) |C| ≤ S(M, 2(g − 1); ρ);
    (4) on writing

(4.3.4)                        Hdeg :=          |hi | ≤ D⌊g/2⌋.
                                            2 i
For one specified color i∗ , we may also require hi∗ ≤ 0.
Proof. Step 1: Preserve the two measures. Initially regard the whole tree as
one object with color weights πi . For a connected object U , use the measure
vector                                               !
                                                      X
                          v(U ) =      |U ∩ A|,               dH (a) .
                                                     a∈U ∩A
                                                36
The degrees are always those of the fixed tree H, including edges to vertices
removed later. The total vector assigned to color i is initially (ai , eπi ).
   Apply Lemma 4.3.2 and freeze every pure object. While a mixed object
contains at least two A-vertices, choose one with largest |U ∩ A| − 1 and cut
it at a vertex of B given by Lemma 4.3.4. Put that vertex in C, replace the
object by the components of its deletion, and give each child the parent’s
color weights. The cut vertex contributes zero to both measures, so all target
vectors remain unchanged. Prune again, keeping frozen objects fixed. After
every pruning there are at most d = 2(g − 1) mixed objects.
   Each cut removes a new vertex. The process therefore terminates. Every
remaining component contains an A-vertex: an undeleted B-vertex retains all
its A-neighbors. At termination each mixed object has exactly one A-vertex.
The objects, together with C, partition the vertices. Every edge whose
endpoints are outside C lies within one object.
   Step 2: Count the cuts. For a nonterminal object put L(U ) = |U ∩ A| − 1.
Its bucket is the unique integer j ≥ 0 for which
                           ρ−j ≤ L(U ) < ρ−(j+1) .
Each child has L at most ρL(U ), so it lies below its parent’s bucket if it
is nonterminal. When the first cut in bucket j is made, no mixed object
remains in a higher bucket, by the choice of a largest object. There are
at most d mixed objects in bucket j at that moment, and no later cut
can create another. The objects cut in that bucket are disjoint, and each
contains at least ⌈ρ−j ⌉ + 1 vertices of A. Their number is at most both d
and ⌊M/(⌈ρ−j ⌉ + 1)⌋. Summing gives the asserted bound on |C|.
   Step 3: Round and check the edges. Keep the colors of the frozen objects.
Their contributions to each A-quota are integers. The fractional quotas on
the mixed terminal objects are therefore nonnegative integers as well. Apply
Lemma 4.3.3 to their single A-vertices, with weights dH (a) ∈ [0, D] and the
requested preferred color. The quotas remain exact. Since the degree targets
before rounding were eπi , its error bounds give all assertions in part (4),
including the preferred sign.
   A frozen component is monochromatic on A. A terminal mixed component
contains just one A-vertex, so rounding its color also makes it monochromatic
on A. Every b ∈ B \ C and all its neighbors lie in one such component. Thus
part (2) survives rounding. If M = 1 or g = 1, the same construction ends
without cuts and the preceding conclusions still apply.                    □
4.3.4. Recovering the position tags. The construction did not preserve B-
counts. Connectedness allows us to recover them from the degree sums. For
vectors of counts with equal total mass, use the unnormalized variation
                                    1X
                         tv(x, y) =      |xi − yi |.
                                    2 i
Dividing by their positive common mass gives the total variation between
the corresponding probability laws.
                                     37
Lemma 4.3.6 (Integral recovery of tags). Let H = (A, B) be a tree with
both parts nonempty. Let A be colored with quotas ai , put M = |A|, N = |B|,
πi = ai /M , and use the quantities Ei , hi , Hdeg above. Suppose C ⊆ B and
every vertex of B \ C has neighbors of a single color. All vertices of B can
be given color tags, agreeing with that forced color outside C, such that their
count vector b satisfies
(4.3.5)                tv(b, N π) ≤ Hdeg + 1 − max πi .
                                                            i

Proof. Every A-degree is positive, so  x = Ei − ai is a nonnegative integer.
                                     P i
Since e(H) = M + N − 1, we have i xi = N − 1. Choose j maximizing aj
and set bi = xi + 1i=j . These are nonnegative integers with total N .
   Suppose C ̸= ∅. Every component of H − C meets C by an edge. Its A-
vertices have one color, because the path between any two of them alternates
through undeleted B-vertices. For color i, let fi be the number of B-vertices
in those components, ti their number of components, and eiC their number
of edges to C. The tree edge identity gives
                            Ei = ai + fi − ti + eiC .
Each component contributes at least one edge to C, and these edges have
their other endpoint in A. Thus eiC ≥ ti and fi ≤ Ei − ai = xi ≤ bi . All
forced tags fit below the chosen counts, so the vertices of C can fill the
remaining deficits.
   If C = ∅, connectedness implies that all A-vertices have one color. The
chosen j is that color, and b assigns it to every vertex of B, as required.
   Finally, in either case,
                          bi − N πi = hi + 1i=j − πi .
The half sum of the absolute values of the last two terms is 1 − πj . The
triangle inequality proves (4.3.5).                                    □
   At separating vertices the tag can instead record all neighboring types.
This retains the positions for matching and pays for them through the tag
distribution.

separating positions introduces no uncounted large-prime deletions.
Corollary 4.3.8 (Type assignment with retained positions). Let G = (A, B)
be a bipartite forest with M = |A| > 0 and N = |B| > 0. Suppose D ≥ 2 is
an integer, ∆G (A) ≤ D, and DM ≥       M + N − 1. Prescribe an integer quota
aI ≥ 0 for every type I ⊆ [s], with I aI = M . Put g = 2s , πI = aI /M , and
                                     P

ρ = (D − 1)/D.
   There are a completing tree H, a type assignment I(a) with these exact
quotas, and tags J ′ (b) at every vertex of B such that I(a) ⊆ J ′ (b) on every
edge of G. Their count vector b′ satisfies
(4.3.9)       tv(b′ , N π) ≤ D⌊g/2⌋ + 1 − max πI + S(M, 2(g − 1); ρ).
                                                   I
The degree errors in H satisfy (4.3.4) and |hI | ≤ D; one specified error may
be required to be nonpositive.

                                          39
Proof. Complete G by Lemma 4.3.1 and apply Proposition 4.3.5 to H. In-
terpret its colors as the g subsets of [s]. Lemma 4.3.6 assigns tags at every
B-vertex with error at most Hdeg + 1 − maxI πI . Outside the resulting
separator C, a tag agrees with all neighboring types in H, hence also in
G. Apply Lemma 4.3.7 to G with Z = C. Its variation cost is at most |C|,
giving (4.3.9). The degree assertions are those of Proposition 4.3.5 on H. □
   The completion and the original forest have different degree sums. The
distributional error in this corollary is measured using H, where e(H) =
M + N − 1. The deterministic deletion bounds of Lemma 4.3.7 may use the
original degrees in G. Any later estimate that replaces these by completion
degrees is an upper bound and must retain the completion’s larger edge
count. Sharper separator constructions and alternative treatments of rare
types require their own bounds. The one-sided construction proved here
supplies the underlying quota and tag arguments.

4.4. Matching estimates
   After the types have been placed, the remaining choice is a permutation
of the labels within each type. The initial matching graph is fixed by the
types and tags. Only conflicts involving larger primes depend on these
permutations. We first bound these deletions, then compare them with the
rectangles required by Hall’s criterion.
   Throughout this section, let G = (A, B) be a bipartite graph and let Y
be a set of odd labels, where |B| = |Y | = R > 0. Partition all odd primes at
most z ≥ 3 into blocks as in Section 4.1. Each vertex a ∈ A has a type I(a),
and each b ∈ B has a tag J(b) satisfying
(4.4.1)            I(a) ⊆ J(b)       whenever ab ∈ E(G).
For each type I, let AI be the set of vertices of that type and let ΛI be a set
of available even half-labels of the same type, with
                              |AI | = |ΛI | = aI .
The sets ΛI are disjoint and contain positive integers. Choose a uniform
bijection j : AI → ΛI for each nonempty class, independently across classes,
and give a the even label 2j(a). All types, tags, and vertex sets are fixed
before this choice. Classes of size zero are omitted from sums involving
averages. Write
                                   X                          X
           da = dG (a),     EI =          da ,   e = e(G) =       EI ,
                                   a∈AI                       I
and fix a real bound D > 0 with da ≤ D for every a.
   Let Γ0 have parts Y, B, with yb an edge when T (y) ∩ J(b) = ∅. Obtain Γ
by deleting the edges for which y is not coprime to every even label adjacent
to b in G. Thus every edge of Γ is an available assignment, although some


                                      40
available assignments may have been excluded by the block types. A perfect
matching in Γ is sufficient for the labeling problem. Let
                               Z = |E(Γ0 ) \ E(Γ)|.
When applying this setup to Corollary 3.4.4, include the h additional positions
as isolated vertices on the B side of G, with empty tags. They are adjacent
to every label in both matching graphs. If βI counts the tags on the original
positions, the enlarged position law is (βI + h1I=∅ )/R. Both type laws and
their variation bounds must refer to this enlarged side size R.
4.4.1. Class permutations. Sampling without replacement gives the moment
bound needed for both individual labels and the total number of deletions.
We prove it for arbitrary nonnegative costs.

consists of odd integers in [n] and all half-labels lie in [m], where m = ⌊n/2⌋.

Proof. Multiplication by two preserves the block type. Thus OI (v) counts
the odd positive integers at most v whose types are disjoint from I. For
a prime p > z, multiplication by p preserves both the block type and
oddness. The number of such odd labels in [n] divisible by p is therefore
OI (⌊n/p⌋). Every label counted by rI (j) shares with j at least one prime
greater than z; summing over these primes can only overcount it. This proves
Qn,I (j) ≥ rI (j).
   Interchange the sums over j and its prime divisors. The number of j ≤ m
of type I divisible by p > z is C̄I (⌊m/p⌋). This gives (4.4.7). All counts
increase when n increases, proving the last assertion.                    □
  The formula concerns the complete prefix. Means for the random per-
mutations must use the actual sets ΛI . If 3 ≤ z ≤ n/3, a removed special
half-label has empty type: powers of two have cost zero, while a prime p
with n/3 < p ≤ n/2 has cost one. Indeed the only odd multiple of such a
prime in [n] is p itself. This permits exact subtraction for those removals.
Other removals must be accounted for separately.

Proof. Each half-label in a class is equally likely at each vertex of that class,

advance and bound its expression; the infimum need not be computed. The
same argument bounds deletions incident with a specified subset of Y when
the costs are defined using that subset.
4.4.4. Excluding Hall obstructions. The local estimates exclude small sides
of an empty rectangle. The remaining rectangles have a uniform positive
supply of compatible pairs, provided by Section 4.2.
Proposition 4.4.6 (Matching from a profile bound). Let Γ0 be the graph
of a type compatibility relation on parts Y, B of size R > 0, and let Γ be a
spanning subgraph of Γ0 . Let µb, νb be the empirical type laws on these parts.
Suppose reference probability laws µ, ν satisfy
           ∥µ
            b − µ∥TV ≤ V,      ∥νb − ν∥TV ≤ V,       0 ≤ V < τ ≤ 1/2.
Let W (V, τ ) be the profile bound of (4.2.13) for this relation and these
reference laws. If every vertex of Γ has degree at least τ R and
(4.4.10)                |E(Γ0 ) \ E(Γ)| < R2 W (V, τ ),
then Γ has a perfect matching.
Proof. Suppose otherwise. By Lemma 3.4.1, there is a nonempty U ⊆ Y
with |NΓ (U )| < |U |. Put X = B \ NΓ (U ). Then U × X has no edges of Γ
and |U | + |X| > R.
    If |U | ≤ τ R, any vertex in U has at least τ R ≥ |U | neighbors, a contra-
diction. If |X| ≤ τ R, any vertex in X has all its neighbors outside U , and
hence has degree at most R − |U | < |X| ≤ τ R, again a contradiction. Thus
both U and X have size greater than τ R.
    Let fb, gb be their type counts divided by R. They are submeasures of
µ
b, νb, their masses sum to more than one, and both masses exceed τ . By
Proposition 4.2.4, the number of Γ0 -edges between U and X is at least
R2 W (V, τ ). All of those edges were deleted, as illustrated in Figure 4,
contradicting (4.4.10).                                                      □

4.4.5. A deterministic bound for selected positions. Bounding the total num-
ber of deleted pairs can be wasteful for a rectangle with few positions. The
forest structure gives a bound depending on the mass of those positions. It
holds for every permutation, so it introduces no further failure event.
Lemma 4.4.8 (Weighted forest bound). Let G = (A, B) be a bipartite forest
with ∆G (A) ≤ D, where D ≥ 1 is an integer. Give each b ∈ B a weight
