   This last bound can replace a global probability estimate for a specified
range of Hall rectangles. To do so, its dependence on v must be compared
with a compatible-pair lower bound at the same rectangle masses. It is
not a single bound on Z at an arbitrarily chosen value of v. The sharper
treatment of exceptional label classes and mass-restricted rectangles will use
this distinction.




                                           48
4.5. Hall inequalities with type restrictions
   A deficient set in the matching graph need not have an arbitrary type
distribution. Degree bounds exclude some label types from small sets, and
a position type present in the complementary set imposes an arithmetic
restriction on every selected label. We use these restrictions to replace a
single rectangle bound by several smaller minimizations.
   We retain the setting of Section 4.4. In particular, Γ ⊆ Γ0 has parts Y, B
of size R > 0, and adjacency in Γ0 means disjointness of the label type and
the position tag. Write T = 2[s] , where s ≥ 1, and abbreviate the empty and
full types by 0 = ∅ and F = [s]. Throughout this section,
                              C(I, J) = 1I∩J=∅ .
A universal label means a vertex of Y adjacent to every position in Γ, after
the actual coprimality deletions.
4.5.1. Degree targets and deficient sets. The least numerous types may be
given weaker degree targets. What matters is their population and the degree
of each label, not the number of events used to prove those degree bounds.
Lemma 4.5.1 (Three ranges of deficient sets). Let
               0 ≤ ε < τ ≤ η ≤ γ,          u ≥ 0,        γ + u ≤ 1.
Suppose E ⊆ Y consists of full-type labels, |E| ≤ εR, and the following strict
bounds hold:                 
                             
                             
                             
                               εR, y ∈ E,
                             τ R, T (y) = F, y ∈
                             
                                                   / E,
                    dΓ (y) >
                             
                             
                              ηR, ∅ ̸= T (y) ̸= F,
                             
                               γR, T (y) = ∅.
                             

Assume also that at least uR labels are universal and have empty type. If
U ⊆ Y is deficient, meaning |NΓ (U )| < |U |, and V = B \ NΓ (U ), then
(4.5.1)         |U | > τ R,     |V | > uR,          |U | + |V | > R.
No universal label lies in U . Moreover, U consists only of full-type labels if
|U | ≤ ηR, and contains no empty-type label if |U | ≤ γR.
Proof. A deficient set is nonempty. If U ⊆ E, every member of U has
more than εR ≥ |U | neighbors, a contradiction. Otherwise U contains a
label outside E, of degree greater than τ R. Its neighbors lie in NΓ (U ), so
|U | > τ R.
   A universal member would give NΓ (U ) = B, also impossible. Thus
|U | ≤ (1 − u)R, and |V | = R − |NΓ (U )| > R − |U | ≥ uR. The same identity
gives the last inequality in (4.5.1). Finally, a member of degree greater than
ηR cannot belong to a deficient set of size at most ηR. This excludes every
type except F in the first range. The identical argument with γR excludes
type 0 in the second range.                                                  □

                                      49
   Some full-type labels meet the stronger target without a probability
estimate. For an integer h ≥ 1 and a full-type odd label y ≤ h, put
                 b0 (h, y) = |{j ≤ ⌊h/2⌋ : T (j) = 0, j ∼z y}|.
Here, as before, j ∼z y means that j, y share a prime greater than the cutoff
z.
Lemma 4.5.2 (Protected full-type labels). Suppose all labels lie in [h],
∆G (A) ≤ D, and R ≥ R0 > 0. If dΓ0 (y) ≥ cR for full-type labels, where
c > τ , then
(4.5.2)                        Db0 (h, y) < R0 (c − τ )
implies dΓ (y) > τ R for every permutation within the even type classes.
Consequently the full-type labels failing (4.5.2), counted among all odd integers
at most h, give a uniform upper bound for the exception population.
Proof. A full-type label is initially compatible only with an empty-tag posi-
tion. Every neighbor of such a position has empty type, by (4.4.1). Each
deletion is therefore witnessed by an empty-type half-label counted in b0 (h, y).
Such a half-label occupies one vertex of degree at most D, so it can witness
at most D deleted positions. Hence
              dΓ (y) ≥ cR − Db0 (h, y) > cR − R0 (c − τ ) ≥ τ R.
The complete endpoint pool includes every usable half-label, and the complete
odd prefix includes every actual exception. This proves the uniform assertion.
                                                                            □
  For example, if the endpoint exception count is e0 , one may use ε = e0 /R0 ,
provided ε < τ . The weak degree target must still be checked for each actual
exception.
4.5.2. Coordinate bounds that preserve mass. Clipping a selected type dis-
tribution at a reference law loses mass. Instead we move the excess to the
full type, which is no more compatible than any other type. This gives
coordinate bounds without changing the size of the selected set.
Lemma 4.5.3 (Moving excess to the full type). Let ρ, π be probability laws
on T with ∥ρ − π∥TV ≤ v, and let 0 ≤ fI ≤ ρI . There is a vector fb of the
same total mass such that
(4.5.3)         0 ≤ fbI ≤ πI     (I ̸= F ),          0 ≤ fbF ≤ πF + v.
For every nonnegative vector g,
                      X                        X
                            C(I, J)fbI gJ ≤          C(I, J)fI gJ .
                      I,J                      I,J

f0 ≤ ρ0 − u, then
                      fb0 ≤ min{π0 , max(0, π0 + v − u)}.

For I ̸= F , the retained coordinate can be realized by reducing weights on the

                                          P
                           X

                            I

Define fbI = min(fI , πI ) and then add e to its full coordinate. This preserves
the total mass and proves (4.5.3). The change moves mass only from a
type I to F , and C(F, J) ≤ C(I, J) for every J. Thus compatibility cannot
increase. The matrix is symmetric, giving the other-side assertion. Since
F ̸= 0, the empty coordinate is only clipped. Its additional bound follows
from ρ0 ≤ π0 + v. Finally, multiply all originally selected weights of a nonfull
type by fbI /fI when fI > 0, and leave a zero coordinate empty.               □
  For finite order intervals, the label coordinates can be bounded by exact
counts. No transport on that side is then needed. Write C I (m) for the
block-type prefix count of Section 4.1, and put
(4.5.4)                    cI (h) = C I (h) − C I (⌊h/2⌋).
Multiplication by two preserves these types, so cI (h) counts the odd integers
of type I in [h].
Corollary 4.5.4 (Uniform finite coordinate bounds). Suppose the order
belongs to an integer interval [l, h], all residual odd labels lie in [n], and their
number satisfies R ≥ R0 > 0. Every selected label submeasure, normalized
by R, has coordinate at most cI (h)/R0 . If the selection excludes at least uR

4.5.3. Finite rectangle minima. We next justify the finite minimizations used
with these coordinate bounds. The assertion is for a nonnegative matrix; it
is not an integrality assertion for arbitrary bilinear optimization.
Proposition 4.5.5 (Capped bilinear minimum). Let Cij ≥ 0, let pi , qj ≥ 0

                  max{a, L −          j qj } ≤ min{     i pi , L − b}.

On a nonempty domain the minimum of i,j Cij xi wj is attained at a point
of one of the following forms:
    (1) all but at most one coordinate, on the two sides together, are either
        zero

             or at their cap;

        zero

Proof. Possible left masses form the interval in (4.5.7); every mass between
zero and the sum of a side’s caps can be filled on that side. This proves
feasibility. The domain is compact, so a minimum exists.
   Start from a minimizer. If neither side-mass constraint is an equality and
two coordinates are strictly between their bounds, transfer mass between
them, leaving their sum fixed. If they are on the same side, the objective is
affine in the transfer. If they are on opposite sides, say xi increases by t and
wj decreases by t, the quadratic coefficient is −Cij ≤ 0. In either case the
objective is concave on the permitted transfer interval. One endpoint has
value no greater than the starting value. Move to that endpoint. A coordinate
now reaches a bound, or a side-mass constraint becomes an equality.
   Keep coordinates at their bounds fixed. If a side-mass constraint has
become an equality, both side masses are fixed, since their sum is L. Transfer
mass only within one side thereafter; the objective is affine and an endpoint

                                           52
puts another coordinate at a bound. Each step decreases the number of
unbounded coordinates unless it first fixes the side masses. The procedure
terminates in one of the stated forms. To enumerate them, choose the
coordinates at their caps, the possible remaining coordinate on each side,
and the indicated mass equality. The remaining coordinates are determined
by these equalities. All candidate values are rational when the data are
rational.                                                               □

4.5.5. Exact minimization with separate costs. The preceding strict inequality
is a finite rational test. We prove this for a general nonnegative compatibility
matrix, so that the reason for the test does not depend on the arithmetic of
a particular interval.

be a finite rational matrix, pi , qj nonnegative rational caps, and 0 ≤ a ≤ b ≤ 1.

with rational breakpoints and rational affine pieces. The minimum of
                                     X                      X

   Step 1: choose a left vertex. For fixed
                                       P
                                           t and w, the objective is affine in x.
Its minimum on the capped simplex i xi = t is attained at a point with at

its t, w fixed, this shows that some global minimum has this form.

  Enumerate a set A0 of coordinates at their caps and a possible remaining

             / A0 . With m0 = k∈A0 pk , the corresponding family is
       xk = pk (k ∈ A0 ),      xi = t − m0 ,      xk = 0 (k ∈
                                                            / A0 ∪ {i}),
restricted to m0 ≤ t ≤ m0 + pi and the feasible interval. Include also all-
bound choices, for which t = m0 is a single point. These families exhaust
the required left vertices, including degenerate caps.
   Step 2: fill the right coordinates. For one such family, divide each interval
[0, qj ] into the linear pieces of hj . Their slopes are nondecreasing within a
column. After adding the bilinear term, each segment has a marginal cost
                         Ajr + Bj t,     Bj = Cij ≥ 0,
where r indexes the segment. Its length is a fixed rational number. For fixed
t, fill segments in increasing order of these marginal costs until total length
1 − t is reached. Ties may be ordered so that earlier segments of a column
come first. Convexity guarantees that this order respects the within-column
constraint.
   This filling minimizes the cost: if positive length were used in a more
expensive available segment while a cheaper segment remained unfilled, trans-
ferring the smaller available length would decrease the objective. Equivalently,
ignoring the within-column constraint gives this same greedy optimum, and
the
P
      ordered construction already satisfies that constraint. Add the constant
     h
   j j (0) to the segment costs. All-bound left choices are evaluated by this
same procedure at their fixed mass.
   Step 3: find the masses that need evaluation. For a family with variable
t, partition its closed feasibility interval at every crossing of two distinct
affine marginal costs. On each open interval the segment order is fixed.
Partition it further wherever 1 − t equals a cumulative segment length in
that order. Between consecutive points, every used segment is fixed in full
except possibly the last. If its preceding length is d0 , the last contribution is
                       (1 − t − d0 )(A + Bt),      B ≥ 0.
All full-segment contributions are affine in t. Thus the optimized value on
this interval is a quadratic with leading coefficient −B ≤ 0, or an affine
function if no segment is partial. It is concave and attains its minimum at
an endpoint.
   At a cost crossing, tied segments have equal marginal cost, so changing
their order does not change the limiting value. When the last segment
becomes empty or full, the two formulas also agree. The optimized value
is therefore continuous at all subdivision points. Evaluating the finite list
of endpoints gives the minimum for this family, and then for all families.
Crossings, cumulative lengths, and feasibility endpoints are rational. Hence
every evaluation is rational, as claimed.                                  □

These functions are convex and piecewise linear. For J ∈ / S, the cap and cost
are zero. With rational costs and parameters the test is therefore exact. The
use of LJ (N w) is deliberate: replacing its argument by (N w − 1)+ would
introduce a slope change that need not preserve this convexity. The weaker
forest bound used here is sufficient and has the required finite minimization.

            5. Certificates for the orders through 1359
   This part proves Theorem 5.8.4: every forest of order n ≤ 1359 has
a coprime labeling by [n]. Section 5.1 defines finite proof graphs for the
statements P(L) and proves that their rules are sound (Theorem 5.1.11).
Sections 5.2–5.5 add branch peeling and certified rooted blocks, which reduce
the number of cases. Sections 5.6–5.8 apply them to the orders 1–299,
300–1352, and 1353–1359.

5.1. Finite labeling certificates
   We return to the finite reductions of Section 3.1. Their arithmetic hy-
potheses concern every forest on a specified label set, sometimes with a
restriction on an attachment vertex. We describe finite certificates for these
assertions. The role of a certificate is to provide the choices in a proved
reduction, together with a complete check of every terminal case.
   It is useful to separate the integer labels from the graph of permitted pairs.
A single graph certificate can then be used for several label sets, provided its
edges and attachment restrictions are checked anew for each set.
5.1.1. Compatibility graphs and marked vertices. Let H be a simple graph on
a set S of m ≥ 1 positions, and let O ⊆ S. For 0 ≤ k ≤ m, write Uk (H, O)
if, for every tree T of order m and every M ⊆ V (T ) with |M | ≤ k, there is a
bijection f : V (T ) → S such that
(5.1.1)      f (v)f (w) ∈ E(H)     (vw ∈ E(T )),      f (M ) ⊆ O.
Vertices of M are called marked. The set O is irrelevant when k = 0; we
then write U0 (H).
   The same assertions hold for forests. Complete the input forest to a tree
by Lemma 3.1.1, retaining its marked set, and restrict the resulting labeling.
Conversely trees are forests. It suffices to check marked sets of size exactly
k, since smaller ones can be enlarged. If O = S, the condition on marks is
automatic, so U0 (H) implies Uk (H, S) for every k ≤ m.
   For a set L of distinct positive integers, its coprimality graph HL has vertex
set L and joins distinct x, y when gcd(x, y) = 1. Thus P(L) is equivalent to


                                       59
U0 (HL ) when L ̸= ∅. If a branch is attached to a vertex labeled c ∈
                                                                    / L, its
required assertion is
(5.1.2)        U1 (HL , Oc (L)),     Oc (L) = {x ∈ L : gcd(c, x) = 1}.
The marked vertex is the actual attachment vertex. It need not be a leaf or
a centroid.
Lemma 5.1.1 (Transfer of a host certificate). Let H, H ′ have the same
order m, let O ⊆ V (H) and O′ ⊆ V (H ′ ), and let 0 ≤ k ≤ k ′ ≤ m. Suppose
a bijection σ : V (H) → V (H ′ ) maps every edge of H to an edge of H ′ . If
k > 0, assume also σ(O) ⊆ O′ . Then
                        Uk′ (H, O)   =⇒         Uk (H ′ , O′ ).
In particular, a graph certificate can be bound to an integer label set by an
explicit bijection preserving all required edges and, when needed, the allowed
attachment labels.
Proof. Given T, M with |M | ≤ k, take a labeling f furnished by the source
assertion. The composition σ ◦ f is bijective, maps every tree edge to an edge
of H ′ , and sends every marked vertex into O′ when marks are present. □
   Equality of the two compatibility graphs is sufficient but unnecessary.
Additional permitted edges cannot invalidate a labeling. The inclusion of
allowed attachment positions must, however, hold for the entire source set
O, not only for a position used by one previously found labeling.
5.1.2. Complete terminal tables. A terminal table is useful only if its index
set is complete. We give a recursive enumeration whose completeness follows
directly from the decomposition of a rooted tree at its root.
   Encode the one-vertex rooted tree by the string (). In general, its code is
an opening parenthesis, the codes of the rooted child trees in lexicographic
order, and a closing parenthesis. The order of a code is its number of opening
parentheses. Such a code also specifies a representative: create a vertex at
each opening parenthesis, joined to the vertex of the innermost still open
pair. Vertices are indexed in their order of creation, starting with the root.
Lemma 5.1.2 (Enumeration by rooted codes). Two rooted trees have the
same code if and only if they are isomorphic by an isomorphism preserving
the root. All rooted types of order m are obtained, once each, by taking all
multisets of previously enumerated rooted types whose orders sum to m − 1,
sorting their codes, and enclosing the result in parentheses.
   For an unrooted tree T , let
                             c(T ) = min c(T, v),
                                     v∈V (T )

where c(T, v) is its code rooted at v. Retaining from the rooted list just
those codes that are the minimum over all rootings of their decoded tree gives
exactly one representative of each unrooted tree type of order m.

                                       60
Proof. Proceed by induction on the order. Matching outer parentheses
uniquely splits a root code into its child codes. A root-preserving isomorphism
permutes the child components and restricts to rooted isomorphisms within
them. Conversely, rooted isomorphisms between matched child components
extend, with the roots identified, to an isomorphism of the whole rooted trees.
By induction, equality of the sorted child-code lists is therefore equivalent to
rooted isomorphism.
   Every child has smaller order, and the child orders sum to m − 1. Thus
the multiset recursion includes the children of every rooted tree. Different
multisets give different sorted lists and hence different codes. Enumerating
multisets as nondecreasing lists of type indices introduces no repetitions.
This proves the first assertion, including its one-vertex base.
   An unrooted isomorphism identifies the sets of rooted codes obtained by
choosing all possible roots. Hence it preserves their minimum. If two minima
agree, their decoded rooted trees are isomorphic, and therefore their unrooted
trees are isomorphic. The minimum is itself one of the enumerated rooted
codes, so each unrooted type has exactly one retained representative.         □
Proposition 5.1.3 (Terminal witness criterion). Let H have order m ≥ 1,
and let 0 ≤ k ≤ m. For every unrooted representative in Lemma 5.1.2 and
every k-subset of its vertices, suppose a table contains a bijection satisfying
(5.1.1). Then Uk (H, O) holds. For k = 1, one may instead use one witness
for each rooted code, requiring that its root map into O.
   For an integer label set L and d ≥ 2, a complete unrooted table restricted
to trees of maximum degree at most d, with a coprime labeling by L in each
row, proves Pd (L).
Proof. Enlarge a given marked set to size k. Map the input tree to its
unrooted representative by an isomorphism. The image of the marked set is
among the checked subsets; compose the corresponding witness with that
isomorphism. This gives the desired bijection and preserves every edge and
mark. For one marked vertex, apply the rooted enumeration instead. All
choices of attachment vertex are included in its rooted types.
   In the degree-restricted case, first complete the input forest to a tree of
maximum degree at most d, using Lemma 3.1.1. Its representative occurs in
the restricted table. Transfer the witness back and restrict to the original
forest.                                                                     □
   The checks in each row are finite: the listed images form a permutation
of the host positions, every tree edge maps to a host edge, and every mark
has an allowed image. For integer labels, the edge check is an exact gcd
computation. Coverage requires equality with the entire expected index set,
not merely the expected number of rows. Counting rows is sufficient only if
validity, distinctness, and membership in that expected set have also been
checked.


                                      61
Proposition 5.1.4 (Two terminal label sets). The following label sets satisfy
P(L13 ) and P(L14 ):
               L13 = [10] ∪ {12, 14, 15},
               L14 = {1, 2, 3, 4, 7, 8, 9, 10, 15, 16, 20, 21, 22, 27}.
Proof. This is a finite verification by complete terminal tables. The recursion
of Lemma 5.1.2 gives 1301 unrooted types of order 13 and 3159 of order 14.
The accompanying terminal-table supplement contains a labeling for each of
these types with the indicated inventory.
   The supplied checker reconstructs both complete index sets and checks their
equality with the table keys. Every row is a bijection onto its stated inventory,
and every edge has coprime endpoint labels. There are 1301 · 12 = 15612
edge checks in the first table and 3159 · 13 = 41067 in the second; all pass.
The row format and a standalone checker are included in the supplement.
Proposition 5.1.3 now gives both forest assertions. No bound on the degrees
of these trees is imposed.                                                    □
5.1.3. Recursive host certificates. Complete tree tables become large. The fol-
lowing terminal rule avoids enumeration when all positions in the designated
set can receive an arbitrary neighbor.
Lemma 5.1.5 (A split host). Let H have order m, and suppose every vertex
of O is adjacent to every other vertex of H. Put e = m − |O|. If
                                      m−k
                                          
(5.1.3)                        e≤            ,
                                        2
then Uk (H, O) holds.
Proof. Given a tree T and at most k marks, delete the marked vertices. Each
component of the resulting forest is bipartite. Taking the larger part in each
component gives an independent set of size at least ⌈(m − k)/2⌉. Assign the
e labels outside O to any e vertices of this independent set, and the labels in
O to all remaining vertices. Every marked vertex receives a label in O. No
edge has both endpoints labeled outside O, and every other edge is permitted
by the hypothesis on O.                                                      □
   The next rule replaces a large tree by the components at a centroid. The
marks must be distributed among those components explicitly; a prescribed
attachment vertex cannot be assumed to be the centroid.
   For m ≥ 2, a centroid case for k marks consists of h ∈ {0, 1} and a multiset
of pairs (ji , ti ) satisfying
                                                X                         X
(5.1.4) 1 ≤ ji ≤ ⌊m/2⌋,      0 ≤ ti ≤ ji ,           ji = m − 1,     h+       ti = k.
                                                 i                        i
There are finitely many cases. They can be enumerated by first listing the
integer partitions of m − 1 with parts at most ⌊m/2⌋, then distributing k − h
marks among the parts within their size bounds, and finally sorting the pairs
to remove duplicates.
                                         62
Proposition 5.1.6 (Marked centroid reduction). Let H have vertex set S of
order m ≥ 2, and let O ⊆ S, 0 ≤ k ≤ m. For every case in (5.1.4), suppose
there are a center c and a partition
                                    G
                        S = {c} ⊔       Si ,   |Si | = ji ,
                                    i
with c ∈ O if h = 1. For each part, require either of the following two
assertions:
    (1) c is adjacent to every member of Si , and Uti (H[Si ], O ∩ Si ) holds;
    (2) there is Oi ⊆ NH (c) ∩ Si , with Oi ⊆ O when ti > 0, for which
         Umin(ji ,ti +1) (H[Si ], Oi ) holds.
Then Uk (H, O) holds. A child assertion with a larger mark budget suffices; if
its designated set is its entire inventory, an unmarked assertion suffices.
Proof. Enlarge the marked set of the input tree to size k. Choose a centroid
z, whose existence was proved in Lemma 3.3.1. Each component of T − z
has order at most ⌊m/2⌋. Let h indicate whether z is marked, and let ji , ti
be the orders and mark counts of these components. They give one of the
listed cases. Match components of the same size and mark count with its
parts in any order, and assign c to z.
   Every component has a unique vertex adjacent to z. In the first alternative,
apply the child assertion to its inherited marks; the attachment edge is valid
because c is adjacent to the whole child inventory. In the second, additionally
mark the attachment vertex. There are at most min(ji , ti + 1) distinct marks.
The child assertion puts its attachment in NH (c), and, if there are inherited
marks, puts all of them in O as well. If z itself is marked, its label c lies in
O by hypothesis.
   The child bijections give all internal edges, and the attachment checks
give all remaining edges. The inventories are disjoint and exhaust S, so their
union is the required bijection. The assertions about larger budgets and an
unrestricted designated set follow from the definition of Uk .                □
  The choice Oi = NH (c) ∩ O ∩ Si handles both inherited marks and the
new attachment. When ti = 0, the intersection with O is unnecessary.
  We next give extensions that avoid new tree tables. Additional host edges
are allowed by Lemma 5.1.1.
Lemma 5.1.7 (Extensions at leaves and a universal vertex). Let H have
m ≥ 1 vertices and designated set O.
   (1) Add a vertex u adjacent to every vertex of H, obtaining H + . If
       0 ≤ k ≤ m + 1 and Umin(k,m) (H, O) holds, then Uk (H + , O ∪ {u})
       holds.
   (2) If U0 (H) holds, then the same H + satisfies U1 (H + , {u}).
   (3) Add vertices u, v, with u adjacent to all other vertices and v adjacent
       only to u. For k ∈ {0, 1}, Uk (H, O) implies Uk (H + , O ∪ {u}) for this
       enlarged host.
                                         63
   (4) If U1 (H, {a}) holds for some a ∈ V (H), adding a single new vertex
       adjacent to a gives an unmarked universal host.
Proof. For the first assertion, remove a leaf of the input tree, assign it u,
and use the child assertion on the remaining tree with its remaining marks.
There are at most min(k, m) such marks. If the removed leaf was marked,
its new label is allowed; its only edge is valid because u is universal.
   For the second assertion, designate a root, choosing an arbitrary vertex if
no mark was specified. Delete it and label the remaining forest using H. Put
u on the deleted vertex. Every restored edge meets u, proving the required
fixed-root assertion.
   For the third assertion, the input has at least three vertices. If there is a
marked vertex, choose a leaf different from it; a tree of order at least two
has at least two leaves. Without a mark, choose any leaf. Remove this leaf x
and its neighbor y, and place them at v and u, respectively. If y was marked,
its label u is allowed. Otherwise the mark remains in the residual forest and
the source assertion places it in O. Every edge incident with the removed
vertices meets y, so its image meets the universal vertex u. This proves the
assertion, also when the residual forest has a single vertex.
   For the last assertion, delete any leaf of the input tree. Root the remaining
tree at its neighbor, embed it in H with that root at a, and assign the new
host vertex to the deleted leaf.                                              □
   The third assertion retains a set of possible root positions. It does not
force the input root to the new universal position, since the root may remain
in the residual forest.
Lemma 5.1.8 (An attached triangle). Suppose H has order m ≥ 1 and
U1 (H, {a}) holds. Add two vertices x, y and the three edges ax, ay, xy. The
resulting host H + satisfies U0 (H + ).
Proof. Every tree of order at least three has either two leaves with a common
neighbor or a leaf whose neighbor has degree two. To see this, take an
endpoint x′ of a longest path and its neighbor y ′ . If y ′ has degree two, the
second alternative holds. Otherwise y ′ has a neighbor off that path. Every
such neighbor is a leaf: a path of length at least two into its component,
followed by the rest of the original longest path, would be longer. Thus x′
and one of these other leaves have a common neighbor.
   In the first alternative, delete the two leaves and call their common
neighbor a′ . In the second, delete the leaf and its degree-two neighbor, and
let a′ be the latter’s other neighbor. The remaining graph is a tree of order
m. Embed it in H with a′ at a. In the first case use x, y for the two leaves.
In the second put the degree-two vertex at x and its leaf at y. The edges
ax, ay or ax, xy, respectively, restore the input tree. Figure 5 shows the two
possibilities.                                                               □


                                      64
                         H                           H
                         a                            a


                   x         y                   x         y
                   two leaves            a leaf and its neighbor

          Figure 5. The attached triangle in Lemma 5.1.8. The re-
          maining tree is embedded in H with its attachment at a.
          Solid edges restore the deleted vertices; the dashed edge is
          available in the host but is unused in that case.

   This conclusion is unmarked: it gives no arbitrary prescribed-root condition
for H + . Adding a universal vertex afterwards restores such a condition by
Lemma 5.1.7(2).
5.1.4. From branch contracts to a label-set assertion. The next reduction
explains which rooted block contracts suffice for an entire tree. Fix integers
b ≥ 2 and n ≥ b + 2. A short-branch profile is a vector of nonnegative integers
k = (k1 , . . . , kb−1 ) such that
                   b−1
                   X                     b−1
                                         X
(5.1.5)    b≤s=          jkj ≤ n − 2,          jaj ̸= b   (0 ≤ aj ≤ kj , aj ∈ Z).
                   j=1                   j=1
The second requirement says that no selection of branches has total order b.
Lemma 5.1.9 (A detached set or short branches). Every tree T of order
n ≥ b + 2 has one of the following configurations:
    (1) a vertex u and a union of components of T − u of total order b;
    (2) adjacent vertices u, v such that, on the side of the edge uv containing
        v, the components attached to v other than the one containing u have
        orders below b and multiplicities forming a short-branch profile.
All components on the v side are included in the second alternative.
Proof. Root T at a leaf and choose a deepest vertex v whose descendant
subtree has at least b+1 vertices. The child of the root has order n−1 ≥ b+1,
so v is not the root and has a parent u. Every child subtree of v has at most
b vertices. If some selection of those subtrees has total order b, it gives the
first alternative, with contact vertex v.
   Otherwise none has order b, all have orders below b, and their total is
at least b. It is at most n − 2, since u, v are not among their vertices. The
multiplicities have no submultiset of total order b, by the failure of the first
choice. They therefore satisfy (5.1.5), and the edge uv gives the second
alternative.                                                                  □



                                        65
Proposition 5.1.10 (Extension through rooted branch blocks). Let L be a
set of n ≥ b + 2 labels, where b ≥ 2. Suppose there is a partition
                           L = {q} ⊔ B ⊔ C,            |B| = b,
such that q is universal in L and P(B), P(C) hold. For each short-branch
profile, suppose there is one of the following partitions, with kj sets Bj,r of
size j:
                                  G
(retain)          L = {c} ⊔ C ⊔         Bj,r ,         gcd(c, x) = 1   (x ∈ C);
                                  j,r
                                     G
(remove)          L = {q, c} ⊔ C ⊔            Bj,r ,   q universal in L.
                                        j,r

In either case require P(C) and U1 (HBj,r , Oc (Bj,r )) for every block. The
partitions and their labels may depend on the profile. Then P(L) holds.
Proof. It suffices to label an arbitrary tree of order n, by Lemma 3.1.1. Apply
Lemma 5.1.9. In its first alternative, put q on the contact vertex, label the
detached forest by B, and label the remaining forest by C. Every crossing
edge meets the contact, so its labels are coprime.
   In the second alternative, put c on v. For each size j, match the kj blocks
with the kj actual child subtrees. Root each subtree at its unique neighbor
of v and use its rooted block contract. This proves all internal branch edges
and all edges from the branches to v.
   In the retain case, delete v and all its child subtrees and label the remaining
tree by C. The parent u remains there. The only edge from that remainder
to the deleted part is uv, and its labels are coprime because the label of u
lies in C.
   In the remove case, also delete u, put q on it, and label the remaining
forest by C. Every further crossing edge meets u and is valid by universality
of q. The empty remainder is allowed. In both cases all inventories are
disjoint and exhaust L. The result is the required bijection, with every edge
checked.                                                                        □
   The two rows distinguish whether the parent belongs to the recursively
labeled remainder. Their remainder orders are n − s − 1 and n − s − 2,
respectively. Neither may be replaced by a different initial interval without
checking the actual inventory.
   For clarity, the profile index set itself has a finite exact test. For a proposed
vector k, start with A0 = {0} and compute
           Aj = {a + tj : a ∈ Aj−1 , 0 ≤ t ≤ kj } ∩ [0, b]        (1 ≤ j < b).
Here [0, b] denotes the integer set {0, 1, . . . , b}. Inductively Aj is precisely
the set of sums at most b formed using the first j branch sizes. Thus the
last condition in (5.1.5) is exactly b ∈
                                       / Ab−1 . Enumerating each kj between
zero and ⌊(n − 2)/j⌋ and retaining just the vectors passing this test and
the total-size test gives every required profile. Smaller sufficient families

                                                 66
of retained cores will be treated separately; they require a selection lemma
before this full checklist can be shortened.
5.1.5. Soundness of a finite proof graph. We can now specify the logical
content of a certificate, independently of how its witnesses were found. A
state records either a marked host assertion Uk (H, O), an arithmetic forest
assertion P(L), or a degree-restricted assertion Pd (L) with d ≥ 2. Its
data include the exact graph or integer inventory and all mark or degree
restrictions. A proof node names a rule, supplies its finite choices, and refers
to the states used as hypotheses.
   The rules in the present system are complete terminal tables, the split-host
rule, marked centroid reduction, the local host extensions, the attached
triangle, host transfer and arithmetic binding, rooted branch extension, and
the leaf-pair and detached-block rules of Section 3.1. Trivial empty arithmetic
inventories and singletons are terminal states. Forgetting a mark restriction
is allowed; forgetting a degree restriction is allowed only when that degree
cap already includes every tree of the stated order. A host on one vertex
meets a positive mark requirement exactly when its position is allowed.
   In particular, a terminal degree-restricted table is checked only against its
declared tree family and is never registered as an unrestricted assertion by
that check alone. In the leaf-pair rule, the child inventory and degree cap
must satisfy Corollary 3.1.3. In a detached-block rule, every required size in
Proposition 3.1.5 must have its own checked partition and child assertions.
Theorem 5.1.11 (Soundness of the certificate rules). Let a finite directed
graph of proof nodes use only the preceding rules. Suppose every referenced
node is present, there is no directed cycle, every rule’s arithmetic and graph
hypotheses are checked, and every finite case list is complete. Then the
assertion at every node is true. In particular, an accepted root state P([n])
proves a prime labeling for every tree of order n.
Proof. Follow an order in which all dependencies of a node precede it; such
an order exists for every finite acyclic directed graph. Terminal states hold
by their direct checks, Proposition 5.1.3, or Lemma 5.1.5. At a nonterminal
node all referenced assertions already hold. Its checked rule is an implication
proved above or in Section 3.1, so its assertion holds as well. Host binding is
the composition of Lemma 5.1.1 with the exact coprimality and allowed-root
relations in (5.1.2).
   For a centroid node, completeness means that every size and mark case
in (5.1.4) is present. For a branch extension it means that every profile in
(5.1.5) is present. The proofs of the corresponding reductions show that
every input tree selects one of these checked cases. Terminal completeness
has the same role through the tree-code enumeration. Thus induction proves
the full universal assertion at each node, and not only a statement about the
representatives used during discovery.                                        □


                                       67
   The centroid rule strictly decreases order, but a host transfer preserves it.
Such transfers must still be checked for cycles. One sufficient convention is
to permit an equal-order transfer only from a host with fewer edges; then the
pair consisting of order and edge count decreases along those dependencies.
Explicit cycle detection also permits transfers between isomorphic states
without assuming that order alone makes the proof well founded.
   This theorem concerns the stated rule system. Further host rules or
shortened profile lists require their own implications and coverage proofs
before they can be used. To establish a finite prefix, one must also supply
certificates whose accepted roots include every initial interval in that prefix.
Those inventories and coverage checks are separate from the soundness result
just proved.

5.2. Peeling branches and finite-prefix induction
   The branch extension in Section 5.1 asks for every short-branch profile,
whose total may be almost n. We now retain only a bounded collection of
branches. The others receive consecutive intervals, and the exterior forest
receives an initial interval of its actual order. This reduces the number of
arithmetic assertions without changing the set of input trees covered.
   Throughout this section b ≥ 2, n ≥ b + 2, and a profile has branch sizes in
{1, . . . , b − 1}. For a profile k, put
                              b−1
                              X                            X
                      |k| =         jkj ,        o(k) =            kj .
                              j=1                         1≤j<b
                                                           j odd

Thus |k| counts vertices and o(k) counts odd-order branches. A subprofile
h ≤ k is obtained by retaining some whole branches. The short-branch
condition remains (5.1.5); in particular no subprofile has total b. For integers
a, j ≥ 1, write I(a, j) = {a, a + 1, . . . , a + j − 1}. Intervals with reversed
endpoints are empty. A branch configuration means the second alternative
of Lemma 5.1.9, with adjacent vertices u, v and all branches at v on the side
away from u.
5.2.1. Selecting a bounded collection of branches.
Lemma 5.2.1 (Minimal retained cores). Let A ≥ b and let k have total
at least A. One may require, in addition, that a retained subprofile contain
an odd-order branch, provided o(k) > 0. There is an inclusion-minimal
subprofile h meeting these requirements, with total t satisfying
                               A ≤ t ≤ A + b − 2.
If all branch orders are even, no odd branch is required, and E = 2⌊(b − 1)/2⌋,
the upper bound improves to A + E − 1.
   Writing ε = 1 when an odd branch is required and ε = 0 otherwise,
minimality is equivalent to
(5.2.1)      hj > 0    =⇒       t − j < A or (ε = 1, j odd, o(h) = 1).
                                            68
Every retained subprofile of a short-branch profile still has no subprofile of
total b.
Proof. If an odd branch is required, choose one first. Its order is below b ≤ A.
Continue choosing branches until their total first reaches A. Without the
extra requirement, start with no branches and use the same procedure. Just
before the last choice the total is at most A − 1; the last branch has order
at most b − 1, or at most E in the even case. These are the stated upper
bounds.
   Now delete a retained branch whenever the remaining total is at least A
and any required odd branch is still present. Each deletion decreases the
number of retained branches, so the procedure terminates. It preserves both
requirements and cannot increase the upper bound. A branch of order j is
removable exactly when both alternatives on the right of (5.2.1) fail.
   It remains to check inclusion-minimality, rather than merely the failure
of a single deletion. If a proper subprofile met the requirements, choose a
branch omitted from it. Deleting that branch alone would leave a profile
containing the proposed subprofile, so it too would meet the requirements.
This contradicts termination. Conversely, an allowed single deletion contra-
dicts inclusion-minimality. Finally, a forbidden sum in a subprofile would
already have been a forbidden sum in the original profile.                    □
   At odd order we will apply this lemma with A = b and no parity require-
ment. At even order, a profile with an odd-order branch will retain one. We
call these the ordinary profiles. The remaining profiles occur at even order
and have only even branch sizes. Their threshold will depend on the chosen
center label. The distinction concerns branch orders; it does not prescribe
the parity of every label inside a branch.
5.2.2. Arithmetic assertions for the retained branches. We specify two as-
sertions about a profile h of total t ≤ n − 2. They concern only its retained
branches and the vertex or vertices at which they attach.
   Write Rn (h) if there are a label q universal in [n] and a partition
                                         G
(5.2.2)     {n − t, . . . , n} = {q} ⊔         Bj,r ,         |Bj,r | = j   (1 ≤ r ≤ hj ),
                                         j,r

such that P(Bj,r ) holds for every block. The center will receive q. No root
restriction on these blocks is needed, because q is universal in the full interval.
   For a specified center label a, write Dn (h; a) if there are a universal label
q and a partition
                                               G
(5.2.3) {n − t − 1, . . . , n} = {q, a} ⊔            Bj,r ,      |Bj,r | = j   (1 ≤ r ≤ hj ),
                                               j,r

such that every block satisfies U1 (HBj,r , Oa (Bj,r )). The center receives a, and
its parent receives q. The displayed partition requires q ̸= a and membership
of both labels in the stated top interval. Universality refers to [n], not just
that interval.
                                               69
  These are finite arithmetic assertions. Their block conditions may be
supplied by the certificate rules of Section 5.1. Choosing a retained profile
does not itself establish either assertion.
Proposition 5.2.2 (Peeling at a universal center). Let k be a short-branch
profile of total s, and let h ≤ k have total t ≥ b. Suppose Rn (h) holds.
Put m = n − s − 1 and order the omitted branches arbitrarily. Give them
consecutive intervals, in that order, partitioning
                               {m + 1, . . . , n − t − 1}.
If each of these intervals satisfies P and P([m]) holds, then every branch
configuration with profile k has a coprime labeling by [n], with its parent
retained in the exterior tree.
Proof. Use the notation u, v of Lemma 5.1.9. Choose the actual branches
described by h, put q on v, and label the retained branches by the blocks
in (5.2.2). Their orders agree, so their forest assertions apply to the actual
branch trees.
   The omitted branches have total s − t. The displayed interval also has
length n − t − 1 − m = s − t, so it is partitioned exactly by their assigned
intervals. Label each omitted branch using its interval assertion. Delete v
and all its branches. The remaining tree contains u and has order m; label
it by [m].
   The three inventories are disjoint and exhaust [n]. Every edge inside a
piece is valid. Every edge between pieces meets v, whose label q is universal.
This proves the assertion.                                                  □
  For even branches we use the stronger interval assertion
(5.2.4)   U1 (HI(a,j) , Oodd (a, j)),     Oodd (a, j) = {x ∈ I(a, j) : x is odd}.
It places the actual attachment vertex at an odd label. It is not a claim
about just one choice of root in an unrooted tree.
Proposition 5.2.3 (Peeling even branches). Let n be even, let k be a short-
branch profile with only even branch sizes, and let h ≤ k have total t ≥ b.
Write s = |k| and m = n − s − 2. Suppose c ≥ 2 is a power of two and
Dn (h; c) holds. Partition
                               {m + 1, . . . , n − t − 2}
into consecutive intervals for the omitted branches, in any order. If each
interval satisfies (5.2.4) and P([m]) holds, the full branch configuration has
a coprime labeling by [n] with its parent removed. Every interval used here
has even length and odd starting label.
Proof. Put c on v and the universal label q on u. Label the retained branches
by (5.2.3), rooting each at its neighbor of v. Label each omitted branch by
its assigned interval, with an odd label at that same attachment vertex. Its
edge to v is coprime because c is a power of two.
                                           70
   After deleting u, v and all branches at v, the exterior is a forest of order
m. Label it by [m]. The intermediate interval has length s − t, and the top
interval has length t + 2; these inventories and [m] partition [n]. Internal
edges and the edges at v have been checked. Every other crossing edge meets
u and is valid by universality of q.
   Both n and s are even, so m is even. The first interval starts at m + 1,
and each later start is obtained by adding even branch orders. Thus every
start is odd, as claimed.                                                    □

        Parent retained: m = n − s − 1
          exterior: m         omitted: s − t           v and core: t + 1
    1               m|m+1                    n−t−1|n−t                       n

        Parent removed: m = n − s − 2
          exterior: m         omitted: s − t          u, v and core: t + 2
    1               m|m+1               n−t−2|n−t−1                          n

          Figure 6. The label inventories in the two peeling construc-
          tions. The numbers inside the boxes are their sizes; the labels
          below give the integer boundaries. Widths are schematic. An
          exterior or omitted-branch interval may be empty.

   The exterior orders in these two propositions depend on the full total s,
not on the retained total t. The intermediate labels are used by the omitted
branches. They are not added to the initial interval used for the exterior
forest.
5.2.3. Exchanging the center label. The power of two in Proposition 5.2.3
must belong to the top interval. If it lies far below n, this may require a
large retained total. The next proposition permits a label 2p near n in that
interval, and uses a power of two only in the final exchange.
Proposition 5.2.4 (Center exchange). Let n be even and b ≥ 3. Choose a
power of two c and an odd prime p such that
(5.2.5)              c ≤ n,     2p ≤ n < 3p,        c − p ≥ b.
Let k be a short-branch profile with only even sizes, and let h ≤ k have total
t ≥ b. Suppose
(5.2.6)                 Dn (h; 2p)   and     n − t − 1 > p.
Put s = |k| and m = n − s − 2. As in Proposition 5.2.3, assign consecutive
intervals to the omitted branches. If these satisfy (5.2.4) and P([m]) holds,
the full configuration has a coprime labeling by [n].

                                        71
Proof. Put 2p on v and the universal label q on u. Use the retained block
labelings, the odd-root interval labelings, and an exterior labeling by [m].
This gives a bijection with coprime internal edges. Every branch root has an
odd label: for a retained branch this follows from coprimality with 2p, and
for an omitted branch it is part of the interval assertion. The only odd label
in [n] not coprime to 2p is p, since 3p > n. Thus an edge from v to a root
labeled p is the only possible failure so far.
   If m ≥ c, then p < c ≤ m. Every branch label exceeds m, so no branch
root is labeled p. All edges at v are valid, and every remaining crossing edge
meets u. This gives the required labeling in this case.
   Suppose now that m < c. The label c occurs in a branch. Indeed it is
outside [m], differs from 2p, and differs from q. The first distinction holds
because an odd prime cannot divide a power of two. For the second, every
universal label in [n], with n ≥ 4, is odd: an even label other than 2 conflicts
with 2, while 2 conflicts with 4. The vertex carrying c is not a branch root,
since all those roots have odd labels.
   Exchange c and 2p. The center now has a power-of-two label, so all of its
branch edges are coprime. We must check the vertex in the branch whose
label changes from c to 2p. Its neighbors all lie inside that branch and have
odd labels, because its previous incident edges were coprime to c. None
has label p. If c is in a retained block, every label of that block is at least
n − t − 1 > p. If c is in an omitted interval of length j < b, any two labels
there differ by at most j − 1 ≤ b − 2, whereas c − p ≥ b.
   Every odd neighbor is therefore coprime to 2p. All other internal edges are
unchanged, and edges at u remain valid by universality of q. The exchange
preserves the inventory and bijectivity. This proves the assertion.           □
   The preliminary labeling in the second case need not be valid at v. The
proof checks those edges after the exchange; it uses only the already valid
internal branch edges to determine the parities of the neighbors of c.
5.2.4. Restricting the longest odd intervals. An interval assertion for a large
branch may be harder to establish than the assertions for smaller branches.
We can restrict the starts needed for the largest odd branch size without
enlarging the retained-core family.
Lemma 5.2.5 (Retaining the largest odd branches). Assume b ≥ 4, and let
j∗ be the largest odd integer below b. An ordinary short-branch profile has a
minimal retained core, of total between b and 2b − 2, chosen as follows:
    (1) if at least two have order j∗ , retain two;
    (2) if exactly one does, retain it and a branch of largest remaining order;
    (3) if none does, use Lemma 5.2.1 with threshold b and the required odd
        branch, if any.
In each case minimality includes the odd-branch requirement when n is even.


                                      72
Proof. Put d = b − j∗ ∈ {1, 2}. In the first case the total is 2j∗ ∈ [b, 2b − 2],
and deleting either branch leaves a total below b. Both branches have odd
order.
   In the second case another branch exists, since j∗ < b and the full total
is at least b. Let h be its largest possible order. If d = 1, a branch of order
1 together with the j∗ -branch would give the forbidden sum b, so h ≥ 2. If
d = 2, a branch of order 2 is forbidden. If every other branch had order 1,
there would be at least two of them, again giving the forbidden sum with j∗ .
Thus h ≥ 3. In both cases h ≥ d + 1, and hence
                            b + 1 ≤ j∗ + h ≤ 2b − 2.
Deleting either retained branch leaves total below b, and the j∗ -branch
supplies the required odd order. The last case is exactly Lemma 5.2.1. All
selected profiles inherit the absence of a sum b.                       □
Corollary 5.2.6 (The required interval starts). Use Lemma 5.2.5 in ordinary
peeling and place the omitted j∗ -branches last. Then the plain interval
assertions of length j∗ are needed only at starts in
(5.2.7)      An,j∗ = {n − (2 + r)j∗ : r ≥ 1, n − (2 + r)j∗ > 0}.
All other plain interval assertions may retain their full range of starts. The
odd-root assertions for even-length intervals are unchanged.
Proof. If any j∗ -branch is omitted, at least three were present and the retained
core consists of exactly two. The peeled intervals end at n − 2j∗ − 1. Since
the j∗ -intervals come last, counting them backwards gives starts n − 3j∗ , n −
4j∗ , . . .. Their actual starts are positive, so all occur in (5.2.7). If at most
one j∗ -branch was present, it was retained and no interval of this length is
used. Profiles with only even branches contain no j∗ -branch at all.            □
  For example, n = 1359 and b = 32 give j∗ = 31 and the 41 starts
26, 57, . . . , 1266. This calculation restricts an arithmetic checklist; it does
not assert that the interval or retained-block assertions have already been
proved.
5.2.5. The finite-prefix step. We can now state the induction step. The
case lists are defined mathematically, so their completeness can be checked
separately from the arithmetic assertions assigned to them.
   Let On,b consist of all short-branch profiles h with
                       b ≤ t = |h| ≤ min(2b − 2, n − 2)
that satisfy (5.2.1) with A = b and ε = 0 for odd n, or ε = 1 for even n. In
the latter case also require o(h) > 0.
   For even n, put E = 2⌊(b − 1)/2⌋. When E ≥ 2, choose a nominal center
label a and define
(5.2.8)          B = max(b, n − a − 1),        H = B + E − 1.


                                        73
       −
Let En,b (a) contain all short-branch profiles with only even sizes and total
                    +
b ≤ t < B. Let En,b   (a) contain all such profiles with B ≤ t ≤ min(H, n − 2)
and
                           hj > 0 =⇒ t − j < B.
The restriction t ≤ n−2 is part of the short-branch definition in both families.
The low family is not filtered by minimality.
   For interval assertions, use all pairs (j, a0 ) with 1 ≤ j < b and 1 ≤ a0 ≤
n − j + 1, except that, when b ≥ 4 and j = j∗ , use only a0 ∈ An,j∗ . Denote
this list by In,b .
Theorem 5.2.7 (A step in the finite prefix). Put ℓ = n − b − 1 and suppose
P([m]) holds for every 0 ≤ m ≤ ℓ. Assume the following assertions.
   (1) There is a label q0 universal in [n] for which
                   {ℓ + 1, . . . , n} = {q0 } ⊔ B0 ,   P(B0 ).
    (2) Rn (h) holds for every h ∈ On,b .
    (3) P(I(a0 , j)) holds for every (j, a0 ) ∈ In,b . If n is even, (5.2.4) also
         holds for every even j < b and every odd start 1 ≤ a0 ≤ n − j + 1.
If n is even and E ≥ 2, make one of the following choices:
     (i) a = c, where 2 ≤ c ≤ n is a power of two;
    (ii) a = 2p, where a power of two c and an odd prime p satisfy (5.2.5),
         and n − H − 1 > p.
For this choice require, in addition,
                                                   −
                  Dn (h; a(h)) for some a(h), h ∈ En,b (a),
                                                   +
                  Dn (h; a),                  h ∈ En,b (a).
There are no even-family requirements when n is odd or E = 0. Then P([n])
holds.
Proof. By Lemma 3.1.1, it suffices to label an arbitrary tree of order n. Apply
Lemma 5.1.9. In its detached-set case, put q0 on the contact vertex, use B0
for the detached forest of order b, and use [ℓ] for the remaining forest. The
first assertion and universality of q0 check all edges.
   In the branch case, let k be the full profile and write s = |k|. Suppose first
that it is ordinary. Choose a minimal core by Lemma 5.2.5 when b ≥ 4, or by
Lemma 5.2.1 otherwise. Its total is at most 2b − 2 and at most s ≤ n − 2, so
it belongs to On,b . Place omitted branches of order j∗ last when that size is
defined. The required plain intervals are all in In,b , by Corollary 5.2.6. The
actual exterior order is m = n − s − 1 ≤ ℓ. Thus Proposition 5.2.2 applies.
   It remains to treat an even n and a profile consisting only of even branches.
                                                                          −
Such a profile requires E ≥ 2. If s < B, the full profile itself lies in En,b (a).
Use its asserted Dn partition and retain every branch. Its center may depend
on the profile: no branch is peeled. Put its universal label on u and label
the exterior forest by [n − s − 2]. This is the remove-parent construction of
Proposition 5.1.10.
                                         74
   If s ≥ B, apply Lemma 5.2.1 with threshold B, no odd-branch requirement,
and the bound E on branch size. The selected total satisfies B ≤ t ≤ H; its
                                           +
minimality puts the selected profile in En,b (a). In particular t ≥ n − a − 1,
so the nominal center belongs to the required top interval. The exterior has
order m = n − s − 2 ≤ ℓ. All omitted intervals have even length and odd
start, as proved in Proposition 5.2.3.
   For choice (i), that proposition finishes the labeling. For choice (ii), the
bound t ≤ H gives n − t − 1 ≥ n − H − 1 > p, so Proposition 5.2.4 applies.
Every branch profile has now been treated, and the detached-set case was
handled at the start. This proves P([n]).                                    □
Corollary 5.2.8 (Induction over a finite range). Suppose P([m]) is estab-
lished for 0 ≤ m ≤ N0 . For each integer N0 < n ≤ N , choose bn with
2 ≤ bn ≤ n − 2 and supply all the arithmetic assertions of Theorem 5.2.7,
using its exact case families. Then P([n]) holds for every 0 ≤ n ≤ N .
Proof. Proceed in increasing order of n. Its required initial intervals have
orders at most n−bn −1 < n, so they are covered by the induction hypothesis.
Theorem 5.2.7 establishes the next order. Induction gives the whole range. □
   All case families in the theorem are finite and explicit. To enumerate them,
use the bounded subset-sum recurrence after Proposition 5.1.10, restrict the
total to the displayed bounds, and test (5.2.1). Retain every low even profile.
The interval list is given by integer endpoints and, for its one restricted
size, (5.2.7). A certificate must give exactly these cases, together with the
detached-set partition, and bind every block to its full arithmetic assertion.
Equality of the expected and supplied case sets checks coverage; the soundness
results check the assertions themselves.
   The remaining block theorems and their complete arithmetic certificates
are needed to apply this induction to a specified numerical prefix.

5.3. Bipartite hosts for finite blocks
   The branch reductions require coprime labelings of intervals with a pre-
scribed root. Checking every rooted tree separately becomes costly as the
interval grows. We instead assign universal labels to the root and a small
set of further vertices. The remaining forest has a bipartition with a fixed
small side and a degree bound on that side. Its nonleaf structure can then
be enumerated independently of the number of leaves.
   We first prove the structural reduction. We then describe complete
certificates for the bipartite hosts and apply one to a rooted block of order
28. The certificate checks every residual tree described by the reduction; it
makes no assumption on the original tree’s maximum degree.
5.3.1. A small side with bounded degrees. Choose a bipartition (C, I) of a
forest F by putting the smaller side of each nontrivial component in C,
and every isolated vertex in I. Thus C contains no isolated vertices and

                                      75
|C| ≤ ⌊|V (F )|/2⌋. The next lemma quantifies how many vertices of I become
isolated when suitable vertices of C are deleted.
Lemma 5.3.1 (One-side deletion). Let (C, I) be a bipartition of a forest of
order N , with no isolated vertex in C, and put c = |C|. Let 1 ≤ k < c and
D ≥ 1. Suppose that
                H = {v ∈ C : dF (v) ≥ D + 1},           h = |H| ≤ k.
There is a set S ⊂ C, of size k and containing H, such that ∆F −S (C \S) ≤ D

                   (k − h)(N + 1 − 2c) + (c − k) max{0, Dh + 2 − c}

      e(F ) = N − z − m1 ,         e(F ) ≥ L + 2q,        N = c + z + L + q.

(5.3.2)                        z + L ≥ N + 1 − 2c.
Here m1 ≥ 1 because c > k ≥ 1. Counting the isolated vertices as separate

          (D + 1)h ≤ a + h + q − 1,         a ≥ Dh − q + 1 ≥ Dh + 2 − c.
For h = 0 we have a = 0 and c ≥ 2, so in either case a ≥ max{0, Dh + 2 − c}.

                      (8, 5, 3),      (9, 6, 4),     (10, 6, 4).

               e(F ) ≥ (D + 1)(k + 1) > 2k + 2b = |V (F )| − 1,

                                       77
5.3.2. From bipartite hosts to rooted blocks. Let X be a bipartite graph with
specified sides of sizes b, a. We say that X covers (b, a, D) if every forest
with specified bipartition (B, A), |B| = b, |A| = a, and ∆(B) ≤ D has a
side-preserving edge-preserving bijection to X. In particular, the bipartition
is part of the input; it is not chosen anew when testing a witness.
Proposition 5.3.3 (Adding universal vertices). Choose b, D, k as in Theo-
rem 5.3.2, and suppose X covers (b, k + b + 1, D). Adjoin k vertices, each
adjacent to every other vertex, to obtain a graph H. Then every forest of
order 2k + 2b + 1 embeds bijectively in H.
   If instead k + 1 such vertices are adjoined, every forest of order 2k + 2b + 2
with a specified vertex v has an embedding that sends v to any specified one
of the new vertices.
Proof. In the first assertion use Theorem 5.3.2 to choose S, and embed the
residual forest in X. Send S bijectively to the new vertices. Edges inside
the residual forest are preserved by its embedding; every other edge has at
least one end sent to a universal vertex.
   For the rooted assertion, delete v first and apply the same construction to
the remaining forest of order 2k + 2b + 1. Send v to the specified new vertex
and S to the other k. The edges incident to v are valid by universality. The
two constructions use every host vertex exactly once.                       □
  It remains to prove the covering assertions for the hosts actually used.
The next reduction retains all vertices of the small side, which we color black.
Vertices on the other side are colored white.
Lemma 5.3.4 (Kernel and leaves). Let T be a tree with a specified bipartition
into b ≥ 2 black vertices and a ≥ 1 white vertices, and with black degrees at
most D. Delete all white leaves. The resulting graph K is a tree containing
all b black vertices and q white vertices, where
                             1 ≤ q ≤ min{a, b − 1}.
Every white vertex of K has degree at least 2, and every black vertex has
degree between 1 and D. The tree T is recovered by attaching ℓv white leaves
to each black vertex v, with
                   X
(5.3.4)                 ℓv = a − q,     0 ≤ ℓv ≤ D − dK (v).
                  v∈B
Conversely every such kernel and vector give a tree of the specified orders
and degree bound.
Proof. Removing leaves preserves connectivity among the remaining vertices.
A black vertex could become isolated only if all its neighbors were white
leaves, in which case its component in T would contain no other black vertex.
This contradicts b ≥ 2 and connectivity. Hence K is a tree, every black
vertex still has a neighbor, and q ≥ 1. No edge at a remaining white vertex

                                       78
was removed, so its degree is at least 2. Counting edges from the white side
gives
                           2q ≤ e(K) = b + q − 1,
and thus q ≤ b − 1. The deleted leaves give exactly (5.3.4). Conversely,
attaching those leaves preserves the tree property, adds precisely a − q white
vertices, and raises no black degree above D.                               □
  The embedding of K is allowed to depend on its leaf vector. An embedding
that works for one vector need not have enough unused neighbors for another.
Our certificates therefore supply a full tree embedding for each required
vector.
5.3.3. Complete enumeration of the kernels.
Proposition 5.3.5 (Star partitions). Every kernel in Lemma 5.3.4 is ob-
tained by the following procedure, up to a color-preserving isomorphism. Take
a tree R on b black vertices and partition its edges into nonempty blocks, each
having a common endpoint. For each block introduce a white vertex adjacent
to all endpoints of its edges, and discard the edges of R. Retain only the
graphs with at most a white vertices and black degrees at most D.
   Conversely, every retained graph is a tree with the stated kernel conditions.
Consequently it is enough to use one representative of each unrooted tree R
and all its edge partitions.
Proof. For each white vertex of a kernel choose one of its black neighbors, and
contract the edge between them. These edges form a forest, each of whose
components contains exactly one black vertex. The quotient is therefore a
tree R on the b black vertices. The uncontracted edges originally incident to
a given white vertex become a nonempty star centered at its chosen black
neighbor. These stars partition E(R). Their endpoint sets are exactly the
original white neighborhoods, so the procedure reconstructs the kernel.
   Conversely, a block of t edges with a common endpoint has t + 1 distinct
endpoints. Replacing it by a new white vertex and its incident edges preserves
connectivity. If there are q blocks, the resulting graph has b + q vertices and
                           X
                                   (t + 1) = (b − 1) + q
                          blocks
edges, and is therefore a tree. Each white degree is at least 2; every black
degree is positive. The two stated filters give the remaining conditions.
Finally an isomorphism of R carries all its edge partitions with it, so passing
to unrooted representatives loses no kernel.                                 □
   The enumeration in Lemma 5.1.2 supplies the trees R. An alternative,
used by the checker below, starts with the one-vertex tree and repeatedly
attaches a leaf at every possible vertex, retaining one representative of each
resulting isomorphism class. It is complete because every tree of order at
least two has a leaf, whose deletion gives a tree of the preceding order.

                                          79
          b1                                       b1
                                   b4                                   b4
                           w2                                 w1 w2
                      b3                                       b3
           w1                                   w1

                           w3                                      w3
                                   b5                                   b5
          b2                                       b2
                Kernel K                             Black tree R

        Figure 7. Contract the dashed edges, one at each white
        vertex. The labels on the edges of R identify their blocks.
        Replacing each block by a white vertex adjacent to its end-
        points recovers K.

   For the colored kernels, put the symbol B or W immediately after each
opening parenthesis in the rooted code. Sort the child codes as before, and
take the least code over all roots. The induction proving Lemma 5.1.2 then
proves that equality of these codes is exactly color-preserving isomorphism.
Thus repeated kernels can be removed without discarding any required case.
   There is a further reduction when black vertices have equal neighborhoods.
It is useful for larger blocks, though the specific table below retains every
feasible leaf vector.
Lemma 5.3.6 (Permuting equal neighborhoods). Partition the black vertices
of a kernel into classes with equal white neighborhoods. The vectors in (5.3.4)
need only be checked when their coordinates are nondecreasing in a fixed order
within each class.
   For a class of size t whose vertices have degree d, put
                                         X
                    Pt,D−d (z) =                       z x1 +···+xt .
                                   0≤x1 ≤···≤xt ≤D−d

The number of vectors that remain is the coefficient of z a−q in the product of
these polynomials over the classes.
Proof. Every permutation within a class, fixing all other vertices, is an
automorphism of the kernel. All vertices in that class have the same degree,
and therefore the same upper bound D − d for their leaf coordinates. Sorting
the coordinates preserves admissibility. Permuting the black vertices and
their attached leaves gives an isomorphism of the enlarged trees, so an
embedding for the sorted vector gives one for the original vector.
  The polynomial for one class lists its allowed nondecreasing coordinate
sequences, with exponent equal to their total. Multiplication makes the
choices independently for the classes, and extraction of the coefficient enforces
the total a − q. No quotient by other kernel automorphisms is asserted or
needed.                                                                        □

                                         80
Proposition 5.3.7 (Bipartite witness criterion). Let b ≥ 2, a ≥ 1, and
Db ≥ a + b − 1. Let X be a bipartite graph with sides of sizes b, a. Enumerate
the kernels by Proposition 5.3.5. For each kernel and every vector in (5.3.4),
supply a side-preserving bijection of the enlarged tree to X. If every edge
maps to an edge of X, then X covers (b, a, D).
   It is enough to supply the sorted vectors of Lemma 5.3.6. In this case,
completeness of a strictly lexicographically increasing list of admissible sorted
vectors is certified by equality of its length with the coefficient in that lemma.
Proof. Lemma 5.3.4 describes every tree with the specified sides and degree
bound. The star partitions contain its kernel type, and the supplied vectors
contain its leaf data, up to the permitted permutations. Pulling back the
checked witness along this isomorphism embeds the tree in X.
   Now let F be any forest with the specified sides and black degree bound.
Lemma 4.3.1, with the black side as its degree-constrained side, completes F
to a tree on the same bipartition: both sides are nonempty and Db ≥ a+b−1.
An embedding of that tree also embeds F . For the final assertion, strict
ordering excludes duplicate vectors, admissibility includes the listed vectors
in the required finite set, and equality of cardinalities proves that none is
omitted.                                                                    □
  The distinction between the two sides also gives a useful extension that
requires no new tree table.
Lemma 5.3.8 (Adding a white vertex). Suppose X covers (b, a, D), where
b ≥ 2, a ≥ b − 1, and Db ≥ b + a. Adjoin a white vertex adjacent to every
black vertex of X. The resulting host covers (b, a + 1, D).
Proof. Apply Lemma 4.3.1 to complete the input forest to a tree on the same
sides. The capacity condition is Db ≥ b + (a + 1) − 1. This tree has a white
leaf. Otherwise its number of edges would be at least 2(a + 1) > b + a, since
a ≥ b − 1, whereas a tree of this order has b + a edges. Delete a white leaf
and embed the remaining tree in X. Send the leaf to the new white vertex.
Its unique neighbor is black, so its edge is preserved. Restricting to the input
forest proves the assertion.                                                  □
5.3.4. A rooted interval of order twenty-eight. We now give one explicit
arithmetic application. Let X be the bipartite coprimality graph with black
labels
(5.3.5)            129, 133, 135, 141, 143, 145, 147, 153, 155
and white labels 130, 132, . . . , 156. An edge joins two labels on opposite sides
exactly when their gcd is 1. Table 4 records the complete witness collection
supplied with this manuscript.
Theorem 5.3.9 (A rooted block of order twenty-eight). Every forest F on
28 vertices with a specified vertex v has a coprime labeling by {129, . . . , 156}
in which v receives 131.
                                        81
        Enumerated or checked objects                          Number
        Unrooted trees on nine black vertices                   47
        Partitions of their eight edge positions             4 140
        Distinct kernels with black degree at most six         493
        Feasible kernel–leaf-vector pairs                8 205 524
        Edges checked in their full embeddings         180 521 528
        Table 4. The exhaustive certificate for the 9-by-14 bipartite
        host. The 4 140 partitions are tested for each black tree; only
        partitions into stars contribute kernels.


Proof. The host X covers (9, 14, 6) by the exhaustive certificate in Table 4 and
Proposition 5.3.7. We specify the checks that establish this finite assertion.
   The checker regenerates all 47 black trees by leaf augmentation. It gener-
ates each partition of eight positions by successively putting the next position
into an existing block or into a new last block. Every partition appears
exactly once when its blocks are ordered by their least elements. The star
construction and the black degree filter give exactly 493 colored kernel codes.
The supplied kernel list is required to contain each of these codes exactly
once.
   For each kernel, with black vertices indexed first, the checker enumerates
all nonnegative leaf vectors of total 14 − q, in lexicographic order, and retains
precisely those satisfying (5.3.4) with D = 6. Each vector has a supplied
record of 23 host indices. The record lists the images of the kernel vertices
first, then those of the attached leaves in black-vertex order. The first nine
entries must permute the black host indices, and the remaining entries must
permute the white indices. The checker reconstructs the arithmetic host by
exact gcd calculations, then checks all kernel edges and all added leaf edges.
Each enlarged tree has 22 edges. The record counts and boundaries must
agree with the independently enumerated vectors, with no missing or trailing
records. All 8 205 524 records pass these checks. Finally, 6 · 9 ≥ 9 + 14 − 1,
so the witness criterion applies also to forests.
   The five labels
                             131, 137, 139, 149, 151
are prime and exceed 156/2. They are therefore universal in {129, . . . , 156}.
Together with the two sides of X they partition that interval. Apply Propo-
sition 5.3.3 with b = 9, D = 6, and k = 4. Its rooted assertion has order
2k + 2b + 2 = 28, and the specified universal label may be chosen to be 131.
This gives the required labeling.                                              □
   The supplement contains the kernel list, every embedding record, and
a standalone checker using only exact integer operations and the Python
standard library. Compression changes only the storage of the records. The
                                       82
checks above, rather than a file hash or a search success rate, establish the
covering assertion. The universal small-side theorem supplies the passage
from arbitrary input forests to that finite assertion.

5.4. Reserved vertices in finite blocks
   A universal label can be placed on a deleted vertex without restricting
its neighbors. Some useful intervals have too few such labels, but contain
a further label with only one possible conflict. We will place its forbidden
partner at a vertex isolated in the residual forest. Controlling that vertex’s
degree before deletion leaves a nonneighbor among the deleted vertices, where
the nearly universal label can be placed.
   This section gives three reductions for forests of order 25. Together with
complete bipartite certificates, they yield rooted blocks of order 26. We also
verify one family of arithmetic transfers over an explicit finite range.
5.4.1. The integer deletion bounds. We refine the averaging step in
Lemma 5.3.1. The improvement uses only that the leaf counts are integers.
Lemma 5.4.1 (Largest integer weights). Let r ≥ 1, 0 ≤ t ≤ r, and let r
nonnegative integer weights have total T . The sum of their t largest values is
at least
(5.4.1)            M (t, r, T ) = t⌊T /r⌋ + min{t, T − r⌊T /r⌋}.
Proof. If two weights differ by at least two, decrease a largest weight by
one and increase a smallest weight by one. The sum of the t largest cannot
increase. Indeed, the only t-element sets whose sums increase contain the
smallest entry and omit the largest; replacing the former by the latter gave
a still larger sum before the change.
   Each change strictly decreases the sum of the squares, so the process
terminates with all weights differing by at most one. Write T = rQ + R,
where 0 ≤ R < r. The final weights are R copies of Q + 1 and r − R copies
of Q. Their t largest sum to tQ + min{t, R}, as claimed.                  □
   The following finite calculation isolates the cases in which leaf counts alone
do not suffice. We give every exceptional tuple; the next proof resolves them
using degree-two vertices.
Lemma 5.4.2 (Finite deletion calculation). Consider the following three
choices, denoted by I, II, and III:
(5.4.2)             (k, D, ε) = (4, 4, 1),        (3, 6, 0),   (3, 8, 1).
Let c, h, z, L, a, q be integers satisfying
(5.4.3)         k + 1 ≤ c ≤ 12,   0 ≤ h ≤ min{k, ⌊(24 − c)/D⌋},
          z, L ≥ 0, z + L ≤ 25 − c, q = 25 − c − z − L, 0 ≤ a ≤ L,
                         max{c + Dh, L + 2q} ≤ 24 − z,
             a = 0 if h = 0,      (D + 1)h − a ≤ h + q − 1 if h > 0.
                                             83
Put
(5.4.4)              Λ = z + a + M (k − h, c − h, L − a).
Then Λ ≥ 12 − c + ε except for the tuples in Table 5.
Proof. This is an exhaustive calculation on bounded integers. For each
choice in (5.4.2), enumerate c and h in the displayed ranges. Enumerate
0 ≤ z ≤ 25 − c and 0 ≤ L ≤ 25 − c − z, set q = 25 − c − z − L, and discard a
tuple if max{c + Dh, L + 2q} > 24 − z. Finally enumerate a from 0 to L and
impose the last line of (5.4.3). These nested ranges include every permitted
tuple exactly once.
   There remain respectively 9 181, 6 142, and 4 010 tuples. Evaluate (5.4.4)
by the quotient and remainder of L − a upon division by c − h. The complete
lists for which the required inequality fails are precisely those in Table 5. The
accompanying checker performs these integer loops and requires equality with
all eight displayed rows. No tree enumeration or numerical approximation
enters this calculation.                                                       □
