Theorem 5.4.3 (Reductions at order twenty-five). For each choice (k, D, ε)
in (5.4.2), every forest F of order 25 has a partition
          V (F ) = S ⊔ B ⊔ A,     |S| = k,   |B| = 12 − k,    |A| = 13,
such that (B, A) is a bipartition of F − S and ∆F −S (B) ≤ D. When ε = 1,
the set A contains a vertex r isolated in F − S with dF (r) ≤ 2.
Proof. Choose (C, I) by taking the smaller side of each nontrivial component
in C and placing all isolated vertices in I. Write c = |C| ≤ 12. If c ≤ k,
delete all of C and any k − c vertices of I. All remaining vertices are isolated.
Their original degrees sum to at most e(F ) ≤ 24. There are 25 − k ≥ 21 of
them, so one has original degree at most one. Put that vertex in A, complete
A to size 13, and put the others in B.
                                       84
  Suppose c > k, and put
                 H = {v ∈ C : dF (v) ≥ D + 1},         h = |H|.
Every vertex of C has positive degree, so c + Dh ≤ e(F ) ≤ 24. In all three
parameter ranges this gives h ≤ k. Let z, L, q count vertices of I of degrees
zero, one, and at least two, and let a count the leaves adjacent to H. As in
the proof of Lemma 5.3.1,
                   max{c + Dh, L + 2q} ≤ e(F ) ≤ 24 − z.
If h > 0, the forest between H and the q nonleaf vertices has at most h + q − 1
edges; thus (D + 1)h − a ≤ h + q − 1. All conditions of Lemma 5.4.2 hold.
   Give each vertex of C its number of leaf neighbors in I as weight. Delete
H and the k − h vertices of C \ H with largest weights. Lemma 5.4.1 shows
that these deletions isolate at least Λ vertices of I, all of original degree zero
or one. Outside Table 5, there are at least 12 − c + ε such vertices.
   We now treat the table. Except for its sixth row, z = 0 and L + 2q = 24.
These equalities force F to be connected and every nonleaf vertex of I to
have degree two. Delete the I-leaves and contract one edge at each remaining
I-vertex. This gives a tree R on C. Whenever both endpoints of an edge of
R are deleted, the intervening degree-two vertex of I becomes isolated. Such
vertices are distinct from the leaves counted by their weights.
   The first and fourth rows. Here H consists of one vertex of weight zero;
the eight other weights sum to eight. Put t = k − 1, which is three in the
first row and two in the fourth. If the largest t weights sum to at least t + 1,
their leaves suffice. Otherwise all eight weights are one. Indeed, if a largest
weight were at least two, either the other t − 1 largest were positive, giving
sum at least t + 1, or fewer than t entries would carry the total eight, making
the largest t sum to eight. Choose t vertices outside H, including a neighbor
in R of its unique vertex. The deletion isolates t leaves and the intervening
vertex, as required.
   The second row. The seven weights outside the two vertices of H sum to
seven. If the largest two sum to at least three, these leaves and the one leaf
at H suffice. Otherwise all seven weights are one. If the two vertices of H
are adjacent in R, their connector is already isolated. If they are not, choose
one of the two additional vertices to be a neighbor of H in R. In either case
the deletion isolates at least 1 + 2 + 1 = 4 vertices.
   The third row. The eight outside weights sum to six. If the largest two
sum to at least three, we are done. Otherwise the weights are six ones and
two zeros. Both vertices of H have degree at least five in R, since a = 0.
They must be adjacent: if not, their neighborhoods lie in the other eight
vertices and have at most one common vertex, since R is a tree. Their degree
sum would then be at most nine, a contradiction. Delete two positive-weight
vertices in addition to H. Their two leaves and the connector between the
vertices of H give three isolates.


                                       85
   The fifth row. There is no vertex of H. If the largest three weights sum
to at least four, we are done. Otherwise the nine weights are eight ones
and one zero. If R has an edge joining positive-weight vertices, delete its
endpoints and a third positive-weight vertex. This isolates three leaves and
their connector. If no such edge exists, every edge of R meets the zero-weight
vertex, so R is a star. Delete its center and two leaves, isolating two original
leaves and two connectors.
   The sixth row. The nine weights sum to nine. Unless the three largest
sum to at least four, all nine are one. Among the seven nonleaf vertices of I
there is one of degree two: otherwise e(F ) ≥ 9 + 3 · 7 > 24. Delete its two
neighbors in C and any third vertex of C. These deletions isolate that vertex
and three leaves. Connectivity of F is not needed in this case.
   The seventh and eighth rows. In each row H consists of one vertex. Its
degree in R is at least D + 1 − a, which is eight in the seventh row and nine
in the eighth. Hence R is a star centered there. The outside weights sum to
seven or six, respectively; their two largest have sum at least two. Delete
those two vertices and H. Their two connectors, their leaves, and any leaf
at H give at least five isolates in the seventh row and four in the eighth,
exceeding what is required.
   Thus in every case S contains H and isolates at least 12 − c + ε vertices
of I of original degree at most two. Move 12 − c of them to C \ S. The
resulting side B has c − k + 12 − c = 12 − k vertices, and the other side A
has 13. Moved vertices are isolated, and every surviving vertex of C has
degree at most D. If ε = 1, retain one of the counted isolates in A and call
it r. This proves every assertion.                                            □
5.4.2. Restoring the deleted vertices. The degree condition on r concerns the
forest before deletion. Isolation after deletion alone would not ensure that a
deleted vertex avoids r.
Proposition 5.4.4 (A reserved label). Let X cover (8, 12, 4) or (9, 12, 8), in
the sense of Section 5.3, and put k = 4 or 3, respectively. Adjoin a vertex e, a
vertex ζ, and a set U of k vertices. Suppose every vertex of U is universal in
the resulting graph H, and ζ is adjacent to every other vertex except possibly
e. No further edges at e are required. Then every forest on 26 vertices, with a
specified vertex v, embeds bijectively in H with v sent to any specified member
of U .
   If X instead covers (9, 13, 6), adjoining four universal vertices gives the
same rooted assertion, with the root at any one of those vertices.
Proof. Write F for the forest obtained by deleting v. Apply case I or III
of Theorem 5.4.3. Put e on the reserved vertex r. All neighbors of r in
this 25-vertex forest lie in S, and there are at most two. Since |S| = k ≥ 3,
choose s ∈ S \ NF (r) and put ζ on s. Assign the other k − 1 vertices of S to
U other than the prescribed root image. Embed the forest on B ⊔ (A \ {r})
in X, and send v to its prescribed member of U .
                                       86
  This is a bijection onto H. Edges within the residual forest are preserved
by its embedding. Every edge meeting a vertex assigned to U is allowed by
universality. Every remaining edge at s is allowed by the condition on ζ: its
only possible forbidden partner lies on r, which was chosen nonadjacent to s.
The vertex r has no neighbor in the residual forest. These checks cover all
edges, including those at the restored root.
  For the last assertion, delete v and use case II of Theorem 5.4.3. Embed
the remaining 9-by-13 forest in X, assign the three deleted vertices to three
universal vertices, and send v to the fourth. Every unexamined edge meets a
universal vertex.                                                          □

Corollary 5.4.6 (Explicit rooted intervals). Every forest of order 26 has a
coprime labeling by {45, . . . , 70}, with a specified vertex assigned any member
of {47, 53, 59, 61, 67}. The same assertion holds for each interval in Table 7,
with its root assigned any label in the corresponding set U .
Proof. For the first interval use X0 , reserve the label 60, and use 47, 53, 59, 61
as U and 67 as ζ in Proposition 5.4.4. These five labels are all universal in
the interval. The proposition gives, in particular, a labeling with root 47.
Exchanging two universal labels preserves every edge and permits any of the
five root labels.
   For the other rows, use Proposition 5.4.5 and the corresponding case of
Proposition 5.4.4. Direct gcd checks give the universal and nearly universal
properties in the table. For example, 253 = 11 · 23 conflicts only with 264
in its interval, and 799 = 17 · 47 conflicts only with 816 in its interval. The
label 841 = 292 is fully universal in {825, . . . , 850}, which also meets the
stated hypothesis. The inventories are disjoint and exhaust their respective
intervals, so the embeddings are the required coprime labelings.                 □
5.4.4. A finite family of arithmetic transfers. The sparse host X0 needs
substantially fewer arithmetic edges than the full coprimality graph on its
vertex names. An edge-preserving image of these 38 edges is enough to
transfer its covering property.
Theorem 5.4.7 (Intervals with five universal labels). Let a be odd, 1 ≤
a ≤ 7125, and put L = {a, a + 1, . . . , a + 25}. If L contains at least five
universal labels, then every forest of order 26 has a coprime labeling by L.
Any specified vertex may receive any universal label of L.

                                        89
Proof. For each of the 3 563 possible starts, compute the set
             U (L) = {x ∈ L : gcd(x, y) = 1 for all y ∈ L \ {x}}.
Exactly 1 944 starts have |U (L)| ≥ 5. For every one of these starts the
transfer table supplies a bijection from V (X0 ) and six additional positions
onto L. The first five additional positions receive distinct members of U (L);
the sixth receives an even reserved label. The eight black vertices of X0
receive odd labels, the twelve white vertices receive even labels, and all 38
edges have coprime images.
   The checker recomputes U (L) for every permitted start, requires the set
of supplied starts to equal the qualifying set, and checks every inventory
and every displayed host edge. All 1 944 transfers pass, giving 73 872 edge
gcd checks. Thus each qualifying arithmetic graph contains a copy of X0 , a
reserve, and five universal vertices. Choose any of the five as the root image
and a different one as ζ. Proposition 5.4.4 then gives the required rooted
labeling.
   The prescribed universal label need not belong to the five selected in the
transfer. If it does not, exchange it with the label at the root. Both labels
are universal in the entire inventory, so all edges incident to either vertex
remain coprime; every other edge and the bijection are unchanged. This
proves the full root assertion.                                             □
   The remaining 1 619 starts fail this theorem’s hypothesis. Of the qualifying
intervals, 360 end at or below 1354. These counts describe blocks of length
26; they do not assert prime labeling for every order up to 7150. Applying the
finite-prefix induction still requires its complete inventory of block assertions.
   The supplement includes the five host certificates, all 1 944 transfer bi-
jections, and the exact deletion calculation. Its checker regenerates the
required kernel, leaf-vector, parameter, and interval sets before checking their
witnesses. The structural cases in the proof of Theorem 5.4.3 account for
every tuple excluded from the numerical leaf-count bound.

5.5. Larger finite blocks
   The same small-side reductions give blocks of order 30. Three of the
intervals below contain six universal labels; their residual hosts have sides

the ten-vertex side of Theorem 5.3.2. We verify these four hosts together,
distinguishing the complete leaf-vector lists from the list reduced by equal
neighborhoods. We then explain how one of the hosts supplies blocks of two
larger orders without another enumeration.

from the interval L. On the remaining labels take the bipartite coprimality
graph, with odd labels black and even labels white. Call the resulting graphs

first three have 9 black vertices, and the fourth has 10.

5.5.2. Rooted blocks and one-label deletions.
Theorem 5.5.2 (Four rooted blocks of order thirty). For every row (L, U )
of Table 8, every forest on 30 vertices with a specified vertex v has a coprime
labeling by L assigning to v any specified label of U .
Proof. Delete v. In the first three rows, apply Theorem 5.3.2 with b = 9,
D = 6, and k = 5 to the remaining forest of order 29 = 2 · 5 + 2 · 9 + 1. It
gives five deleted vertices and a residual forest with sides 9 and 15, with
black degree at most six. Embed that forest in the appropriate host by
Proposition 5.5.1. Assign the specified label to v and the other five labels of
U to the deleted vertices.
   For the fourth row use b = 10, D = 6, and k = 4. Now 29 = 2 · 4 + 2 · 10 + 1,
and the residual sides have sizes 10 and 15. Embed in X4 , and use the five
labels of U on the four deleted vertices and v, respecting the specified root
label.
                                           92
   In both cases the residual embedding uses precisely L \ U . The restored
labels are distinct and exhaust U , so the full labeling is a bijection. Every
edge not in the residual forest meets a vertex carrying a universal label. This
also checks all edges incident to v and completes the proof.                 □
Corollary 5.5.3 (Deleting a universal label). For each row (L, U ) of Table 8
and every u ∈ U , the assertion P(L \ {u}) holds.
Proof. Adjoin an isolated vertex to any forest on 29 vertices. Use Theo-
rem 5.5.2 with that vertex specified to receive u, and restrict the resulting
labeling. The restriction uses exactly L \ {u} and preserves all edges of the
original forest.                                                            □
5.5.3. Enlarging the source block. An added white vertex and an added
universal vertex have different roles. The first need only meet the black
side; the second must meet every other vertex. Keeping the two possibilities
separate gives useful source graphs of orders 31 and 32.
   Write Jt (X) for the graph obtained from X by adjoining t vertices, each
adjacent to every other vertex of the resulting graph. In particular, the new
vertices form a clique. If X is bipartite, write X + for the bipartite graph
obtained by adding one white vertex adjacent to all black vertices and to no
white vertices.
Proposition 5.5.4 (Larger source blocks). Suppose X covers (10, 15, 6).
Then every forest of order 31 embeds bijectively in each of J6 (X) and J5 (X + ).
Every forest of order 32 with a specified vertex v embeds bijectively in J6 (X + ),
sending v to any specified one of its six added universal vertices.
Proof. Let F have order 31. Theorem 5.3.2 with b = 10, D = 6, and k = 5
gives a set S of five vertices such that F − S has sides B, A of sizes 10, 16
and black degree at most six.
   To embed in J6 (X), choose any vertex w ∈ A and delete it as well. The
remaining forest has sides 10, 15, and deleting w cannot raise a black degree.
Embed it in X, and send S ∪ {w} to the six added universal vertices. Every
edge outside the residual forest meets one of those vertices, so it is preserved.
The vertex w need not be a leaf; universality permits all of its edges.
   For the second host, Lemma 5.3.8 applies because 15 ≥ 10 − 1 and
6 · 10 ≥ 10 + 15. Thus X + covers (10, 16, 6). Embed F − S directly in X + ,
and assign S to the five added universal vertices. This again uses every host
vertex once and preserves every edge.
   Finally delete the specified vertex of a forest of order 32. Embed the
remaining forest in J5 (X + ) by the second construction, using five of the six
universal vertices. Send the specified vertex to the remaining, prescribed
one. Every edge restored at that vertex is allowed. This proves the rooted
assertion.                                                                     □
  These constructions use the same certified host X4 . The two source
graphs of order 31 place the extra vertex in different parts: J6 (X4 ) has one
                                        93
more universal vertex, whereas J5 (X4+ ) has one more white vertex. For an
arithmetic application, an edge-preserving bijection from the chosen source
graph to the actual label inventory must still be supplied. In the rooted case
it must also carry a permitted universal vertex to the required root label.
Lemma 5.1.1 then gives the labeling assertion. No statement about every
interval of length 31 or 32 follows without those arithmetic transfers.
   The supplement contains all four host headers, their complete embedding
streams, and a checker regenerating the kernel and vector families used above.
The first three streams contain every feasible vector; the fourth contains
exactly the representatives justified by equal-neighborhood permutations.
These finite host assertions can now be used in the finite-prefix certificate
system of Sections 5.1 and 5.2. Verifying that system’s complete arithmetic
inventories remains a separate step.

5.6. The initial finite range
   We next assemble a finite proof for the first 299 orders. The certificate uses
the local reductions of Section 3.1 and the branch rules of Section 5.1. We
first record the few additional forms needed to apply those rules to the saved
arithmetic inventories. The complete proof graph, including all terminal
tables, accompanies this section. No previously accepted interval is used as
a terminal assertion.
5.6.1. Extensions near a prime.
Lemma 5.6.1. Let p ≥ 3 be prime. If P([p−1]) holds, then P([p]), P([p+1]),
and P([p + 2]) hold.
Proof. The label p is universal in each of the three intervals, since p + 2 < 2p.
For a tree of order p, delete a leaf and label the remaining tree by [p − 1];
give the leaf label p. For order p + 1, delete a leaf and its neighbor, label
the remaining forest by [p − 1], and restore the leaf and neighbor with labels
p + 1 and p.
   For order p + 2, use the configuration in the proof of Lemma 5.1.8. If
two leaves have a common neighbor, delete those three vertices, label the
remaining forest by [p − 1], and restore the neighbor with label p and the
leaves with labels p + 1, p + 2. Otherwise a leaf has a neighbor of degree
two. Let x, y, z be the leaf, that neighbor, and its other neighbor. Delete
all three, label the remainder by [p − 1], and put p + 2, p + 1, p on x, y, z,
respectively. The edge xy has consecutive labels; every other restored edge
meets z, whose label is universal. Both constructions use the entire required
inventory. Forest completion proves the stated forest assertions.              □
   This implication advances from order p − 1 by at most three vertices. It
requires the smaller assertion even when several of these intervals share the
same prime.


                                       94
5.6.2. One omitted block size. The parity version of the detached-block lemma
already gives most of the required block choices. Two certificate nodes use a
different shortening of the range.
Lemma 5.6.2. Let b ≥ 4 and n ≥ b + 2. Every tree T of order n has a
vertex u and a union A of components of T − u such that
                     b ≤ |A| ≤ 2b − 2,      |A| ̸= 2b − 3.
Consequently, the conclusion of Proposition 3.1.5 also holds if its size set is
replaced by
                  {b, b + 1, . . . , 2b − 4, 2b − 2} ∩ [1, n − 1]
and each block assertion is the unrestricted assertion P(Bk ).
Proof. Root T at a leaf and choose a deepest vertex v whose descendant
subtree has at least b + 1 vertices. It has a parent, and its child subtrees
have orders at most b. Their total is at least b. If a selection of them has a
permitted total, take that selection and put u = v.
   Suppose there is no such selection. No child subtree has order b. Choose
an inclusion-minimal selection with total at least b. Deleting its last selected
member shows that its total is at most 2b − 2; it must therefore be 2b − 3.
Deleting any member leaves at most b − 1 vertices, so every selected member
has order at least b − 2. Three such members would have total at least
3b − 6 > 2b − 3. There are exactly two, of orders b − 1, b − 2.
   An additional child subtree of order a would give a total b − 1 + a between
b and 2b − 2 together with the larger selected subtree. Thus a = b − 2.
But the two subtrees of order b − 2 would then have permitted total 2b − 4,
a contradiction. Hence there are no additional children. Take the whole
descendant subtree of v, of order 2b − 2, and put u at its parent.
   For the consequence, label u by the universal label qk , the detached forest
by Bk , and the remaining forest by Ck , exactly as in Proposition 3.1.5. Here
no degree bound on the detached forest is needed, because its block assertion
is unrestricted.                                                              □
5.6.3. Branches of orders at most four. For small b, the full profile condition
(5.1.5) has a simple description. The total s in the following statement is
always at most n − 2.
Lemma 5.6.3. The short-branch profiles for b = 3, 4, 5 are exactly the
following families, subject to b ≤ s ≤ n − 2:
                    b   profile      additional conditions
                    3   (0, k)       k ≥ 2,
                    4 (0, t, k)      t ∈ {0, 1},
                    5 (0, a, 0, d)   a, d ≥ 0,
                    5 (0, 0, c, d)   c ≥ 1, d ≥ 0,
                    5 (1, 0, c, 0)   c ≥ 2.
In the row for b = 4, the total condition says k ≥ 2 when t = 0, and k ≥ 1
when t = 1.
                                       95
Proof. For b = 3, a branch of order one together with a branch of order
two gives the forbidden sum, as do three singletons. A profile containing a
singleton therefore has total below three. The remaining profiles consist of
at least two branches of order two.
   For b = 4, two branches of order two are forbidden. A singleton cannot
coexist with a branch of order three. If there is a singleton and no branch of
order three, avoiding four also permits at most one branch of order two and
at most one singleton when that branch is present. Without that branch
there are at most three singletons. Thus any profile containing a singleton
has total below four. The stated family follows.
   For b = 5, first suppose there is no singleton. Branches of orders two and
three cannot coexist. This gives the first two rows for b = 5, with the second
row requiring a branch of order three to avoid overlap. If there is exactly
one singleton, order four is forbidden and there can be at most one branch
of order two. Such a branch excludes order three and gives total only three.
Therefore there is no branch of order two, leaving at least two branches of
order three. Finally, two or more singletons exclude orders three and four.
Two branches of order two together with a singleton are also forbidden, and a
single branch of order two permits at most two singletons. In either case the
total is below five. Conversely, none of the listed profiles has a submultiset
of total b.                                                                  □
   The one-vertex rooted block needs only a label coprime to the center.
For a two-vertex block, a coprime pair with at least one permitted root
label suffices. The next lemma gives the remaining two forms. It is stated
for graphs, so it applies equally to a coprimality host and to a temporarily
enlarged host.
Lemma 5.6.4. The following rooted host assertions hold.
  (1) If a graph on {a, b, d} contains ab, bd, and O contains b and at least
      one of a, d, then U1 (H, O) holds.
  (2) If a graph on {a, b, e, f } contains ab, ae, af, be, and {a, b} ⊆ O, then
      U1 (H, O) holds.
Proof. Every three-vertex tree is a path. Its prescribed root is either the
middle vertex, which can receive b, or an endpoint, which can receive the
endpoint belonging to O.
   A four-vertex tree is a star or a path. In the star, put its center at a; a
marked leaf can be put at b. In the path, a marked endpoint can be put at
b using the order b, e, a, f , whereas a marked internal vertex can be put at
b using the order e, b, a, f . All displayed successive pairs are edges of the
host. These choices cover every possible marked position. Forest completion
retains the prescribed root.                                                □
  Thus, for a three-label branch (a, b, d) attached to a center labeled c, the
conditions used in the certificate are
  gcd(a, b) = gcd(b, d) = gcd(c, b) = 1,     gcd(c, a) = 1 or gcd(c, d) = 1.
                                      96
For a four-label branch (a, b, e, f ), it suffices to check the four internal pairs
ab, ae, af, be and the two contact pairs ca, cb. The parity conditions used to
find some of these blocks are not needed for this implication.
5.6.4. Retaining the contact of a three-vertex block. In a few cases, the contact
vertex is retained in the smaller labeling problem. The label it receives may
vary within a certified root set. We must therefore check the attachment for
every label in that set.
Lemma 5.6.5. Let L have n ≥ 5 elements. In the three-vertex detached
alternative of Lemma 5.1.9, the following data suffice to label the tree by L.
For each row of Table 10, choose a set A ⊂ L of size three and a nonempty
set G ⊆ L \ A such that
                                U1 (HL\A , G)
holds. For every g ∈ G, require an ordering (a0 , a1 , a2 ) of A such that the
internal pairs in that row are coprime and gcd(g, ai ) = 1 at every indicated
contact position. The sets and orderings may depend on the row, and the
ordering may depend on g.

Proof. Let u be the contact vertex and F the detached forest. Each com-
ponent of F has exactly one neighbor of u in the original tree: at least
one by connectivity, and at most one by acyclicity. The four rows list ev-
ery possibility on three vertices, up to an isomorphism preserving these
attachments.
   Delete F , retain u, and use the asserted rooted labeling with u marked.
Let g ∈ G be its resulting label. Choose the checked ordering for this g and
put its labels on F according to the appropriate row. The internal edges are
coprime, the contact-position checks prove every edge to u, and there are no
other crossing edges. The two inventories partition L.                     □
   This lemma replaces only the detached alternative. The short-branch
alternative must still be checked for every profile in Lemma 5.6.3. If the
residual assertion is unmarked, one may take G = L \ A, since it then allows
every possible label at the retained contact.

5.6.5. A whole short-branch block. It is sometimes more economical to label
the center and all its short branches as a single block. The appropriate
internal degree bound follows directly from the profile.
Lemma 5.6.6. In the second alternative of Lemma 5.1.9, let k be the branch
profile and put
                                                                        

The subtree consisting of v and all its child branches has order s + 1 and
maximum degree at most d(k). In particular, if
                         L = {q} ⊔ B ⊔ C,         |B| = s + 1,
where d = d(k), q is universal in L, Pd (B) holds, and P(C) holds, then this

                                                                               P
Proof. The degree of v in this subtree is the number of child branches, j kj .
A vertex in a branch of order j has internal branch degree at most j − 1; its

the degree bound. Also d(k) ≥ 2, since a single branch has order below b
and cannot have total s ≥ b.
   Put q on the parent u, label the whole subtree at v by B, and label the
forest outside that subtree and u by C. Every crossing edge meets u, so
universality checks it. The three inventories are disjoint and exhaust L. □
   The bound concerns degrees after deleting the parent. Its use as a terminal
restriction is justified by forest completion at degree at least two. Recursive
deletion of a leaf and its neighbor within the block uses precisely the degree
decrease of Corollary 3.1.3.
5.6.6. Temporary exceptional edges. One node of the initial certificate uses
the repair operation of Section 3.2. Its children still assert ordinary coprime
labelings. Only the graph used for joining these children is enlarged.
Proposition 5.6.7. Let L contain 1 and two distinct powers of two greater
than 1. Form HL+ from HL by adjoining the edges q(2q) for q ∈ Q(L).
Suppose a checked finite construction embeds every tree of order |L| into HL+ .
Then P(L) holds.
   In particular, the three-vertex branch construction may check its contact
labels and internal block edges in HL+ , while using ordinary coprime labelings
for its smaller residual inventories. Its conclusion is unmarked.
Proof. An embedding into HL+ is a bijective labeling whose only possible
noncoprime edges have labels q, 2q with q ∈ Q(L). The input tree is C4 -free,
so Lemma 3.2.1 repairs it. Forest completion gives P(L).
   For the stated application, the detached three-vertex block needs a path
on its three labels, since every three-vertex forest extends to that path. Give
its contact a label adjacent to all other labels in HL+ . In the other alternative,
                                        98
the profiles are (0, k), so every child branch is a single edge. For each branch,
check its internal edge and the edge from its attachment label to the chosen
center. In the retain case, check adjacency from the center label to every
residual label; in the remove case, use a parent label adjacent to all other
labels. These are exactly the edge checks in the proof of Proposition 5.1.10,
now made in HL+ . Ordinary child labelings satisfy them because HL is a
subgraph of HL+ . The repair is applied only after the whole labeling has been
assembled. It is not asserted to preserve a specified root label.              □
5.6.7. The complete certificate. A node in the present arithmetic proof graph
specifies a finite label set, with an allowed root set when one is required. Its
dependencies are the exact smaller inventories in its chosen reduction. The
only same-order dependencies are explicit edge-preserving bijections of label
sets. These are subject to cycle detection.
   The terminal tables use the codes of Lemma 5.1.2. An unrestricted table
includes every unrooted tree of its order. A rooted table includes every rooted
tree, and its first image lies in the stated allowed set. A degree-restricted
table includes exactly those unrooted trees within its declared degree cap.
In every case the checker regenerates the index set and checks equality with
the supplied keys before checking the witnesses. Duplicate keys are rejected
when reading the certificate.
Theorem 5.6.8. Every forest of order n with 1 ≤ n ≤ 299 has a coprime
labeling by [n].
Proof. We apply the soundness argument of Theorem 5.1.11, adding the
implications proved in this section. The supplied arithmetic proof graph has
1195 nodes. Its root list is exactly
                         P([1]), P([2]), . . . , P([299]).
The checker verifies this list, every dependency, every inventory, and the
absence of directed cycles. All nodes are reachable from these roots. Empty
inventories, singletons, and coprime pairs are checked directly; no published
initial interval is accepted as a base.
   Table 11 lists the node types. The local extensions and host transfers use
Lemma 5.1.7 and Lemma 5.1.1. Prime-contact extensions use Lemma 5.6.1.
Detached-block nodes have a checked partition for every size required by
Proposition 3.1.5 or Lemma 5.6.2. A bounded block is proved by a com-
plete degree-restricted table, possibly preceded by leaf-pair reductions with
explicitly checked degree caps.
   For each short-branch node the checker regenerates the entire family (5.1.5)
by the subset-sum recursion of Section 5.1. It requires exactly the cases in
that family. Their simpler forms are justified by Lemma 5.6.3. The branch
inventories and contacts are checked by Proposition 5.1.10 and Lemma 5.6.4;
the alternatives retaining a three-vertex contact or using a whole bounded
block invoke Lemmas 5.6.5 and 5.6.6, respectively. The sole enlarged host
uses Proposition 5.6.7.

5.7. Continuation of the finite prefix
   We now apply the branch-peeling theorem to a consecutive range of orders.
Each step uses only initial intervals already proved and finite assertions about
its retained blocks. We first specify the case lists used in this range, then
explain how the block assertions are certified. This separates coverage of all
input trees from the arithmetic checks for a particular block.
5.7.1. Complete bounded case lists. Fix 3 ≤ b ≤ n − 2. Let O
                                                           bn,b consist of
all short-branch profiles h with
          b ≤ |h| ≤ min(2b − 2, n − 2),      o(h) > 0    if n is even.
Here and below the short-branch condition includes the absence of a subprofile
of total b. Unlike On,b , this family does not impose minimality. For even n,
choose a nominal center a as in Theorem 5.2.7, and put
    E = 2⌊(b − 1)/2⌋,       B = max(b, n − a − 1),       H = B + E − 1.

                                      100
Let Ebn,b (a) consist of all short-branch profiles having only even sizes and
satisfying
                             b ≤ |h| ≤ min(H, n − 2).
These enlarged lists allow the same bounded subset-sum enumeration to be
used throughout the range.
Proposition 5.7.1 (Sufficient complete lists). Put ℓ = n − b − 1 and suppose
P([m]) holds for 0 ≤ m ≤ ℓ. Suppose a detached block satisfies the first
condition of Theorem 5.2.7, and that
   (1) Rn (h) holds for every h ∈ O   bn,b ;
   (2) P(I(s, j)) holds for every 1 ≤ j < b and 1 ≤ s ≤ n − j + 1;
   (3) if n is even, every interval in the preceding item of even length admits
        every prescribed root at an odd label.
For even n, also require Dn (h; a(h)) for every h ∈ Ebn,b (a) of total below
B, and Dn (h; a) for every remaining profile in that family. The choice of
a must satisfy all the arithmetic hypotheses of Theorem 5.2.7, including
n − H − 1 > p when a = 2p. Then P([n]) holds.
Proof. The ordinary family contains On,b : it has the same order and parity
bounds and merely omits the minimality test. The even family contains
        −            +
both En,b (a) and En,b (a). Indeed, H ≥ B, all low totals are below B, and
the high family has upper bound H. The same bound n − 2 is imposed
throughout. Its low profiles are checked with their individual centers, and
its high profiles with a, as required. Finally, the interval list contains every
member of In,b ; its rooted part includes every even length with odd start.
Thus every hypothesis of Theorem 5.2.7 holds.                                 □
   For completeness, the profile enumeration can be described without ref-
erence to any stored list. Process the possible sizes j in increasing order.
Given the chosen multiplicities and the set R of attainable sums at most b,
adjoining hj copies of j replaces R by
                  {r + uj : r ∈ R, 0 ≤ u ≤ hj , r + uj ≤ b}.
Start with R = {0} and reject a choice as soon as b ∈ R. Bound each
multiplicity by the remaining total divided by j. At the final size, test the
lower bound and the stated parity condition. Every bounded profile has
a unique sequence of multiplicities, so this procedure enumerates exactly
the displayed families. The checker requires equality of the regenerated and
supplied case sets; it rejects missing and repeated cases.
5.7.2. Certificates for the arithmetic blocks. A block model is a graph G on
j indexed positions, together with a nonempty allowed set R. Its assertion
is U1 (G, R). For an ordered label inventory L = {x1 , . . . , xj } and a contact
value d, a block record supplies a permutation ρ of the positions. We check
             uv ∈ E(G) ⇐⇒ gcd(xρ(u) , xρ(v) ) = 1 (u ̸= v),
(5.7.1)
                  u ∈ R ⇐⇒ gcd(xρ(u) , d) = 1.
                                       101
Thus one certified model can serve several arithmetic inventories, but the
entire graph and allowed set are checked for each use. For a plain interval
we take d = 1; for an odd-root interval we take d = 2. For a retained branch,
d is its proposed center label. The value d = 1 serves only to specify an
unrestricted root menu; it need not denote a separate contact vertex in that
application.
   There are two ways of proving the model assertion. The first gives a
complete rooted-tree table, with a permutation in each row whose root
image lies in R. The second binds the model to a recursive host certificate
of Section 5.1. Only split hosts, complete marked tables, and centroid
reductions are needed here.
   We specify the centroid choices to make the treatment of inherited marks
explicit. A node asserts Uk (H, O). For each case (h; (ji , ti )i ) in (5.1.4), its
record gives a center c, belonging to O if h = 1, and an ordered partition of
the other positions into sets Si of sizes ji . The child graph must be exactly
H[Si ] in the recorded order. The following choices suffice:
    (1) If c is universal in H, use O ∩ Si with mark budget ti .
    (2) If c is adjacent to every member of O \ {c}, use O ∩ Si with budget
         min(ji , ti + 1).
    (3) If k = 0, use NH (c) ∩ Si with budget 1.
The general choice NH (c) ∩ O ∩ Si when ti > 0, and NH (c) ∩ Si when ti = 0,
is also valid with budget min(ji , ti + 1). It is accepted by the checker but
is not needed in the certificates for this range. A larger child budget is
permitted. If the child’s designated set is all its positions, an unmarked child
assertion suffices.
   The centroid case list is regenerated as all multisets of pairs 1 ≤ j ≤ ⌊m/2⌋,
0 ≤ t ≤ j, with total size m − 1 and total mark count k − h, separately
for h = 0, 1 with h ≤ k. Listing pairs in nondecreasing lexicographic order
removes repetitions. These are exactly the cases in (5.1.4). In particular,
equal component orders do not justify discarding different distributions of
marks.
Proposition 5.7.2 (Soundness of the block checks). Suppose every terminal
table has its complete index set and valid witnesses, every split node meets
Lemma 5.1.5, and every centroid node passes the checks above. Suppose the
dependency graph is acyclic. Then every node asserts its stated Uk (H, O).
   A model (G, R) is certified either by a complete rooted table or by an edge-
preserving bijection σ : V (H) → V (G) from a proved node. If R ̸= V (G),
require σ(O) ⊆ R and either k ≥ 1 or O = V (H). If R = V (G), any proved
mark budget suffices. Every arithmetic block passing (5.7.1) then satisfies
U1 (HL , Od (L)).
Proof. Induct through the dependency graph. Complete marked tables and
split nodes are valid by Proposition 5.1.3 and Lemma 5.1.5. At a centroid
node, the first choice above is the first alternative of Proposition 5.1.6. In
the second choice, O ∩ Si is contained in NH (c) because c ∈/ Si . It therefore
                                       102
handles both the inherited marks and the attachment. The third choice has
no inherited marks. The general intersection choice likewise meets the second
alternative of that proposition. The regenerated case list is complete, so the
proposition proves the parent assertion. This establishes the induction.
   A complete rooted table proves the model assertion directly. For a trans-
ferred model with a proper allowed set, the source assertion permits one mark:
either k ≥ 1, or its designated set is unrestricted and imposes no condition.
The inclusion σ(O) ⊆ R then permits Lemma 5.1.1. If R is unrestricted,
forget any source marks, transfer U0 (H), and observe that the root condition
in G is automatic. Thus in every case the model satisfies U1 (G, R). Applying
the same transfer lemma to u 7→ xρ(u) proves the arithmetic assertion. □
  The detached block needs only P. In the direct format it has a complete
unrooted table. In the recursive format its labels are bijected to a proved
host, with every gcd edge checked, and any mark condition is forgotten. This
use does not require a rooted conclusion.
5.7.3. Assembly through order one thousand.
Theorem 5.7.3. Every forest of order n with 1 ≤ n ≤ 1000 has a coprime
labeling by [n].
Proof. We induct on n, from 300 through 1000, using Theorem 5.6.8 as the
base. Whenever one of n − 2, n − 1, n is prime, use Lemma 5.6.1; its required
order p − 1 is smaller than n. There are 302 such steps. For the other
399 orders use Proposition 5.7.1. The supplement contains one certificate
for each of these orders, and its checker reconstructs this exact division by
trial-division primality tests. No order is omitted or accepted from a saved
range report.
   For each branch-peeling step, the checker reads b, the power of two c, and,
when used, the odd prime p. It recomputes ℓ = n − b − 1, the nominal center
a, and the bounds B, H. In the exchange case it checks
               2p ≤ n < 3p,        c − p ≥ b,       n − H − 1 > p.
It verifies that the detached inventory is exactly {ℓ + 1, . . . , n} \ {q0 } and that
q0 is universal in [n]. Its block assertion is proved by the direct or recursive
checks just described.
   For every regenerated ordinary profile, the recorded center is universal
and the branch blocks give exactly (5.2.2). For every even profile, the parent
is universal and the blocks give exactly (5.2.3); the center must equal a when
the total is at least B. Each branch model is bound to its actual block and
contact by (5.7.1). Finally, every interval of every required length and start
is checked, with the odd-root condition for every even length when n is even.
Hence all the arithmetic assertions of Proposition 5.7.1 hold. Its remaining
hypothesis concerns only orders at most ℓ < n, already proved by induction.
   Table 12 records the complete finite check. The block proofs include
6567024 rooted witnesses and 13274 detached-block witnesses in the direct

   The last step gives a small example of the construction. For n = 1000, take
b = 3, c = 512, and p = 499, so a = 998, B = 3, and H = 4. The detached
inventory is {998, 999, 1000}, with universal contact 997 and exterior [996].
It labels the three-vertex path by placing 999 at its middle; forest completion
gives its unmarked assertion. There are no ordinary profiles: using a branch
of order one would create a subprofile of total three as soon as the total
reached three. The only even retained profile consists of two branches of
order two. Its exact partition is
   {995, 996, 997, 998, 999, 1000} = {997, 998} ⊔ {995, 996} ⊔ {999, 1000}.
Each pair is consecutive, and its attachment may receive the odd label
995 or 999, respectively, both coprime to 998. All omitted intervals have
length two and can put their attachment at the odd endpoint. Finally,
512 − 499 = 13 ≥ 3 and 1000 − H − 1 = 995 > 499, so the center exchange
applies whenever it is needed. This checks the hypotheses at the endpoint
without any new tree table.
   The supplement includes the exact case inventories, every model and
host proof, and all permutations used in their arithmetic transfers. The
checker uses integer arithmetic throughout. Its default run checks the initial
                                     104
certificate through 299 before checking this continuation. The separate
continuation run treats Theorem 5.6.8 as its explicit base. We next extend
the host rules while keeping the same arithmetic case lists.
5.7.4. Reusing host proofs and removing leaves. A block need not have a new
centroid decomposition if a proved host of the same order already embeds in
it. By Lemma 5.1.1, a bijection from a source host H ′ to a target host H
suffices whenever it preserves every source edge and, for a marked assertion,
sends the entire source allowed set into the target allowed set. The source
mark budget must be at least the target budget. In the certificates below,
we additionally require e(H ′ ) < e(H). This last condition is not needed for
the labeling implication; it gives a decreasing parameter for the proof graph.
   The following reductions are the local extensions of Lemma 5.1.7, applied
inside a larger host. We state the inherited mark conditions explicitly, since
a centroid reduction may have introduced several marks.
Lemma 5.7.4 (Leaf reductions with inherited marks). Let H be a graph of
order m ≥ 2, let O ⊆ V (H), and let q be universal in H.
    (1) For 0 ≤ k ≤ m, suppose q ∈ O whenever k > 0. If
                         Umin(k,m−1) (H − q, O \ {q})
        holds, then Uk (H, O) holds.
    (2) Suppose m ≥ 3, k ∈ {0, 1}, and a ̸= q is a vertex of H. Require
        q ∈ O if k = 1. If
                          Uk (H − {a, q}, O \ {a, q})
         holds, then Uk (H, O) holds.
In either assertion, a larger child mark budget suffices. If the child designated
set is its entire inventory, an unmarked child assertion suffices.
Proof. For the first assertion, choose a leaf x of the input tree and give it
the label q. The remaining tree has at most min(k, m − 1) marks, so apply
the child assertion to it. If x was marked, its label is allowed. Its unique
incident edge is valid because q is universal. The two inventories are disjoint
and exhaust the host.
   For the second assertion, choose an unmarked leaf x. Such a leaf exists:
the endpoints of a longest path are leaves, and at most one vertex is marked.
Let y be its neighbor. Give x the label a and y the label q. Label the
remaining forest by the child assertion, retaining all marks that remain. If
y was marked, its label belongs to O; every other marked vertex receives a
label in O \ {a, q}. The only edge incident to x joins it to y. Every other
edge leaving the remaining forest also meets y, whose label is universal. The
child assertion checks the internal edges, and the disjoint inventories give
bijectivity.
   Forest completion gives the same assertions for forests. The observa-
tions about larger budgets and unrestricted designated sets follow from the
definition of Uk .                                                           □
                                       105
  The restriction to at most one mark in the second assertion ensures that
an unmarked leaf can be chosen. A path whose two endpoints are marked
has no such leaf. Thus this rule cannot be used at a node with two marks
merely because its child certificate allows two marks.
Proposition 5.7.5 (Soundness of the enlarged host system). Add the fol-
lowing rules to the block system of Proposition 5.7.2: a same-order transfer
satisfying Lemma 5.1.1 and having strictly fewer source edges, and either
leaf reduction of Lemma 5.7.4. Require every child inventory and designated
set in a leaf reduction to be the exact inherited ones. Then every completely
checked node asserts its stated Uk (H, O), and the arithmetic binding rule
(5.7.1) remains valid.
Proof. Order host assertions by the lexicographic pair
                                (|V (H)|, e(H)).
A centroid child has at most half the parent order. A leaf reduction decreases
the order by one or two. A transfer preserves the order and strictly decreases
the number of edges. Thus every dependency has smaller rank. Since both
coordinates are nonnegative integers, induction on this rank is well founded.
  The terminal, split, and centroid nodes are justified as in Proposition 5.7.2.
At a transfer node apply Lemma 5.1.1, checking the whole source allowed set
when the target budget is positive. At a leaf node apply Lemma 5.7.4 to the
exact induced child graph. Its inherited designated set and sufficient mark
budget are part of the checked hypotheses. These implications prove the
induction. The arithmetic binding is then the same composition of bijections
as before.                                                                   □
   The extra host rules change neither the retained profiles nor the integer
labels required by a branch-peeling step. Every block still carries its full root
assertion. In particular, a transfer with no marks may ignore the designated
sets, but this does not make it a valid transfer of a rooted block. The block’s
final use must separately pass the root condition in (5.7.1).
5.7.5. Extension through order 1352.
Theorem 5.7.6. Every forest of order n with 1 ≤ n ≤ 1352 has a coprime
labeling by [n].
Proof. Start from Theorem 5.7.3 and proceed through all 352 integers from
1001 to 1352. Trial-division primality checks identify 136 orders of the form
p, p + 1, or p + 2. For each, Lemma 5.6.1 applies to the already proved order
p − 1.
    For every other order the supplement supplies one branch-peeling cer-
tificate. Its acceptance conditions for the detached block, retained profiles,
interval assertions, initial-interval dependencies, and center exchange are
exactly those used in the proof of Theorem 5.7.3. They are rechecked for
the new numerical inventories. Only the host proof system is enlarged, as in
                                       106
Proposition 5.7.5. All case sets are regenerated, every arithmetic transfer is
checked, and every referenced host node is verified. Hence Proposition 5.7.1
applies at each of these 216 orders. Its required initial intervals have orders
at most n − b − 1 < n, so induction supplies them.
   Table 13 gives the complete division of the certificates by their host rules.
The check covers 163165 retained profiles, 2266427 interval assertions, and
867973 retained-branch assertions. There are 3902347 terminal witnesses
and 21462240 centroid cases. All terminal witnesses, all child inventories
and mark budgets, all 11323 same-order transfers, and all 63825 leaf-pair
reductions pass. Each transfer preserves every required edge and decreases
the edge count; each leaf-pair node has at most one mark. The one-vertex
leaf rule is supported by the checker but is not needed in these certificates.
   The index contains exactly the non-prime-contact orders in the range, and
the induction visits every integer in order. The fresh verification therefore
establishes all its premises, rather than importing an accepted range from
an earlier report. Proposition 5.7.5 supplies the block assertions, Proposi-
tion 5.7.1 gives the next initial interval, and induction proves the theorem. □

5.8. Completing the finite prefix
   The last seven orders of the finite argument use the minimal cores of
Section 5.2. Their larger branch blocks are supplied by the bipartite hosts
already proved. Two further marked reductions allow these hosts to be used
inside the recursive certificates. We prove those reductions, specify the source
graphs, and then check the seven induction steps together.
5.8.1. Deleting marked vertices. Deleting a prescribed vertex reduces the
number of marks by one, although it may leave several components. This is
why our host assertions are formulated for forests as well as trees.
                                      107
Lemma 5.8.1. Let H have order m, let O ⊆ V (H), and let u ∈ O be
universal in H.
    (1) If m ≥ 2, 1 ≤ k ≤ m, and
                            Uk−1 (H − u, O \ {u})
       holds, then Uk (H, O) holds.
   (2) Suppose m ≥ 4, choose a ̸= u, and assume
                         U2 (H − {u, a}, O \ {u, a}).
       If H has a spanning path with both endpoints in O, then U2 (H, O)
       holds.
Proof. For the first assertion, take a forest with at most k marks. Choose
a marked vertex v if one exists, and otherwise choose any vertex. Delete v,
embed the remaining forest using the child assertion, and send v to u. At
most k − 1 marks remain, including when the original marked set was empty.
The inventories are disjoint and exhaustive. Every restored edge meets u,
and a mark on v has an allowed image. This proves the first assertion.
   For the second, it suffices to consider a tree, by Lemma 3.1.1. If it has an
unmarked leaf x, delete x and its neighbor y. Embed the remaining forest
using the assumed two-mark assertion, and send x to a and y to u. All
remaining marks are respected; a mark on y receives u ∈ O. Every edge
meeting a deleted vertex has an endpoint at y, so all restored edges are
allowed.
   Otherwise every leaf is marked. A tree has at least two leaves and there
are at most two marks, so there are exactly two leaves, both marked. The
identity                                      X
                  #{v : d(v) = 1} = 2 +             (d(v) − 2)
                                           v:d(v)≥3
shows that no vertex has degree greater than two. Hence the tree is a path,
with precisely its endpoints marked. Map it in order to the assumed spanning
path of H. The endpoint condition respects both marks and the path edges
respect every tree edge. This completes the proof.                        □
   The path condition in the second assertion is checked by one permutation
of the entire host inventory. Checking only the child with two marks would
leave the marked-endpoint path case untreated.
5.8.2. Source graphs for the larger blocks. We use the graph Jt (X) defined
in Section 5.5: it is obtained by adjoining t universal vertices to X. For a
graph X and t ≥ 1, write Rt (X) for the graph on
                      V (X) ⊔ U ⊔ {ζ, e},       |U | = t,
whose edges are those of X, all edges meeting U , and all edges from ζ to
V (X). Thus every member of U is universal, ζ is adjacent to every other
vertex except e, and e is adjacent only to U . Write Rt+ (X) when the edge
ζe is also added. In the latter graph ζ is universal as well.
                                     108
  The subscripts on the arithmetic hosts in the next statement refer to their
own defining sections.
Proposition 5.8.2. Each of the following source graphs admits a bijective
embedding of every forest of its order, sending any specified vertex to any
specified added universal vertex:
    (1) R4 (X0 ) and R4+ (X0 ), of order 26, for the sparse host X0 of Sec-
         tion 5.4;
    (2) J4 (X1 ) and R3 (Xi ), i = 2, 3, 4, of order 26, for the other four hosts
         of that section;
    (3) J5 (X), of order 28, for the 9-by-14 host X in Theorem 5.3.9;
    (4) J6 (Xi ), i = 1, 2, 3, of order 30, for the first three hosts of Section 5.5.
Consequently, an edge-preserving bijection from one of these sources to a
host H, taking one of its added universal vertices into O, proves U1 (H, O).
Proof. Proposition 5.4.5 supplies the covering assertions for the first two
items. Proposition 5.4.4 then gives their rooted embeddings. Adding the
edge ζe preserves all these embeddings. In R4+ (X0 ), exchanging ζ with a
member of U preserves every edge, since both are universal; hence ζ is also
an allowed prescribed image.
   The host in the third item covers (9, 14, 6), by the complete verification
in Theorem 5.3.9. Apply Proposition 5.3.3 with b = 9, k = 4, and D = 6.
The first three hosts in the fourth item cover (9, 15, 6), by Proposition 5.5.1.
Apply the same extension with b = 9, k = 5, and D = 6. These constructions
require only the stated bipartite edges and the added universal vertices.
   For the consequence, fix an added universal vertex whose image lies in O.
Embed the input forest with its marked vertex there, and compose with the
supplied bijection. Every edge is preserved, and the mark lies in O. If no
vertex is marked, designate an arbitrary one.                                □
   A source certificate records that entire bijection. The check reconstructs
the bipartite source from its stated inventory, then checks every source edge
and the distinguished root image. Extra edges in the target cause no difficulty.
The covering assertions themselves are supplied by the complete certificates
of Sections 5.3–5.5; a graph name or a previously saved acceptance report
does not replace them.
5.8.3. Soundness of the endpoint certificates.
Proposition 5.8.3. Enlarge the system of Proposition 5.7.5 by the two
rules of Lemma 5.8.1, the source transfers of Proposition 5.8.2, and the
following previously proved extensions: a fixed-root host with an added leaf,
an unmarked host with an added universal vertex, and a fixed-root host with
an attached triangle. Every accepted host assertion in this enlarged system is
valid. The arithmetic model binding of (5.7.1) remains valid.


                                        109
Proof. Use induction on (|V (H)|, e(H)), ordered lexicographically, as in
Proposition 5.7.5. A source transfer is a terminal implication from Propo-
sition 5.8.2; it does not create a dependency on another host of the same
order. Every other new rule has a child of smaller order.
   For marked deletion, check the universal image in O, the exact induced
child graph and inherited designated set, and the child budget k − 1. For
two-mark deletion, check these conditions with two deleted positions and
child budget two. Also check that the extra path record uses every position
exactly once, has both endpoints in O, and preserves every consecutive
edge. The lemma then applies. A larger child budget suffices, as does an
unrestricted child designated set.
   The single-leaf and universal-vertex extensions are Lemma 5.1.7(4) and
(2), respectively. The triangle extension is Lemma 5.1.8. In the leaf and
triangle rules the child has a singleton designated set and a budget of at
least one, and the conclusion is unmarked. In the universal-vertex rule the
child need only be unmarked; if the conclusion has a mark, its new universal
position must lie in O. Each rule checks the full residual inventory and all
required source edges. These checks supply exactly the hypotheses of the
cited lemmas.
   All recursive assertions therefore follow by induction. The final arithmetic
check is unchanged: compose the host-to-model and model-to-label bijections,
preserving all edges and the entire required root menu.                      □

                                      111
            6. Interval certificates through order 34999
   This part covers the orders from 1360 through 34999 by certificates on
intervals of orders. Sections 6.1–6.5 provide the ingredients: local probability
bounds, type assignments controlled by decomposition height and in the
original forest, finite arithmetic bounds, and the directed rational evaluation
of probabilities. Section 6.6 states the certificate criterion on an integer
interval (Theorem 6.6.6), and Sections 6.7 and 6.8 verify it for the orders
1360–1521. Sections 6.9–6.11 continue through 3499 with modified transport
and Hall estimates, and Section 6.12 continues through 34999 with retained
positions.

6.1. Sharper local probability bounds
   We return to the matching construction. The local estimate in Corol-
lary 4.4.2 uses only a degree bound and an expectation. For the finite
parameter ranges we can also retain the number of conflicting labels, the de-
grees in the original forest, and the fact that the deletion counts are integers.
We establish these refinements before applying them to numerical intervals.
   The probability space is the one in Section 4.4: types and tags are fixed,
and the bijections within the classes are independent and uniform. A fixed
set of bI half-labels in ΛI therefore occupies a uniform bI -subset of AI . This
observation allows us to regard the local conflict count as a sum sampled
without replacement from the class degree lists.
6.1.1. Comparison with a binomial variable. The comparison between sam-
pling with and without replacement is due to Hoeffding [4, Theorem 4]. We
include the argument, followed by the two elementary convexity steps that
give the form needed here. For a real number x, write x+ = max(x, 0).
Lemma 6.1.1 (Convex comparison). Let g ≥ 1 be an integer. For each
i ∈ [g], let xi1 , . . . , ximi ∈ [0, d], where mi ≥ 1 and d > 0, and fix an integer
0 ≤ bi ≤ mi . Independently in each class, choose a uniform subset Si ⊆ [mi ]
of size bi , and put
                      g X
                      X                        X
                X=               xij ,   b=        bi ,   m = EX.
                      i=1 j∈Si                 i

If b > 0 and Z is binomial with parameters b, m/(db), then
(6.1.1)                           Eϕ(X/d) ≤ Eϕ(Z)
for every convex function ϕ : R → R. More generally, if B ≥ max(1, b) is
an integer and M ≥ m, put p = min(1, M/(dB)). Then (6.1.1) holds with Z
binomial with parameters B, p for every increasing convex ϕ. If b = 0, then
X = 0 surely.
Proof. Divide all entries by d. We compare the resulting sum with three
successive sums of independent variables, preserving its mean until the final
possible enlargement of the Bernoulli parameters.
                                         112
                                                             x1 , . . . , x a
  Step 1. Sampling with replacement. Consider one population P
and a sample of size r ≤ a. For an integer vector c ≥ 0 with j cj = r,
define                                           
                             1 X X
                     F (c) =      ϕ      cj xπ(j)  ,
                             a! π     j
where the sum is over all permutations. Suppose two coordinates of c are
u ≥ 2 and 0. Pair the permutations by interchanging the population entries
x, y in those two positions. If the contribution from the other positions is s,
the two arguments are s + ux and s + uy. After replacing (u, 0) by (u − 1, 1),
the arguments become
                   s + (u − 1)x + y,          s + x + (u − 1)y.
They are convex combinations of the original arguments, with coefficients
1 − 1/u and 1/u in opposite order. Hence the sum of their ϕ-values does not
increase.
   As long as some coordinate is at least two, there is a zero coordinate,
since r ≤ a. Repeating the replacement terminates: the integer j c2j
                                                                        P

strictly decreases. The final vector has r ones and all other coordinates
zero. Its value of F is the expectation for sampling without replacement. In
sampling with replacement, condition on the unordered multiplicities of the
chosen population positions. Their assignment to positions is uniform, so
the conditional expectation is the corresponding F (c). Averaging proves that
replacement can only increase the convex expectation. We may condition on
the sum from all other classes and apply the same argument to one class at
a time.
   Step 2. Bernoulli variables. After Step 1, all b draws are independent and
belong to [0, 1]. For 0 ≤ w ≤ 1 and any s,
                    ϕ(s + w) ≤ (1 − w)ϕ(s) + wϕ(s + 1).
Conditioning on the other draws and averaging over w replaces this draw
by an independent Bernoulli variable of the same mean without decreasing
the expectation. Repeating gives a sum of b independent Bernoulli variables
whose parameters have sum m/d.
   Step 3. Equal parameters. For two Bernoulli parameters u, v and a fixed
value s of the remaining sum, the expectation is
                                                                       
    ϕ(s) + (u + v) ϕ(s + 1) − ϕ(s) + uv ϕ(s + 2) − 2ϕ(s + 1) + ϕ(s) .
The last coefficient is nonnegative. Replacing u, v by their average therefore
cannot decrease this expression. On the compact set of parameter vectors
with sum m/d, choose a maximizer of the convex expectation, and among
these one minimizing the sum of squares of the parameters. If two parameters
differed, averaging them would give another maximizer with a smaller sum
of squares. Thus all parameters are m/(db), proving the first assertion.
   For the second assertion, insert B − b Bernoulli variables with parameter
zero before Step 3. This gives parameter m/(dB), which is at most p.
                                        113
Coupling Bernoulli variables by the same independent uniform variables
shows that increasing all parameters to p cannot decrease the expectation
of an increasing function. This also covers b = 0 by starting with B zero
parameters.                                                             □
  For an integer B ≥ 1, 0 ≤ p ≤ 1, and 0 < t ≤ B, define

Proof. When b > 0, Proposition 6.1.5, with B0 = nβ, bounds log EeλKn
by n(λθ − a). Markov’s inequality gives e−na . For a > 0, this is at most
e−ℓa ; otherwise use the bound one. An empty sample has zero probability of
exceeding the positive threshold. The integer-threshold replacement follows
by the event inclusion proved in Proposition 6.1.3.                      □
  There is no need to solve for an optimal λ: each fixed positive choice gives
a valid upper bound. Numerical evaluation must bound the logarithmic
moment upwards and the resulting positive rate downwards. A numerical
search for a parameter and a verification of its inequality are separate
operations.
6.1.4. Which local events must be checked. Different odd labels can give the
same conflict event. More generally, one event may contain another. We use
this inclusion before taking the union bound.
Lemma 6.1.7 (Maximal prime supports). Fix the class permutations and
the original degrees da . For a block type J and a finite set P of primes greater
than z, put

odd labels, put
                            P (y) = {p > z : p | y}.

One may first remove any primes that divide no available half-label. In
particular, if every half-label is at most ⌊u/2⌋, all primes greater than u/2
may be removed.
                                            118
Proof. The divisibility indicator in (6.1.14) is increasing under inclusion of
P , proving the first claim. Every support in a finite family is contained in a
maximal member of that family, with the same J. This proves inclusion of
the left union in the right union. Each maximal support is itself the support
of a label in Y , proving the reverse inclusion. Removing a prime dividing no
available half-label leaves every indicator unchanged. A positive integer at
most ⌊u/2⌋ cannot have a prime divisor greater than u/2.                     □
Corollary 6.1.8 (Simultaneous local estimates). In the matching setup of
Section 4.4, fix positive thresholds LJ for the label types being estimated
probabilistically. For each maximal support event in Lemma 6.1.7, let εJ,P
be an upper bound for Pr{K(J, P ) ≥ LJ }. Then, with probability at least
                                   X X

Proof. For fixed J, P , let bI be the number of half-labels in ΛI divisible by a
prime in P , setting bI = 0 when I ∩ J ̸= ∅. Their assigned positions form
independent uniform bI -subsets of the classes. Thus K(J, P ) has precisely
the sampling form used in this section. The local deletion count for y is at
most K(T (y), P (y)), as proved in Section 4.4. Use (6.1.15) and the union

A final union bound with the additional failure event proves the last assertion.
No independence between these events is required.                             □
   The thresholds must be common within each family to which Lemma 6.1.7
is applied. With several degree targets, apply it separately within each target
class, or use a common smaller threshold. Labels protected deterministically
by Lemma 4.5.2 need not occur in these families. Distinct maximal supports
with the same numerical bound still contribute separately to the sum; only
actual event inclusion or equality justifies deleting an event. Likewise, this
reduction does not change the multiplicities of labels in a global edge count.
   Corollary 6.1.8 supplies the local probability term in Corollary 4.5.7. For
a numerical order interval, the remaining task is to construct the type
assignment, establish all mean and population bounds uniformly, and verify
the matching budgets. The present section proves the probability estimates
conditional on those quantities; it does not assert a new interval of prime-
labelable orders.



                                      119
6.2. Assignments controlled by decomposition height
   The connected construction of Section 4.3 separates mixed components
until each contains one A-vertex. Its cost depends on how quickly these
components can be split by deleting vertices of B. We now count the cuts
by the height of such a decomposition. Integer bounds for this height retain
information that is lost in a single geometric contraction factor.
   We first sharpen the rounding and tag estimates used at the end of the
construction. Neither improvement changes the two preserved measures. The
resulting assignment theorem will keep the separator positions and give all
of them the full tag.
6.2.1. Rounding without a prescribed sign.
Lemma 6.2.1 (Averaging the slot matchings). Under the hypotheses of
Lemma 4.3.3, let q be the number of positive quotas and let R = b − a be the
span of the item weights. There is an integral assignment with exact quotas
such that
                                     1X           qR
(6.2.1)                 |ϵi | ≤ R,        |ϵi | ≤    .
                                     2 i           4
No sign is prescribed for an individual error. The assignment may be chosen
from a finite convex decomposition of the fractional item–slot matrix into
perfect matchings.
Proof. Use the sorted slots and their convex decomposition from the proof of
Lemma 4.3.3. Choose a matching with the probabilities in that decomposition.
For color i, write Xi for its matched degree sum and Ti for its fractional
target. The decomposition gives EXi = Ti . Let Li , Ui be the sums of the
smallest and largest weights in its slots. Every matching satisfies
                      Li ≤ Xi ≤ Ui ,         Ui − Li ≤ R.
If Ui > Li , the chord bound for the convex function (x − Ti )+ gives
                                (Ui − Ti )(Ti − Li )   Ui − L i
               E(Xi − Ti )+ ≤                        ≤          .
                                     Ui − L i             4
If Ui = Li , the error is zero. Since the sum of the errors is zero for every
matching, their half-ℓ1 norm equals the sum of their positive parts. Its
expectation is therefore at most qR/4. At least one matching attains a value
no larger than this expectation. The individual error bound holds for every
matching, by Lemma 4.3.3.                                                  □
   In a completing tree the relevant degrees lie in [1, D], so the span in this
lemma is D − 1. The concentration bound on a single degree remains D.
A construction requiring a nonpositive error in a distinguished class must
instead use the preferred-sign rounding lemma, or pay explicitly for a possible
positive error.

6.2.2. Integer bounds for the decomposition height. Let U = (AU , BU ) be a
bipartite tree with m = |AU | ≥ 1. Set L(U ) = m − 1. A component with
L(U ) = 0 is terminal. In a nonterminal component we may delete a vertex
b ∈ BU having at least two neighbors in that component. Such a vertex
exists on the path between two AU -vertices. Each component of U − b has a
positive, strictly smaller number of AU -vertices.
   Define its minimum decomposition height by the finite recursion

where the minimum is over the admissible BU -vertices just specified. A

this minimum need not be efficient; it is a finite construction for every input

nondecreasing and aD (L) < L for L > 0. All quantities bi (L), ci (L), Bj (L)

Lemma 6.2.3 (Peeling at a weighted centroid). Let U be a bipartite tree with
L = L(U ) > 0 and ∆U (AU ) ≤ D. A single admissible cut can make every
child’s L-value at most aD (L). For each 1 ≤ j ≤ D, there is a decomposition
through at most j levels, stopping terminal components, for which every
remaining L-value is at most Bj (L).
   More precisely, if a weighted centroid lies in AU , order the positive AU -
counts
P
        in its branches as s1 ≥ · · · ≥ sd > 0, where d ≤ D, and put Si =
  r≤i sr . Deleting its BU -neighbors in that order leaves a central component
with L-value L − Si after the ith cut. Every component detached at that cut
has L-value at most si − 1.
Proof. Use the weighted centroid from Lemma 4.3.4, with weight one on AU
and zero on BU . Every branch has at most ⌊(L + 1)/2⌋ vertices of AU . If
the centroid belongs to BU , deleting it leaves every child with L-value at
most ⌊(L − 1)/2⌋. It is admissible, since otherwise its unique branch would
contain all L + 1 vertices of AU .
   If the centroid belongs to AU , its branch counts sum to L. The largest is
at least ⌈L/D⌉. Delete its neighbor in that branch. The central component
has L-value at most L − ⌈L/D⌉, while every other child has L-value at most
⌊(L − 1)/2⌋. This proves the single-cut assertion.
   For repeated peeling, ignore branches with zero AU -count. The neighbor in
every positive branch has a further AU -neighbor, so its deletion is admissible.
Distinct branches meet only at the centroid. Thus cutting branch i removes
exactly si vertices of AU from the central component, and each detached
component has at most si such vertices. This proves the last assertion.
   Pad the branch counts by zeros to a list of length D. The average of
its first i entries is at least the average of all entries, giving Si ≥ ⌈iL/D⌉.
Also si ≤ ⌊L/i⌋ and si ≤ ⌊(L + 1)/2⌋. Consequently the central remainder
is at most ci (L), and each component detached at step i has remainder
                                         122
at most bi (L). Apply ordinary single cuts to the detached components at
subsequent levels. Monotonicity of aD bounds their remainders at level j by
  j−i
aD    (bi (L)). If fewer than j positive branches exist, the central component
becomes terminal earlier. This proves the bound Bj (L).
   When the fresh centroid is in BU , all children are detached at the first
level and have remainder at most b1 (L). Their further ordinary cuts are
covered by the i = 1 term in Bj (L). Terminal components may always be
left unchanged.                                                             □
  Instead of making all detached components wait until level j, we may
decompose each of them as soon as it appears. The next recursion takes the
better of these two constructions.
  Fix 1 ≤ k ≤ D. Starting with F (0) = 0, define successively for L ≥ 1
                                                               
                                                            
(6.2.7)   Gj (L) = max j + F (cj (L)), max i + F (bi (L))           ,

Only previously defined values occur on the right.
Proposition 6.2.4 (A height bound with a fixed set of stopping levels).
The sequence in (6.2.8) is nondecreasing, satisfies 0 ≤ F (L) ≤ L, and bounds
h(U ) ≤ F (L(U )) for every bipartite tree whose A-degrees are at most D.
Proof. Induct on L. Nondecreasingness is built into the recursion. For a
given j, apply the first j levels of Lemma 6.2.3. Every remaining component
has smaller remainder, at most Bj (L). By induction and monotonicity its
height is at most F (Bj (L)). This gives the candidate j + F (Bj (L)).
   Suppose next that the weighted centroid is in A. Peel j branches, or stop
earlier if they are exhausted, and immediately decompose each detached
component. A component detached at step i has depth at most i + F (bi (L)).
The surviving central component has depth at most j + F (cj (L)). Thus
Gj (L) is another valid bound. If the centroid is in B, its first cut gives the
bound 1 + F (b1 (L)), which is a term of every Gj (L). The same conclusion
follows.
   Every candidate bounds a full decomposition. Their minimum does also,
and increasing it to at least F (L − 1) preserves that property. Finally the
candidate with j = 1 is at most 1 + F (aD (L)) ≤ L by induction. Together
with F (L − 1) ≤ L − 1, this proves F (L) ≤ L.                               □
6.2.3. Choosing the stopping level from the branch counts. The preceding
bound chooses among predetermined levels. We can do better by allowing
that choice to depend on the actual branch counts, while still verifying the
height bound by a short integer test.
   Suppose a nondecreasing sequence H has been defined through L − 1, with
H(0) = 0. For every integer r, set

Proposition 6.2.5 (A height bound with an adaptive stopping level). The
preceding test defines a nondecreasing integer sequence with 0 ≤ H(L) ≤ L.
Every bipartite tree with A-degrees at most D satisfies h(U ) ≤ H(L(U )).

centroid is in B, one cut and (6.2.10) give height at most h.P  Otherwise let

(6.2.15)       max j + H(L − Sj ), max i + H(si − 1)                   .

Here all arguments of H are smaller than L. Figure 10 shows the separate
contributions to this maximum.
   Suppose no j ∈ [d] makes (6.2.15) at most h. There is then a first index
t with t + H(st − 1) > h. Indeed, otherwise the choice j = d works: the
central remainder is zero and the last detached-branch inequality gives d ≤ h.
Moreover t ≤ h + 1, since every positive branch with index h + 1 would
violate its inequality.
   By monotonicity of H, the failure at t implies st ≥ TL (h − t) + 2 = at .
Hence at ≤ u and tat ≤ L, so neither (6.2.11) nor (6.2.12) holds. For j < t,
all detached components fit within height h. The assumed failure at that
stopping point therefore gives j + H(L − Sj ) > h, equivalently Sj ≤ Cj .
But Sj ≥ jat , excluding (6.2.13). Every later branch count is at most
⌊Sj /j⌋ ≤ ⌊Cj /j⌋. Padding by zeros to D terms yields
                   L ≤ Cj + (D − j)⌊Cj /j⌋      (1 ≤ j < t).

stopping point works, so the proposed height bounds h(U ).

If t = L + 1 is tested, then at = 1 and again tat > L. These are all possible
tested indices, so every one satisfies (6.2.12). The choice h = L passes.
It follows that H(L) ≤ L; its imposed lower bound makes the sequence
nondecreasing. This completes the induction.                               □

Theorem 6.2.6 (Type assignment with a height budget). Let Q = (A, B)
be a tree with both parts nonempty, M = |A|, e = e(Q), and ∆Q (A) ≤ D,
where D ≥ 3 is an integer. Prescribe nonnegative integer quotas a1 , . . . , ag
of total M , and put πi = ai /M . There are a coloring of A with these exact
quotas and a set C ⊆ B such that every vertex of B \ C has neighbors of a
single color,
                                                                g(D − 1)
(6.2.17) |C| ≤ CR (M, 2(g − 1)),      |hi | ≤ D − 1,    Hdeg ≤             ,
                                                                    4
where hi = a: c(a)=i dQ (a) − eπi and Hdeg = 12 i |hi |. No preferred error
              P                                   P

sign is required or asserted.
                                            125
Proof. Use the fractional construction of Proposition 4.3.5, preserving A-
count and the sum of degrees in the fixed tree Q. After each pruning, at most
d = 2(g − 1) mixed objects remain. Among the nonterminal mixed objects
choose one of largest actual height h(U ), and cut it at a vertex attaining
the minimum in (6.2.5). The cut belongs to B and contributes zero to both
measures. Give the children their parent’s fractional weights, prune again,
and freeze pure objects as before.
   Consider the first cut at a particular positive height j. No mixed object
of larger height remains. At that moment there are at most d mixed objects
of height j, and every later cut produces children of strictly smaller height
than its parent. Thus at most d objects of height j are ever cut. They are
pairwise disjoint: no descendant of a cut object can have the same height.
Since j = h(U ) ≤ R(L(U )), each contains at least sj vertices of A. There are
at most ⌊M/sj ⌋ such objects. Summing over heights proves the separator
bound.
   Each cut removes a new B-vertex, so the construction terminates. A
terminal mixed component contains just one A-vertex. Its degree in the fixed
tree lies in [1, D], even if all its current neighbors have already been cut.
Apply Lemma 6.2.1 to these last fractional assignments. The residual quotas
are integers, since pure components contribute integer counts. There are at
most g positive residual quotas, giving the degree bounds in (6.2.17). Finally,
every edge outside C lies in one object, and rounding a one-A-vertex object
leaves it monochromatic on A. This proves the neighborhood assertion. □
Corollary 6.2.7 (Retaining all positions with full separator tags). Let
