C these tags already agree with every neighboring type. Change the tags on
C to [s]. The variation cost is at most |C|, as in Lemma 4.3.7, which proves
(6.2.18). Both tag rules give inclusion on every edge of Q, and therefore of
G. Tag recovery does not alter the rounded A-assignment.                  □
                                      126
   The full tag at each separating position has a useful additional consequence:
a label of nonempty type is incompatible with that position. Any allowed pair
involving such a label therefore uses a position outside C, where all adjacent
even half-labels have exactly the position’s tag. This is the hypothesis used
in the support-sensitive forest estimate of Section 4.5. The local bounds
of Section 6.1 may still charge original degrees, with the completion credit
proved there. The degree errors in (6.2.17) themselves refer to Q, whose
edge count is M + N − 1.

   All recursions and cut budgets in this section use integers; an exact
implementation is included in the supplement. The interval calculation retains
the completion-capacity hypothesis and does not replace any arithmetic,
probability, or Hall inequality.

                                      127
6.3. Assignments in the original forest
   A connected completion changes the degree sums used in the probability
estimates. We now give an alternative that uses the original forest throughout.
It preserves a third quantity, the number of opposite-side positions of each
type. The resulting separator may meet both sides. We move its nonempty
types to cheaper positions, then enlarge the tags on the affected neighborhood.
Every position remains available for matching.
   There are two costs to keep separate. Rounding creates signed errors in
the class degree sums. The subsequent exchanges transfer a nonnegative
amount of degree from each nonempty type to the empty type. We retain
both quantities until estimating the deletion means.
6.3.1. Three measures and two choices of separator. For nonnegative integers
v, e, d, define the finite sum
                                            v      e
                                 X                
(6.3.1)             C(v, e, d) =     min d, j , j         .
                                 j≥1
                                            2   2 −1
It counts cuts against both the vertices and the edges available in the forest.
We also need a separator confined to B, particularly when the degrees on A
are small.
Lemma 6.3.1 (A cut confined to the opposite side). Let U = (AU , BU ) be
a bipartite tree with v ≥ 2 vertices and ∆U (AU ) ≤ D. If D ≥ 2, there is a
vertex b ∈ BU such that every component V of U − b satisfies
                                     D−1
                          |V | − 1 ≤      (v − 1).
                                       D
If D = 1, a vertex of BU can be deleted so that every remaining component
is a singleton.
Proof. Choose an ordinary centroid c, as in the proof of Lemma 3.3.1. If
c ∈ BU , all remaining components have order at most v/2, which proves the
first assertion. Otherwise the branch orders at c sum to v −1, and there are at
most D branches. Let t be the largest branch order and let b be its neighbor
of c. Then (v − 1)/D ≤ t ≤ v/2. After deleting b, the component containing
c has order v − t, so its order minus one is at most (D − 1)(v − 1)/D. Every
other component has order at most t − 1, and satisfies the same bound. For
D = 1, every nontrivial component is a star with its center in BU ; delete
that center.                                                                 □

Here S is defined in (4.3.3). No prescribed sign is asserted for any hi .
Proof. Step 1. Preserve the three measures. Start with the connected com-
ponents of G, each carrying color weights πi . Give an object U the vector
                                                                      !
                                              X
                    v(U ) =     |U ∩ A|,              dG (a), |U ∩ B| .
                                             a∈U ∩A
All degrees are measured in the fixed original forest. Apply Lemma 4.3.2
with three coordinates. Freeze pure objects; at most d = 3(g − 1) mixed
objects remain.
   Choose a largest mixed object having more than one vertex. Cut it at
an ordinary centroid for the first construction, or at a vertex of B from
Lemma 6.3.1 for the second. Put the cut vertex in C. Replace the object by
the cut vertex as a singleton and the components of its deletion, each with
the parent’s weights. Keeping the singleton is essential: a cut vertex can
contribute to the preserved measures. The vectors add to the old vector, so
all color targets remain (ai , eπi , N πi ). Prune again and continue. Each cut
is at a new vertex; the process ends with all mixed objects singletons.
   Step 2. Count the cuts. In the ordinary construction, put an object
in bucket j ≥ 1 when 2j ≤ |U | < 2j+1 . Its children have order at most
⌊|U |/2⌋ < 2j , and the cut atom has order one. When bucket j is first cut, no
larger mixed bucket remains. There are at most d objects in that bucket, and
no later cut creates another. Objects cut in one bucket are pairwise disjoint.
Each contains at least 2j vertices and, being a tree, at least 2j − 1 edges of G.
Their number is therefore bounded by every term of the minimum in (6.3.1).
   For the second construction with D ≥ 2, use buckets for |U | − 1 and
contraction ratio (D − 1)/D. The counting argument in Proposition 4.3.5,
now counting all vertices, gives the stated S bound. If D = 1, each cut leaves
only singletons. Thus at most the initially mixed nontrivial objects are cut;
there are at most d, and their disjoint orders are each at least two. This
proves both choices.
                                              129
   Step 3. Round the singletons. The residual quotas on mixed A-singletons
are nonnegative integers, since pure objects contribute integer counts. Apply
Lemma 6.2.1 to their fixed degrees in [0, D]. Its bounds give the last two
inequalities in (6.3.2).
   The residual targets on the B-singletons need not be integers. Write them
as ti = ⌊ti ⌋ + ri , with
                       P
                          0 ≤ ri < 1. Their total is the integer number of these
singletons, so k = i ri is an integer. Round up the k largest remainders
and round the others down. The resulting counts are nonnegative integers
with the correct total. Their variation from the targets is
                                X                     k2  g
                       k−                  ri ≤ k −      ≤ .
                            i rounded up
                                                      g   4
For the middle inequality, the average of the k largest remainders is at least
the average of all g; the case k = 0 is immediate. Assign the singletons to
these counts. The two rounding operations act on different sides and do not
interfere.
   Finally, an edge whose endpoints avoid C stays within one final object. If
that object is pure, its endpoints have equal colors; it cannot be a singleton.
Hence the asserted edge property survives both roundings. For g = 1, color
everything alike and take C = ∅; all errors vanish.                          □
  For an interval lower endpoint ℓ and an original forest with at most n

Indeed, divide each summand of the S bound by n, use M + N ≤ n, and
drop the ceiling and floor in the upper bound. After the terms 0 ≤ j < J,
the remaining sum is at most ρJ /(1 − ρ). For D = 1 the normalized bound
is d/ℓ. Thus a finite evaluation with an explicit geometric remainder also
bounds the cuts throughout an order interval.
6.3.2. Deleting the highest-degree even positions. The following preparation
uses all the special even labels on vertices of largest degree. There is no
reserve of odd positions in this variant. It supplies an edge budget that also
pays for isolated even positions.

Proof. Since S is independent, deleting U does not change the degrees of the
remaining vertices of S in T − K. Every selected vertex therefore had degree
at least D. Their incident edge sets are disjoint, so at least wD edges are
deleted at this step.

neighbor in U ⊆ S. It has at least one such neighbor because T is a nontrivial

6.3.3. Counting the available empty positions. We call a vertex of empty
type a donor when its type is exchanged with a nonempty type on the
separator. We need low-degree donors, including isolated vertices. Their
number depends on the individual empty-class error, not just on its sign.

exchanges. Also e + I ≥ M : every nonisolated A-vertex contributes at least
one to the degree sum. In the preparation above, an order–degree pair with
n − 1 − wD < M is therefore impossible. Such pairs may be omitted from
interval calculations.

additional unsigned rounding error would lose this cancellation.

Proof. The cut budget is nondecreasing in both its vertex and edge arguments.
Proposition 6.3.3 therefore justifies c∗ for every order. Its isolation bound
justifies the paid stock formula, and its excess bound justifies the expression
for δ∗ . Apply Theorem 6.3.7 separately at each order, and divide its bounds
                                        136
by n ≥ ℓ. The maxima are over finite sets of integers, with exact rational
calculations in the stock formula. There is no union bound over orders. □
   For example, at n = 1360 and D = 9, using four types formed from
3, 5, 7, 11, 13, the exact arithmetic gives
             w = 46,      M = 634,           a∅ = 216,     E = 945.
The ordinary cut bound is c = 65. Paying h = 9 gives ℓ1 = 61, ℓ2 = 120,
and all later stock bounds at least 120. Thus the boundary bound is
65 + (65 − 61) = 69. These are construction quantities only; the example
does not check a labeling range.
   The stock counts at different degree thresholds belong to the same order.
Taking the maximum of their combined cost, as above, retains that depen-
dence. These construction bounds still require the arithmetic population
estimates and the matching inequalities before they yield a numerical range
of prime labelings.

6.4. Finite arithmetic for the matching estimates
   The constructions in the preceding two sections fix the type at each even
position and the tag at each odd position. We now express the remaining
expectations and population bounds by finite arithmetic. The distinction
between the original forest and a connected completion remains in force: the
former allows degree transfers to the empty class, while the latter allows a
credit for the added edges.
   Throughout this section, fix integers 12 ≤ ℓ ≤ u and a cutoff 3 ≤ z < ℓ/3.
Partition all odd primes at most z into s ≥ 1 nonempty blocks. Write
T = 2[s] , 0 = ∅, and F = [s] for the type set, its empty type, and its full
type. The block type T and the complete counts C̄I are those of Section 4.1.
All extrema over [ℓ, u] in this section range over integers.

event is indexed by its type J and its prime support P . Inclusion-maximal
supports may replace all supports only with the common-threshold condition
of Lemma 6.1.7; equality of numerical profiles alone does not identify events.

Proof. It suffices to bound the sum of exponential excesses over usable half-
labels, since the mean in (6.4.19) is one plus that sum divided by the usable
population.
   For the upper bound, enlarge the prefix to [mu ], replace each cost by Qu,I ,
and replace n in the exponent by ℓ. Every usable term can only increase.
If 0 ∈ J , each of the rn removed prime half-labels is still in this enlarged
prefix and has Qu,0 (p) ≥ 1: the odd label p itself is counted. Their omitted
exponential excess is therefore at least r− (et/ℓ − 1). If 0 ∈
                                                             / J , omitting the
subtraction remains valid. Powers of two contribute zero in either case. This
proves the upper bound by VI+ and its nonnegativity.
   For the lower bound, use only the half-labels in [mℓ ] and replace their costs
by Qℓ,I and the denominator by u. A removed prime present in this smaller
prefix satisfies p > n/3 ≥ ℓ/3. Its only odd multiple in [ℓ] is p, so its lower-
endpoint cost is exactly κ. At most r+ such primes are removed. Subtracting
that many unit-cost excesses and truncating at zero gives VI− . Finally divide
by the population bounds and use monotonicity of the logarithm.                □
   The proof uses inclusions of the usable sets into the complete endpoint
prefixes. Subtracting a removed term after independently bounding n in its
exponent would give different, generally weaker, bounds. The chosen subtrac-
tions are justified by the endpoint costs of the removed primes themselves.

   The same inequalities hold if each vI− is replaced by a proved nonnegative

in VI− . The subtracted exponential excesses require the opposite directions.
The logarithms are then bounded in their respective directions. A search for
t supplies a candidate; it does not certify any of these inequalities. Directed
rational evaluation will be specified with the probability certificate.
6.4.5. From quota laws to degree targets. The arithmetic law needed on the
position side is the usable quota law π(n) itself. This avoids transporting it
first to a product law and then back to finite populations.
Proposition 6.4.7 (Exact reference laws and local thresholds). Suppose the
retained position side has size R(n) ≥ nρ > 0. Let its tag law be ν(n) and
assume that the construction gives
             tv(β(n), R(n)π(n)) ≤ nε,         βI (n) = R(n)νI (n).

(6.4.22)         {dΓ (y) ≤ tJ R(n)} ⊆ {K(J, P (y))/n ≥ θJ }

                          κI = max πI (n) + v1I=F

for the Hall estimates after transport to the full type.
Proof. Divide the count variation bound by R(n) ≥ nρ to get ∥ν(n) −
π(n)∥TV ≤ v. For J ̸= 0, apply this bound to the indicator of disjointness
from J and then take the minimum over orders. An empty-type label is
compatible with every tag, giving the exact value c0 = 1. If its final degree
fails the target, at least (cJ − tJ )R(n) ≥ nθJ initially incident edges were

same reference law and variation bound.                                    □
   For an original-forest assignment on all R(n) positions, Theorem 6.3.7
permits any ε with nε ≥ g/4 + η, where η bounds the changed tags. For
the connected construction the corresponding count bound is (6.2.18). If
isolated reserve positions are added afterwards, their empty tags change the
count vector; that contribution must also be included before applying the
proposition. The arithmetic law does not remove a combinatorial tag error.
   The exact odd counts give the label caps in Corollary 4.5.4. For full-type
labels, Lemma 4.5.2 applies with R0 = ℓρ and c = cF . Thus the fixed
endpoint set
      E = {y ∈ [u] : y odd, T (y) = F, DB0 (mu , P (y)) ≥ ℓρ(cF − τ )}
contains every label not protected at the degree target τ , provided cF > τ .
Its size bounds the actual exception population; the weaker degree target for
those exceptions still requires a local probability estimate. This separates the
deterministic exception test from the probabilistic estimate on the remaining
events.
   Every quantity introduced here is obtained from a finite prefix count, a
prime support, a class histogram, or an extremum over the stated integer
interval. These formulas connect the constructions to the probability and
Hall criteria. Proving a new interval of orders still requires their simultaneous
acceptance, the supply inequalities in the preparation, and complete coverage
of the residual degrees.

6.5. Rational evaluation of the probability bounds
  The estimates obtained so far involve exponentials and logarithms, but
their acceptance can be reduced to integer comparisons. We give the rounding
rules and prove their directions before assembling the failure budget. The
parameters used to choose a bound are distinct from the arithmetic used to
verify it. Any permitted parameter gives a valid bound; a numerical search
need not find the best one.
  For a positive integer Q and a rational number x, write
                              ⌊Qx⌋               ⌈Qx⌉
                      ⌊x⌋Q =        ,    ⌈x⌉Q =        .
                               Q                   Q


                                      144
All integer floors and ceilings are evaluated exactly. A nonnegative mean
may be rounded upwards when it is used as an upper bound. A positive
decay rate must instead be rounded downwards.

(6.5.8), 0 ≤ r ≤ a whenever a > 0. Thus e−a ≤ e−r . When a ≤ 0, the trivial
probability bound is valid.                                              □



                                        146
   Capping a large rate weakens the bound and is harmless. In contrast,
a small probability cannot be replaced by zero unless the event is proved
impossible. The only zero probabilities used below come from an empty
sample, a zero mean, or a threshold outside the support.
   Table 16 fixes the arithmetic constants. Every positive exponential argu-
ment is checked to lie in [0, 10]. For the reciprocal calculation, rate capping
instead gives a Taylor argument in [0, 64].

       Table 16. Integer constants for directed probability evalua-
       tion. The inequalities above justify the rounding; the number
       of digits alone does not do so.


     Quantity                                          Choice
     Scale for positive exponentials and logarithms    Q = 1080
     Series length                                     N = 128
     Positive exponential grid                         L = 216
     Scale for reciprocal exponentials                 S = 10150
     Rate grid and cap                                 R = 220 , c = 64
     Reciprocal Taylor length                          m(r) = 4⌈r⌉ + 64
     Scale for local means and support bounds          109
     Scale for final binomial bounds                   1080


6.5.3. A moment bound with a finite sample cap. The local bounds can use
the number of half-labels that may conflict, as well as their total expected
degree. The next estimate allows a real upper bound for that number, which
is useful after normalization.
Lemma 6.5.4 (A local moment with a support bound). Let K be a sum of

   A bound on the support of an arbitrary random variable would not by

may take s = DBP /ℓ, with BP from (6.4.15).

 B k         B−k divided by v B . Summing these masses and first moments

this route supplies only the bound one. The mean used for Xn does not
receive a completion credit. That credit belongs to the separate estimate
using the original degrees.
   One may also take the minimum with (ii), or omit that calculation when
another bound is already small. Omitting an estimate cannot invalidate
the remaining ones. Replacing a small positive estimate by zero would be a
different operation and requires a separate proof.
6.5.6. Exact acceptance of a simultaneous event. The final step is an integer
comparison, but it is meaningful only after the events and their multiplicities
have been specified.
Proposition 6.5.6 (A rational failure budget). Fix an order interval and one
construction whose deterministic hypotheses have been proved uniformly on
that interval. At each order let {Fe : e ∈ E} be a finite family of local failure
events that covers all required local degree failures. Suppose rational numbers
pe ∈ [0, 1] bound their probabilities, and rational numbers q1 , . . . , qh ∈ [0, 1]
bound the remaining global failure events. If avoidance of these events implies
the chosen Hall criterion, then
                                X           h
                                            X
(6.5.16)                             pe +         qj < 1
                               e∈E          j=1

                                        150
guarantees a perfect matching at every order of the interval.
   If events with the same certified bound are grouped, their contribution
is mp, where m is the number of events in that group. Events may be
removed from the family only when their failure is already excluded, or their
inclusion in a retained event has been proved. If all bounds have a common
denominator S, condition (6.5.16) is exactly the comparison of their summed
integer numerators with S.
Proof. Fix an actual order and an actual input forest. All construction
choices are made before the class permutations. The union bound makes
the probability of any listed failure at most the left side of (6.5.16). If this
is less than one, an outcome avoiding all failures exists. By hypothesis the
matching criterion applies to that outcome. The bounds hold separately for
each input and order, so this proves the assertion for all of them; there is no
union bound over input forests or over orders.
   Grouping is an exact regrouping of a finite sum and preserves its mul-
tiplicities. Removing an event requires one of the stated reasons, so the
remaining union still covers every relevant failure. Multiplying a strict ratio-
nal inequality by its positive common denominator gives the stated integer
comparison.                                                                   □
   In our application, the local family consists of maximal prime-support
events with a common threshold within each type and degree target, as
in Lemma 6.1.7. A maximal event may represent many labels; conversely,
distinct maximal events with equal arithmetic data still contribute separately.
The three global families are those in Table 2. A Hall range is omitted only
when it is empty, its capped domain is infeasible, or a proved deterministic
estimate excludes all its obstructions. Otherwise its positive deletion budget
and failure estimate must both be checked. For one global event, the minimum
of its Markov and exponential bounds may be taken before summing the
different events.
   The choices in Table 16 make every exponential failure bound an integer
multiple of 1/S. The rounded binomial bound also has that property because
1080 divides S = 10150 . An exact Markov bound may instead be kept as
a rational number, in which case integer cross-multiplication or a common
multiple of the denominators gives the same strict test. Rounding any
additional probability term upwards is permitted; rounding it downwards is
not.
   This establishes the numerical soundness of the probability tests. A range
certificate must still supply the correct arithmetic and construction inputs,
verify the Hall budgets and the deterministic preparation, and cover every
possible residual degree and every integer order claimed. The next assembly
uses these requirements together; the evaluation rules alone assert no new
range of prime labelings.


                                      151
6.6. Preparation and certificates on integer intervals
   We now connect the numerical bounds to an arbitrary input tree. The
order interval and its arithmetic data are fixed first. The tree then determines
a residual degree, and this degree selects a row of the certificate. In particular,
a complete table must account for every degree that can occur after the
preparation, not merely every degree at which a numerical search succeeded.
   We use the preparation of Proposition 6.3.3. All special even labels are
assigned to vertices of largest degree; all remaining odd positions are retained.
Thus no position reserve is introduced in this section. The final matching is
between two sets of the same size.

Corollary 6.6.5 (Uniform caps and a vacuous range). The initial degree of
a label of type J is at least cJ N . The position caps κI are valid after full-type
transport, and pI bounds the normalized number of remaining odd labels of
type I. At least υN labels have empty type and are universal. If the local
degree conditions of Lemma 4.5.1 hold and
(6.6.16)                               pF ≤ τ,
then no deficient set belongs to the full-only range, for any η ≥ τ .
Proof. The tag variation, divided by N ≥ nρ, is at most v. Apply Propo-
sition 6.4.7 and Corollary 4.5.4. The universal labels were established in
Proposition 6.6.1. Finally, every deficient set has size greater than τ N ,
whereas a set containing only full-type labels has size at most pF N ≤ τ N .
These statements contradict each other.                                  □
  The strict numerical margin pF < τ is convenient in a table, but the proof
only needs the weak inequality in (6.6.16). This removes one global deletion
event. It does not remove the local degree estimates for full-type labels.

6.6.5. Soundness of an interval certificate. We can now state the remaining
acceptance conditions without referring to how the parameters were found.
All type assignments and tags in the next theorem are constructed by the
preceding propositions.
Theorem 6.6.6 (Certificate criterion on an integer interval). Suppose (6.6.2)
holds. For each 1 ≤ D ≤ D∗ that can occur, choose an admissible construction
row as in Proposition 6.6.4, with positive quota lower bounds. Form cJ , κI , pI
as above. For each such row suppose the following conditions are verified
uniformly for its feasible orders.

                                       159
6.7. Local degree certificates
   We verify the local conditions of the interval criterion. The possible odd
labels are first divided by type and prime support. We then specify which
full-type labels require the weaker degree target, bound one event for each
maximal support, and add the resulting probabilities. The arithmetic bounds
and the event cover are both checked on the complete endpoint population.
   Throughout this section, fix one of the construction rows of Section 6.6,
on an interval [ℓ, u]. Retain its degree D, cutoff z, blocks, and normalized
construction bounds. Write F for the full type. We may replace an initial
degree bound cJ by a smaller bound ceJ , with ce0 = 1. This permits several
types to use a common lower bound; it does not identify their random conflict
events.
6.7.1. The complete endpoint event cover. For every odd y ≤ u, define
                       Pu (y) = {p : z < p ≤ ⌊u/2⌋, p | y}.
For each type J, let MJ (u) be the inclusion-maximal members of
                     {Pu (y) : 1 ≤ y ≤ u, y odd, T (y) = J}.
An empty type family contributes no event. If its only support is empty, the
empty support is its unique maximal member. To obtain a disjoint record of
the covered labels, assign each y to the first P ∈ MT (y) (u) containing Pu (y),
in lexicographic order. Denote the resulting label sets by YJ,P .
Lemma 6.7.1 (Endpoint coverage and multiplicities). The sets YJ,P partition
all odd integers in [u]. For arbitrary real thresholds LJ and every actual
order n ∈ [ℓ, u],
           [                                      [         [

Events with the same numerical bound may be evaluated together, but that

Proof. Every member of a finite family of sets is contained in a maximal
member. Thus the specified first member exists and assigns each odd label
exactly once. Since every usable half-label is at most ⌊u/2⌋, larger primes
contribute nothing to its conflict indicator. Lemma 6.1.7 now gives (6.7.1):
each actual label belongs to the endpoint population and its support is
contained in its assigned maximal support, with the same type. Apply the
union bound once to each pair (J, P ). Several labels may be covered by the

                                        160
same event; conversely, equality of probability bounds does not make distinct
events equal. Grouping equal summands proves the last assertion.           □
  The cover is formed before deleting the odd core labels. This can include
events unnecessary for a particular tree, but cannot omit an event needed by
that tree. In particular, no choice of the core primes is hidden in the event
enumeration.

6.8.1. Minimization at fixed mass. The high-range deletion bound depends
on the number of selected positions. We therefore keep that mass fixed before
taking a minimum of the compatible pairs. The next lemma also gives a

Lemma 6.8.1 (Concavity between subset masses). Let Cij ≥ 0 be a finite
matrix and let pi , qj ≥ 0 be coordinate caps. Suppose
                          h                                               i
                       I = max{0, 1 −     j qj },                i pi }

                                                        min{1,

(6.8.1)                       G(t) = min           Cij xi wj ,

where the minimum is over
                                             X                       X
          0 ≤ x ≤ p,       0 ≤ w ≤ q,                  xi = t,            wj = 1 − t.

The function G is continuous. Partition I at all points of the forms
                             X                    X
(6.8.2)                            pi ,     1−           qj
                             i∈A                  j∈B
that lie in I. On each resulting closed interval G is concave. For fixed t, it is
enough to enumerate the vertices of the left capped simplex and fill the right
coordinates in increasing order of their costs. Rational data therefore give
exact rational values.
Proof. For fixed w, the objective is affine in x, so some minimizing x has
at most one coordinate strictly between its bounds. With this x fixed, the
same assertion holds for w. Thus some joint minimum is attained at vertices
of both capped simplices.
   A left vertex family has a set A of coordinates at their caps, one possible
remaining coordinate i ∈ / A, and
                                            X
                                xi = t −          pk .
                                            k∈A
Its interval of validity has endpoints that are subset sums of p. The analogous
right family has wj = 1 − t − k∈B qk , and its endpoints are complements of
                                 P

subset sums of q. All-bound vertices are included at their fixed masses. This
also covers zero caps and one-point feasible intervals.
   On an open interval between consecutive points in (6.8.2), the available
vertex families are fixed. For one pair of families the objective is a quadratic
in t with leading coefficient −Cij ≤ 0. It is therefore concave. The minimum
of finitely many concave functions is concave: apply the defining concavity
inequality to every function and then take their minimum. This proves the
assertion on the open intervals.
   To check continuity at an endpoint t0 , a convergent subsequence of mini-
mizers shows that any limiting value is at least G(t0 ), by compactness and
continuity of the objective. In the other direction, choose a minimizer at
t0 and any feasible pair at a mass on the specified side of t0 . Convex inter-
polation between the two pairs is feasible at every intermediate mass, and
its objective converges to G(t0 ). This gives the reverse limiting inequality.
Concavity therefore extends to the closed intervals.
P
   Finally, for a fixed left vector x, the cost of the right coordinate j is
   i Cij xi . Filling cheaper coordinates first minimizes this linear expression
under their caps and the prescribed total mass. An exchange from a more
expensive occupied coordinate to a cheaper unfilled one proves the assertion.
All vertices, costs, and partial fills are rational for rational data.         □
   In particular, to minimize G(t) on a closed subinterval of I, it is enough
to check its endpoints and the points in (6.8.2). More generally, the same is
true of G(t) − L(t) after adding every breakpoint of a continuous piecewise
linear function L. On each remaining interval L is affine, so G − L is concave.
This use of concavity does not assert that G is concave across the subset
masses.

completion edges are not counted as actual conflicts.
   For the finite test, apply Lemma 6.8.1 with t = 1 − v. The second term
of Ψ is continuous and piecewise linear. Its only additional breakpoints are
1/H and (6.8.5). Between all these points Ψ is concave, so its minimum
occurs at an endpoint. When D = 1 the cost function is constant, and the
redundant point 1/H is harmless. A one-point interval is checked directly.
All evaluations are rational.                                             □
   The distinction between the transported and original positions in this
proof is useful. Artificial full-type mass need not represent a set of actual
full-tag positions. We use transport only to lower the compatible mass; the
forest deletion bound is applied to the original weighted set, whose total
mass is unchanged.

merely from inclusion of neighbor types in a tag.

                                            168
   For completeness, an independent evaluation can use the fact that C(Q, J)
is either zero or one. Fix a left vertex family with variable mass t. The
marginal costs of the right segments have slopes zero or one in t. Within each
slope class their order is fixed. Enumerate the two prefixes filled completely
and the possible last partial segment. Requiring the full segments to be no
more expensive than the last, and the unused segments to be no cheaper,
gives linear inequalities in t. Its remaining length is 1 − t − d, where d is
the total full-segment length. The capacity inequalities give another closed
interval. On its intersection with the family domain the objective is concave,
so its two endpoints suffice. Include all-bound left choices and the case in
which every right segment is full. This enumerates the same feasible optima
without locating all pairwise crossings of marginal costs.

Theorem 6.8.4 (The interval from 1360 through 1521). Every tree of order
n with 1360 ≤ n ≤ 1521 has a prime labeling.
Proof. Fix such an integer n and a tree T of order n. Proposition 6.6.7
supplies the uniform preparation and a row covering its residual degree. If
the residual forest is edgeless, the immediate case in Theorem 6.6.6 completes
the labeling. Otherwise choose the row for its positive degree D. The
necessary degree exclusions cannot remove this actual order–degree pair, by
Lemma 6.6.2.
   Fix the type assignment supplied by that row. Proposition 6.7.4 provides
the exception bounds, strict local targets, and their joint local failure estimate.
Proposition 6.8.3 supplies the Hall conditions and makes the full failure sum
strictly less than one. Theorem 6.6.6 therefore gives a permutation for which
the residual matching exists, and restores a prime labeling of T .
   The preparation and probability bounds hold separately for every input
tree at every integer order in its interval. The 32 adjacent intervals cover
all of [1360, 1521]. Thus the conclusion holds for every T and n in the
statement.                                                                      □
Corollary 6.8.5 (The established initial interval). Every tree of order at
most 1521 has a prime labeling.
Proof. Theorem 5.8.4 treats 1 ≤ n ≤ 1359. Theorem 6.8.4 treats the next
integer interval, whose first order is 1360 = 1359 + 1.              □
   The argument for subsequent orders must provide its own construction
bounds and accepted range data, followed by the estimates for an unbounded
interval. The finite certificate just proved establishes no assertion about
those remaining orders.

6.10. Two-sided type transport through order 2209
   Both remaining Hall ranges will use exponential estimates. To sharpen
their coordinate bounds, we compare the odd-type distribution with a ref-
erence law and move excess mass to the full type. We apply the same
operation to the position tags. Both selected masses are preserved, and their
compatibility can only decrease.

                    τ < |U |/N ≤ γ          or   γ < |U |/N ≤ 1 − υ.

The position coordinates satisfy (6.10.2). In range 2 the empty label coordi-

universal labels are outside U . The last assertion of Lemma 4.5.3 therefore
gives (6.10.4). Thus the transported pair lies in the appropriate capped
domain.
   If that domain is infeasible, we already have a contradiction. Otherwise
its compatible mass is at least βbk . Every compatible pair in the original
weighted rectangle belongs to U × V and is absent from Γ. Its weight is at
most one. Hence at least N 2 βbk relevant pairs were deleted. In range 2 these
pairs have nonempty label types; transport has introduced no empty label
mass. This contradicts the assumed deletion bound. There is no deficient
set, and Hall’s criterion gives a perfect matching.                          □
   The two transports are comparisons between measures, not changes to the
labeling or to its random permutations. In particular, the deletion variables
still count the original compatible pairs. If the local degree failures and these
two deletion events have total probability less than one, the union bound
gives a labeling to which Proposition 6.10.1 applies.

Proof. The accompanying finite checker recomputes the complete construc-
tion, local and Hall data. We specify how these checks establish each

   Preparation and construction. At all 456 integer orders, direct prime
counts verify the core supply, special-label counts and universal odd-label
supply. They also give the degree bound D∗ . The interval endpoints are
adjacent and every degree list is complete. Each actual order–degree pair is
tested against (6.6.5) before using the paid donor stock.
   The rows use 70 original-forest constructions with cuts confined to B, 399
with donor exchanges, and 153 connected height constructions. For each
row the checker recomputes the separator or height budget, degree variation,
transfer, tag error and side bounds of Proposition 6.6.4. It checks completion
capacity wherever a completing tree is used. All supplied upper and lower
bounds have the required directions, and all endpoint quota lower bounds
are positive.
   Reference laws and local degrees. The exact usable quota laws give the
initial degree bounds. Equations (6.10.6) and (6.10.7) give the two transport
errors, including every core size in their finite maxima. The comparison
Ω < λ0 holds in every row. An independent computation obtains the types
by gcd tests, forms the odd laws after removing k empty-type labels, and
sums the positive coordinate differences. It gives the same maxima as one
half of the sum of absolute differences.
   There are 531 rows using the complete full-type exception pool and 91
using the protected pool of (6.7.3). The latter include 76 donor rows and 15
connected rows. Each omitted full-type label passes the strict deterministic
degree test. In every row the exception fraction is less than τ , all local
margins are positive, and γ + υ ≤ 1.
   The maximal-support cover has 256,870 event occurrences and covers
616,062 endpoint label occurrences across the rows. For every event, the
checker reconstructs its class frequencies, sample bounds, integer threshold
and selected probability estimates from Theorem 6.7.3. Exactly 224,210
events have a deterministic zero bound. Summing over the maximal events
gives ploc , retaining the multiplicity of different events with equal numerical
bounds. The independent arithmetic calculation also checks each profile
against the actual usable labels at every order of its interval.
   The two Hall events. The checker forms the transported caps in (6.10.2)–

tion of the vertices in Proposition 4.5.5 gives the same 1,244 positive minima.
This comparison includes the zero empty coordinate in the middle range and
the reduced empty coordinate in the high range.
   For each range the endpoint cost histograms count precisely its allowed
odd-type family. They are independently reconstructed by counting odd
multiples of each larger prime. The directed moment calculation then applies
the bounds of Proposition 6.5.5, including the special-label subtractions and
the actual quota-weighted average. There are two exponential evaluations in

every row. The local estimates and these two bounds concern one and the
same random permutation of the even classes.

tributions are about 0.007607, 0.002053 and 0.917355, respectively. The

6.12. Retained positions and the orders through 34999
   From order 3500 through 34999 we use the three constructions of Propo-

is applied. Seven certificates cover this range. They use the same criterion
and differ only in their interval covers, prime blocks, and parameters. We
state the criterion once (Theorem 6.12.1) and then record the certificates
(Proposition 6.12.2).

6.12.2. The criterion.
Theorem 6.12.1 (A criterion with retained positions). Let [ℓ, u] be an
interval of orders satisfying (6.6.2), and put D∗ = ⌈1/(2W )⌉. Suppose that
for every integer 1 ≤ D ≤ D∗ a row is specified: one of the three constructions
of Proposition 6.6.4 with its rational parameters, a partition of the odd primes
up to a cutoff into two prime blocks, and a rational parameter τ0 . Define the
target by (6.12.1), bb by (6.12.2), and ploc and pglob as above. Suppose that
every row satisfies, for all orders n ∈ [ℓ, u] permitted by (6.6.5):
    (1) the preparation and construction conditions of Section 6.6;
    (2) positive local degree margins at the target (6.12.1);
    (3) τ + υ ≤ 1 and bb > 0;
    (4)
(6.12.3)                         ploc + pglob < 1.
Then every tree of every order in [ℓ, u] has a prime labeling.
Proof. Let T have order n ∈ [ℓ, u] and prepare it by Proposition 6.3.3. If
the residual forest is edgeless, assign its labels arbitrarily and finish with the
restoration and repair of that proposition. Otherwise Lemma 6.6.2 places
the residual even-side degree D in {1, . . . , D∗ }, and n satisfies the feasibility
condition (6.6.5) of the row for D.
   Fix the row’s type assignment and retained tags before taking the class
permutations. The target (6.12.1) gives ε < τ = η = γ, and τ + υ ≤ 1 by the
third condition. Hence Proposition 6.7.2 applies to the full-type pool E, and
|E ∩ Y | ≤ εN . By the first three conditions and Theorem 6.7.3, some local
degree condition fails with probability at most ploc . By the construction of
(6.11.4), the global conflict cost is at least n2bb with probability at most pglob .
By (6.12.3), some choice of the class permutations satisfies every local degree
condition and makes the global conflict cost less than n2bb.
   For this choice we verify the hypotheses of Lemma 6.11.1. The avoided
local events give its degree conditions (Proposition 6.7.2). Proposition 6.6.1
supplies at least υN universal labels of empty type, since the cutoff satisfies
                                        187
3z < ℓ. Lemma 6.10.2 gives the variation bounds, and bb > 0 is the positivity
of the rectangle minimum. By Lemma 4.3.7, the number of deleted pairs
is at most the global conflict cost, which is less than n2bb = (nρ)2 βb ≤ N 2 β,
                                                                              b
with βb as in (6.11.1), because N ≥ nρ. The lemma therefore supplies a
perfect matching between the remaining odd labels and positions. Assign
the matched labels, restore the special labels, and apply the repair of Propo-
sition 6.3.3. The resulting labeling of T is prime.                           □

   Cover and preparation. The intervals of each cover are adjacent, and

   Constructions. For each row, the checker recomputes the cut or height
budget, rounding variation, transfer, changed-tag bound, and residual side
bounds of Proposition 6.6.4 on the whole interval and checks every supplied
bound against its defining expression. The donor boundary uses the complete
sequence of paid stocks at each feasible order, so the test does not assume
that the chosen donors have degree at most one. In a connected row, the
degree variation is measured in the completing tree, and the completion
capacity and added-edge credit are checked for every permitted core size.
   Types and targets. Exact usable quotas give the compatible fractions for
the local degree estimates. The two finite type errors of Lemma 6.10.2 are
maxima over every order of the interval and every permitted number of
removed core labels. The endpoint full-type count gives ε, and the checker
verifies ε < τ = η = γ and τ + υ ≤ 1. Thus only the last Hall range remains,
while the full-type labels keep their weak local degree condition. When
(6.12.1) raises the base target, the local bounds are evaluated at the raised
value.
   Local sum. The endpoint event cover is formed anew on each interval, and
Lemma 6.7.1 covers every smaller order: an actual label has the same type as
its assigned endpoint event and a support contained in that event’s support.
The representing endpoint label need not itself occur at the actual order. For
each maximal-support event, the checker reconstructs its arithmetic profile.
For each type threshold θ, the checker computes minℓ≤n≤u ⌈nθ⌉/n over all
integer orders. The probability bounds are evaluated as in Sections 6.5 and 6.7.
Distinct events with the same numerical bound keep their multiplicity in the
local sum, and all local margins are positive.
   Global bound. The checker reconstructs the endpoint cost histograms and
computes both bounds in (6.11.4). The directed exponential calculation uses
the same cost family and the actual quota-weighted average; both estimates
retain the donor term or the completion credit. The transported rectangle
minimum is positive in every row. The smaller of the two global bounds is
                                      189
added once to the local sum, and all joint comparisons (6.12.3) are strict.
All comparisons use exact rational arithmetic.
    Independent checks. Each checker also runs reference calculations that test
its implementation; the proposition does not depend on them. For every cer-
tificate, they compare each arithmetic profile with the actual usable labels at
every order of the interval, recompute the endpoint cost histograms by direct
counts of compatible odd multiples, and obtain every rectangle minimum
by a separate enumeration of the rectangle vertices, using Proposition 4.5.5.
From order 6772 on, the population count is organized as follows.   Q
                                                                        For a tail-
prime support P , list the half-labels j of each type with gcd j, p∈P p > 1;
restricting to j ≤ ⌊n/2⌋ gives the complete population at order n. Powers
of two contribute no such half-label, and a removed odd prime contributes
precisely when it belongs to P . Subtracting these contributions gives the
usable population. From order 20000 on, independent factorization also
reconstructs the endpoint covers with their maximal supports and assigned
label sets. For the certificates through order 12499, independent gcd-based
counts reproduce the two type errors, the odd reference law, and the usable
even quota laws, and the direct cost counts, together with the removed-label
counts, give independent values for the first-moment data of Proposition 6.4.2.
The supplement lists these checks for each certificate.
   The seven certificates also involve the following particular features.
     (1) On 3500 ≤ n ≤ 5219, the blocks partition the odd primes through
         one of 13, 17, 19 into two sets, giving four types. The first-moment
         bound is smaller in 13 rows and the exponential bound in the other
         833. In the largest joint sum, the local term is about 1.30731 · 10−11
         and the global term about 0.9490722798334362.
     (2) On 5220 ≤ n ≤ 6771, all rows have two prime blocks and hence four
         types. Universality of the special odd labels is checked by listing
         their possible even multiples: for a prime p > n/3 the only possible
         one in [n] is 2p, and when it is present it belongs to the removed
         special family; the label 1 is universal directly.
     (3) On 6772 ≤ n ≤ 12499, two prime blocks suffice throughout. In the
         degree-13 row on [7272, 7521] the target is raised by (6.12.1).
     (4) On 12500 ≤ n ≤ 24999, degree 4 uses the blocks {3, 13} and {5, 7, 11}
         with cutoff 13; the other degrees use {3, 13, 19} and {5, 7, 11, 17} with
         cutoff 19. On 25000 ≤ n ≤ 34999 the latter blocks are used for every
         degree. The base target is raised in no row on 12500 ≤ n ≤ 14999,
         in 4 rows on 15000 ≤ n ≤ 19999, in 16 rows on 20000 ≤ n ≤ 24999,
         and in 27 rows on 25000 ≤ n ≤ 34999.
     (5) The core bound b(n) = ⌊log2 (n−2)⌋+2 changes inside some intervals,
         for example b(16385) = 15 and b(16386) = 16, and b(32769) = 16
         and b(32770) = 17. Taking b∗ = b(u) accounts for all values in the
         core supply and the residual side bounds. The label 32768 enters


                                        190
        the special even family at order 32768; the removed label set is
        reconstructed at every order.
    (6) At order 20677 = 23 · 29 · 31 the first support of size three above
        cutoff 19 appears, and the support {23, 29, 37} also occurs by order
        24999. These supports are included before maximality is tested, and
        no bound on the cardinality of a support is imposed.
    (7) On every interval from 12500 on, the exponential global bound is the
        smaller one. All joint sums are less than 1/1000 on 12500 ≤ n ≤ 19999
        and less than 1/100 on 20000 ≤ n ≤ 34999.
On 5220 ≤ n ≤ 12499, too, the exponential global bound is the smaller one
in every row. This proves the proposition.                                 □
6.12.4. The orders through 34999.
Theorem 6.12.3 (The initial interval through 34999). Every tree of order
at most 34999 has a prime labeling.
Proof. Theorem 6.11.3 covers the orders at most 3499. The seven covers
of Table 24 partition the orders from 3500 = 3499 + 1 through 34999 into
adjacent intervals: the consecutive ranges end at 5219, 6771, 12499, 14999,
19999, and 24999, and each next range begins at the following integer. By
Proposition 6.12.2, every interval satisfies the hypotheses of Theorem 6.12.1.
That theorem therefore gives a prime labeling for every tree of every order
from 3500 through 34999.                                                   □
   The next finite range begins at 35000. It requires the Hall criterion with a
label-side reserve of Section 8.1 and is treated in Section 8.2. First, Section 7
develops the assignment needed for the separate unbounded-range argument.

          7. Uniform estimates and the unbounded range
   This part proves Theorem 7.7.2: every tree of order n with log n ≥ 256 has
a prime labeling. Sections 7.1–7.3 give an assignment with rare arithmetic
types (Theorem 7.1.4), a Hall estimate with a reserve of universal positions
(Theorem 7.2.2), and a bound on the conflicts caused by prime divisors above
the cutoff (Theorem 7.3.2); several logarithmic bridges of Section 9 also use
them. Sections 7.4–7.7 choose the parameters as functions of log n and verify
all conditions analytically.

7.1. Assignments with rare arithmetic types
   For a growing set of primes, keeping a separate rounding coordinate for
every type is expensive. We shall keep the types with small support and
combine the others during rounding. The exact types of the even labels are
still recorded. On the opposite side, a position whose tag becomes too large
is marked † and later receives a universal label. The distinction matters:
combining label classes is a temporary step in meeting quotas, whereas
marking a position creates an obligation to assign it a compatible label.
                                       191
   Throughout this section, G = (A, B) is a bipartite forest, N = |A|+|B| ≥ 1,
and D ≥ max{1, ∆G (A)}. For two      measures of the same total mass on a
                                1P
finite set, we use ∥ξ − ζ∥TV = 2 x |ξ(x) − ζ(x)|. Thus errors in counting
measures are measured in numbers of positions, without normalization by
|B|.
7.1.1. Component rounding with prescribed quotas. We first cut the forest
into small components. Unlike the one-sided cuts used earlier, this cut may
meet either part of the forest.
Lemma 7.1.1. For every integer t ≥ 1, a forest on N vertices has a set C
with
                                       N
                                          
                              |C| ≤
                                      t+1
such that every component after deleting C has at most t vertices.
Proof. Root each component and process its vertices from the leaves upwards.
At a vertex v, join v to the residual sets returned by its children. If their union
has more than t vertices, put v in C and return the empty set; otherwise
return that union. Every returned nonempty set is connected and has at
most t vertices.
   When a vertex is put in C, charge it together with the residual sets just
received. These charged sets are disjoint and each contains at least t + 1
vertices. This proves the bound on |C|. Each component of the remaining
forest is a residual set detached from a deleted parent, or a residual set at
an original root. Its size is at most t.                                          □
  Let A1 , . . . , Aq and B1 , . . . , Bb partition A and B into nonempty classes.

Proof. PCombine the classes AI with |I| > r into one class, prescribing its total

corrections require at most Ri additional vertices in total. Let W be the final
selected set, and retain the original union V∗ of selected components. Use

and place its other labels on the remaining vertices. This is a bijection and
preserves the type at every vertex with respect to the first i − 1 primes.
Add pi to every ordinary tag whose position lies in T , marking it † if its
size exceeds r. This gives the required containment of actual neighboring
divisors.
   For an ordinary old class of size sJ , let uJ of its positions receive the new
prime. The two daughter counts differ from (1 − ρi )sJ , ρi sJ by opposite
errors. Their total-variation contribution is exactly |uJ − ρi sJ |. Combining
the overflow classes cannot increase this error. The old † class has no error
because it is left fixed by both transitions. The preceding rounding bound
proves (7.1.5). There are at most the displayed number of small classes on
each side, and at most one additional class on each side. This proves the
bound on di .                                                                   □

Theorem 7.1.4 (Assignment with rare tags). Starting with empty tags, the
successive refinements produce a placement of the given even labels and a tag

Proof. The independent transition at step i leaves † fixed, and either adds pi
to an ordinary tag or leaves it unchanged. It sends the compressed product
law on the first i − 1 primes to that on the first i primes: a set already larger
than r cannot become small when a new coordinate is added. Figure 12
shows this transition.
   Every Markov transition contracts total variation. Indeed, if its matrix is
K, then

error is zero, so induction gives the first inequality in (7.1.6). Apply it to the
event {†} to obtain the second. Tag containment and exact quotas follow at
every step from the same proposition. A marked position is counted once in
C, even if it would overflow at several later steps.                            □

7.1.5. Assigning labels to marked positions. The bound on C must be paid

                                         197
types larger than r has been moved to the empty type. Contraction and the
triangle inequality give error at most E + sβ for the s positions. Adding h

Likewise the original label counting measure differs from ℓµ by at most
ℓa. Deleting C empty-type labels adds at most C to this error relative to
(ℓ − C)µ. This proves (7.1.14). Both replacements of C by c are valid because
(x + C)/(ℓ − C) increases with C for x ≥ 0. Finally, the fixed pairs use
distinct labels and positions and are disjoint from the remaining matching.
Their union is the required extension.                                     □
   In the residual problem of Proposition 3.4.3, the adjoined positions are
the artificial universal vertices used in Corollary 3.4.4. Every unused label
in O∗ is coprime to every label in E0 : its only possible exceptional even
partner has already been removed. If all primes in P are at most z < n/3,
these universal labels also have empty P -type. A completed matching
therefore gives the residual injection; the restoration and repair remain those
of Proposition 3.4.3.
   The counting and assignment arguments in this section hold for every
finite prime set. To use them for a growing cutoff, it remains to bound the
two errors in (7.1.14) and (7.1.15), and to control deletions caused by larger
primes. The direct Hall estimate with universal reserves will provide the
corresponding matching criterion in Section 7.2.

7.2. A direct Hall estimate with universal reserves
   The preceding construction leaves an ordinary matching problem after its
marked positions have been assigned universal labels. We now give a criterion
for that matching. It uses the two full type laws, rather than conditioning
either law on a rare class. This distinction permits a total variation error
proportional to the mass of the empty position type.

  The next result keeps track of the edges needed to violate Hall’s condition.

Theorem 7.2.2 (Direct Hall criterion with a reserve). Let a bipartite graph
have ℓ vertices on each side, with empirical type laws µ   b, νb satisfying
                                                       b

and

Proof. Suppose Hall’s condition fails. There is a nonempty set Q on one
side and a nonempty set R on the other such that |Q| + |R| > ℓ and all
compatible edges between Q and R were deleted. One may take R to be
the complement of the remaining neighborhood of Q. Neither set contains a
universal vertex, for such a vertex would retain all of its edges. In particular
                             h < |Q|, |R| ≤ ℓ − h.
Put u = |Q|/ℓ, v = |R|/ℓ, x = min{u, v}, and r = h/ℓ. Thus u + v > 1,
r < x ≤ 1 − r, and r ≤ b/100. We count the compatible edges in Q × R
before deletion.
   First suppose x < b/4. A vertex in the smaller set originally has at
least (b − ϵ)ℓ ≥ (24/25)bℓ compatible neighbors, by Lemma 7.2.1. Since



                                       200

contains strictly more than

  We state explicitly how this criterion enters the residual labeling problem.
The number M below is a bound on pairs, rather than on bad labels or
positions; it is estimated from larger prime divisors in Section 7.3.
Proposition 7.2.3 (Conditional residual completion). Use the residual
construction of Proposition 3.4.3 and the assignment of Section 7.1. Let
s = |Z0 | and ℓ = s + h = |O0 |. Suppose the hypotheses of Lemma 7.1.7 hold
with its parameters α, E, β, c, H, where α denotes the label-law error called a
in that lemma. Suppose the model laws satisfy (7.2.1). Assume
(7.2.9)
  max{ℓα, E + h + ℓβ} + c         b           b(ℓ − c)           19
                             ≤ ,        h≤             ,    M ≤ b(ℓ − c)h.
             ℓ−c                 25             100              20
Here M bounds the number of pairs in the remaining balanced square that are
compatible for primes in P but incompatible for a prime outside P . Suppose
also that the h artificial positions and at least h of the remaining labels are
genuinely universal for the residual problem. Then the original tree has a
prime labeling.
Proof. By Lemma 7.1.7, the marked positions use distinct universal labels
and C ≤ c. Set ℓ′ = ℓ − C. The two empirical laws of the remaining square
are within the two bounds (7.1.14) and (7.1.15) of their respective model

                                      201
laws. Equation (7.2.9) makes each bound at most b/25. It also implies
h ≤ bℓ′ /100 and M ≤ 19bℓ′ h/20, since ℓ′ ≥ ℓ − c.
   An ordinary tag contains every prime of P that divides a neighboring
even label. Consequently disjointness of a label type and a tag guarantees
coprimality at those primes. Delete the at most M pairs that fail because
of a prime outside P . The stated universal pairs survive this deletion.
Theorem 7.2.2 supplies a perfect matching of the remaining square. Add back
the fixed marked pairs and discard the h pairs at artificial positions. What
remains is an injection from Z0 to O0 satisfying (3.4.6). Proposition 3.4.3
restores the removed labels and repairs the allowed exceptional pairs.    □
   In applications the even labels may be permuted within each exact P -type
before M is bounded. Such a permutation preserves every exact label quota
and every already constructed P -tag: each neighbor’s type remains the same.
It changes only which pairs share a prime outside P . The estimate for those
pairs is supplied by Theorem 7.3.2.

7.3. The cost of larger prime divisors

be removed from the type-compatible graph. We bound these removals by
choosing the even labels within their exact types. The choice leaves every
tag from Section 7.1 unchanged.

7.5. The residual forest at large orders
  We now choose the sets in Proposition 3.4.3 for every order in the analytic
range. The two sides of the residual forest need nearly half the original
vertices, while the special labels must pay for a reserve and reduce the
remaining degrees. The stronger prime-counting estimates (7.4.3) and (7.4.4)
supply the needed lower count of special even labels.
  Throughout this section let n be an integer with L = log n ≥ 256, and put
m = ⌊n/2⌋ and Mo = ⌈n/2⌉. We shall use the elementary size bound
(7.5.1)                          n > 106 L2 .

total-variation budgets stay within this reserve.

7.7. The unbounded range
   The type errors and the universal reserve have been controlled. It remains
to compare the number of edges removed for larger prime divisors with the
Hall budget. We use the sorting within exact types from Theorem 7.3.2; it
leaves every tag and every estimate of Section 7.6 unchanged.

Theorem 7.7.2 (Prime labelings in the unbounded range). Every tree of
integer order n with log n ≥ 256 has a prime labeling.
Proof. Fix such a tree. Proposition 7.5.2 gives the residual forest, the h
artificial universal positions, and H = 2h available odd labels genuinely
coprime to every residual even label. Apply the rare-type assignment to this
forest with the parameters (7.6.1) and (7.1.7). Its exact even-label quotas
are preserved, and every ordinary tag contains the types of all its neighbors.
   Lemma 7.6.3 verifies the model-law conditions (7.2.1). Proposition 7.6.4
pays for every marked position with a distinct universal label, leaves at least
h universal labels, and verifies both type errors and the reserve inequality
in (7.2.9). Sort the remaining even labels within their exact types as in
Lemma 7.7.1. This does not change their types or any ordinary tag, and its
deletion bound supplies the final inequality in (7.2.9).
   Proposition 7.2.3 now gives a matching on the residual problem, restores
the special labels and the separator, and repairs every possible pair p, 2p.
The resulting bijection labels every edge of the original tree by coprime
integers.                                                                    □

             8. Integer bridges through order 1982759
   This part covers the orders from 35000 through 1982759. Section 8.1
proves a Hall criterion with a reserve on the label side (Theorem 8.1.2).
Sections 8.2, 8.4, 8.6, and 8.7 give interval certificates for consecutive ranges
of orders, and Sections 8.3 and 8.5 prove the construction lemmas they need.
The resulting cumulative result is Theorem 8.7.2.




                                      214
8.1. An asymmetric reserve criterion
   The direct criterion of Section 7.2 uses universal vertices on both sides.
In the finite continuation, the special odd labels give a larger reserve on the
label side, while the positions need not contain a comparable reserve. The
following criterion keeps the two sides distinct. Its slot term uses the whole
distribution of compatibility degrees, rather than only their minimum.
   Use the block product law µ, and the depleted law νξ of (4.2.2). Fix
0 ≤ ξ ≤ ω <Q  γ, where γ is defined in (4.2.1). Put b = bω and θ = θω . Recall
that c(J) = j∈J (1 − qj ) is the µ-mass compatible with the slot type J; in
particular α ≤ c(J) ≤ 1.
   Let X be a set of odd labels and B a set of positions, both of size R > 0.
Their empirical type laws are denoted by µ   b, νb. The graph Γ0 joins a label
to a position when their types are disjoint. For 0 < t ≤ 1 define the slot
shortfall
                                    X                
(8.1.1)                  AB (t) =         t − c(J(v)) + .
                                    v∈B
The subscript emphasizes that the sum is over actual positions. It may be
bounded without enumerating their individual tags.
Lemma 8.1.1 (Clipped slot shortfall). If ∥νb − νξ ∥TV ≤ V , then
              AB (t)    1 X
(8.1.2)              ≤       µ(J)(t − c(J))+ + V (t − α)+ .
               R       1−ω J
The sum on the right is a finite rational expression when the block probabilities
and t are rational.
   There is also an unnormalized form. Suppose B is obtained by deleting
positions from a set B + of at most on positions. If the counting measure Ξ+
of its tags satisfies ∥Ξ+ − |B + |νξ ∥TV ≤ en, then
               AB (t)    o X
(8.1.3)               ≤       µ(J)(t − c(J))+ + e(t − α)+ .
                n       1−ω J
Proof. Put f (J) = (t − c(J))+ . Since c(∅) = 1 and c(J) ≥ α, we have
f (∅) = 0 and 0 ≤ f (J) ≤ (t − α)+ . Total variation changes the expectation
of a function in this interval by at most V (t − α)+ . On the other hand, the
definition of νξ gives
                                     Eµ f     Eµ f
                            Eνξ f =       ≤        .
                                    1−ξ      1−ω
As AB (t)/R = Eνbf , this proves the claim. For the second assertion, removing
positions cannot increase the sum of the nonnegative f (J(v)). Apply the
same total-variation estimate to the two counting measures on B + , divide
by n, and use |B + |/n ≤ o.                                                 □
   The next theorem is deterministic. The numerical certificate will apply
it to one permutation for which the local degrees and the total number of
larger-prime deletions both meet their bounds.
                                        215
Theorem 8.1.2 (Hall criterion with a label-side reserve). Suppose
                                                                1

are adjacent to every position in Γ. Choose a real g0 with 0 < g0 ≤ g and
g0 ≤ τ R, and let t satisfy t − V > τ . Define
                                        
(8.1.4)         B1 = g0 (t − V )R − g0 − RAB (t),
(8.1.5)         B2 = R2 τ (t − V − τ ) − RAB (t),
               Bsp = R2 b (1 − θ)τ (1 − τ ) − V + (1 − θ)V 2 ,
                                                            
(8.1.6)
(8.1.7)        Bpr = R2 W (V, τ ),

(8.1.8)              M < min{B1 , B2 , max(Bsp , Bpr )},
then Γ has a perfect matching.
Proof. Suppose Hall’s condition fails. There are nonempty sets U ⊆ X
and V0 ⊆ B with |U | + |V0 | > R and no edge of Γ between them; take
V0 = B \ NΓ (U ). Write u = |U | and v = |V0 |. No universal label lies in U ,
so u ≤ R − g and v > g ≥ g0 .
   If u < τ R, the degree of any label in U is at most |NΓ (U )| < u < τ R,
contrary to the local-degree hypothesis. Hence u ≥ τ R.
   Suppose next that v < τ R. A position of type J has at least R(c(J) − V )
compatible labels in Γ0 : the type event has µ-mass c(J), and total variation
changes its empirical mass by at most V . At most R − u of those labels lie
outside U . Since u > R − v, the number of compatible labels in U is strictly
greater than R(c(J) − V ) − v. Using c(J) ≥ t − (t − c(J))+ and summing
over V0 , the number of compatible pairs in U × V0 is strictly greater than
                                           
                         v (t − V )R − v − RAB (t).
The quadratic is concave in v. On g0 ≤ v ≤ τ R its minimum occurs at an
endpoint, where the displayed values are B1 and B2 . Every compatible pair
in this rectangle was deleted, contradicting (8.1.8).
   It remains that u, v ≥ τ R. Reduce the two selected type submeasures
fractionally until their masses sum to one. Truncate them at µ, νξ , losing at
most V from each side, and then reduce their masses by the same amounts.
This leaves model submeasures of masses x, y ≥ τ − V with x + y = 1 − 2V .
Lemma 4.2.3 pulls the second submeasure back to νω without increasing
its compatible pair mass. Proposition 4.2.4 therefore gives at least Bpr
compatible pairs in the original rectangle.
   There is also the coupling estimate. By Corollary 4.2.2, the rectangle
contains at least                 q                  
                         2
                       R b xy − θ xy(1 − x)(1 − y)
                                     216
compatible pairs. Since x + y = 1 − 2V ,
          q
              xy(1 − x)(1 − y) ≤ xy + V,        xy ≥ τ (1 − τ ) − V + V 2 .
The first inequality follows from (1 − x)(1 − y) = xy + 2V ; the second is
the minimum of a concave quadratic on the allowed mass interval. These
inequalities yield Bsp when θ ≤ 1. If θ > 1, the bracket in Bsp is negative,
since τ (1 − τ ) > 0, so Bsp ≤ 0 and the bound holds trivially. Thus the
original rectangle has at least max(Bsp , Bpr ) compatible pairs. All were
deleted, again contradicting (8.1.8). Hall’s theorem completes the proof. □
Corollary 8.1.3 (One permutation for the local and global bounds). Sup-
pose the types, tags, and universal labels are fixed before the even labels
are independently permuted within their classes. Assume the type-law and
universal-reserve hypotheses of Theorem 8.1.2 hold for every such permuta-
tion, while the probability that some required local-degree estimate fails is at
most δ and the expected number of larger-prime deletions is at most m0 . Let
λ > 1 satisfy δ + 1/λ < 1. If
                       λm0 < min{B1 , B2 , max(Bsp , Bpr )},
then some choice of the class permutations has a perfect matching.
Proof. Markov’s inequality gives Pr{M ≥ λm0 } ≤ 1/λ when m0 > 0; if
m0 = 0, then M = 0 almost surely. The union bound makes the local
conditions and the deletion bound simultaneous with positive probability.
Apply Theorem 8.1.2 to such a permutation.                            □

Proposition 8.2.1 (The integer bridge certificate). The thirty adjacent
intervals of Table 26 cover every integer order from 35000 through 49999.
For every possible positive residual degree, their specified row satisfies the
preparation, quota, separator, donor or connected-completion, and prime-
supply conditions. It also satisfies the following matching conditions: the
local degree after deletion exceeds τ R; the unused universal stock is positive
and at least r− n; both type laws are within V of their stated models; and the
charged deletion mass is strictly below n2 mini Bi .
Proof. The electronic certificate consists of the interval parameter table,
the exact evaluator, its local modules and fixed arithmetic data, and the
output report. The command and file inventory are given in the supplement
under integer_bridge_35000_49999. The bundled checker recomputes
prime counts and complete residue periods, then reconstructs every row with
rational arithmetic. It does not read the displayed decimal approximations
to decide a condition.
   For an interval [a, b], the checker computes the exact minimum special-
even supply over every integer n ∈ [a, b]. The degree bound of Lemma 6.6.2
determines the complete list 1, . . . , D∗ . It rejects any missing degree, any gap
between successive order intervals, and a row whose selected construction
fails its capacity or reserve inequalities. The original-forest rows use the
degree-transfer and cancellation bounds of Sections 6.3 and 6.4; connected
rows pay for the completed edges and the full tag error. Every row has no
rare group.
   The evaluator uses the exact finite class populations and local union
counts of Section 6.4. It computes the two empirical variation bounds and
the clipped shortfall of Lemma 8.1.1; the profile minimum is computed at
                                       218
the rational breakpoints of Lemma 4.2.5. The local expectation uses the
class degree sums, including the signed empty-class transfer. Corollary 4.4.2
gives a total local failure probability at most 1/10000. The global mean is
bounded before applying Markov’s inequality with factor 1001/1000. In each
row the exact local-degree and the three Hall-budget comparisons are strict;
the union of the local and global failure probabilities has measure less than
one.
   The checker passes all 887 rows and 39915 stated comparisons. Of these
rows, 827 use the original forest and 60 a connected completion. Its inde-
pendent outer check verifies the thirty adjacent intervals, the full integer
degree lists, and the strict ratio bounds of Table 26. It also verifies that the
absorbed-position fraction is less than one quarter of the special odd-label
supply in every row. Thus the stated stock and all uses of it are paid for.
These are finite exhaustive checks of the displayed sufficient conditions, not
an enumeration of trees.                                                      □
Theorem 8.2.2 (The initial interval through order 49999). Every tree of
order at most 49999 has a prime labeling.
Proof. Theorem 6.12.3 gives the conclusion through 34999. Let 35000 ≤ n ≤
49999, and prepare an arbitrary tree as in Proposition 6.3.3. If its residual
forest has no edges, that proposition gives the labeling directly. Otherwise
Lemma 6.6.2 selects a degree row in the unique certified interval containing
n.
   Fix the row’s type assignment and tags. By Proposition 8.2.1, the special
odd labels remain universal, and the local estimates and deletion budget
satisfy Theorem 8.1.2. The local union bound and Markov’s inequality meet
the hypothesis of Corollary 8.1.3, since
                                1      1000
                                    +       < 1.
                              10000 1001
Choose a class permutation in the positive-probability event. The resulting
perfect matching assigns all residual odd labels to positions with coprimality
on every residual edge. Proposition 6.3.3 restores the special labels and the
separator and repairs the permitted exceptional pairs. The two intervals
meet at consecutive orders 34999 and 35000, completing the proof.           □

8.3. Paired cuts in the connected construction
   The two-measure assignment of Proposition 4.3.5 cuts only vertices on
the position side of a bipartite tree. For the next finite range, we need a
smaller bound on the number of such cuts. A single cut still has the same
contraction factor; the gain appears after two consecutive cuts.
   For a tree U , write L(U ) = |V (U )| − 1. Fix an integer D ≥ 2 and put
                  D−1                                D−2 D−1
                                                                  
(8.3.1)      ρ=          ,    λD = min ρ2 , max            ,           .
                    D                                  D     2D − 1

                                      219
The second quantity bounds the contraction  √ across a pair of cuts; it does
not assert that either single cut has factor λD .
Lemma 8.3.1 (Two position-side cuts). Let U = (AU , BU ) be a bipartite
tree with ∆U (AU ) ≤ D and L = L(U ) ≥ 1. There is a vertex b ∈ BU such
that every component V of U − b satisfies L(V ) ≤ ρL. In every component
V with L(V ) ≥ 1, a second vertex bV ∈ BU ∩ V can be chosen so that every
component W of V − bV satisfies
(8.3.2)              L(W ) ≤ ρL(V ),         L(W ) ≤ λD L.
Proof. Choose an ordinary centroid c of U : every branch of U − c has at most
(L + 1)/2 vertices. For example, a vertex minimizing the sum of distances
to all vertices has this property, since a move into a larger branch would
decrease that sum.
   If c ∈ BU , take b = c. Each child V has L(V ) ≤ (L − 1)/2 ≤ ρL. Apply
the same ordinary-centroid construction to each nonsingleton V . As the
argument in this paragraph and the next shows, it gives a position-side
cut whose children have L-value at most ρL(V ). This is the first bound
of (8.3.2); the second follows because ρ/2 ≤ λD . Indeed ρ2 ≥ ρ/2 and
(D − 1)/(2D − 1) ≥ ρ/2.
   Suppose c ∈ AU . Let s be the size of a largest branch of U − c. Since
dU (c) ≤ D and the branches have total size L,
                                 L        L+1
                                   ≤s≤           .
                                 D           2
Take b to be the neighbor of c in that branch. The component R containing
c has L(R) = L − s ≤ ρL. Every other child has L(V ) ≤ s − 2 ≤ L/2, so the
same ordinary-centroid cut in each such child satisfies both bounds as above.
   It remains to cut R. The same ordinary-centroid construction gives the
first bound in (8.3.2). For a second choice, look at the branches of R−c. They
number at most D −1, have total size L−s, and each has size at most s. Let t
be their largest size and cut the neighbor of c in that branch. The component
containing c then has L-value at most (D − 2)(L − s)/(D − 1); every other
component has L-value at most s. With x = s/L and a = (D − 2)/(D − 1),
the better of the two cuts therefore gives
               maxW L(W )
                             ≤ min{ρ(1 − x), max(x, a(1 − x))}.
                     L
If a(1 − x) ≥ x, this is at most a(1 − 1/D) = (D − 2)/D. Otherwise it is at
most min{x, ρ(1 − x)} ≤ ρ/(1 + ρ) = (D − 1)/(2D − 1). The ordinary cut
also bounds it by ρ2 . Taking the minimum proves the second inequality of
(8.3.2). Both candidate cuts are in BU , and the selected one retains the first
inequality.                                                                  □
  We turn the two-step estimate into a √   bound suited to fractional quota
pruning. Choose a rational number η with λD ≤ η ≤ ρ, and set k = ρ/η ≥ 1.
Such a choice exists because λD ≤ ρ2 . At the beginning of a pair of cuts give
                                       220
a piece U potential F (U ) = L(U ). After the first cut, let a child V retain its
parent value L = L(U ) and give it potential
                          F (V ) = min{kL(V ), ηL}.
After the second cut, reset each grandchild W to F (W ) = L(W ).
Lemma 8.3.2 (Paired separator budget). Every transition in this alternating
procedure satisfies F (child) ≤ ηF (parent). A nonsingleton piece has 1 ≤
F (U ) ≤ kL(U ).
   Suppose fractional pruning has at most d mixed pieces at any time and
always cuts one of largest potential. If N is the number of vertices in the
initial tree, the set C of cut vertices satisfies

(8.3.4)               ≤     min dQ,                  +        .

Proof. After the first cut the definition gives F (V ) ≤ ηL. After the second,
(8.3.2) gives
    F (W ) = L(W ) ≤ min{ρL(V ), λD L} ≤ η min{kL(V ), ηL} = ηF (V ).
The upper bound on F follows from its definition. In an even phase, a
nonsingleton has F = L ≥ 1. In an odd phase it√has kL(V ) ≥ 1 and ηL ≥ 1:
a nonsingleton child requires L ≥ 2, while η ≥ λD ≥ 1/2 by (8.3.1).
   Put pieces with η −j ≤ F < η −j−1 in bucket j. Children of a cut piece
leave its bucket. When the first piece in a bucket is cut, no higher-potential
bucket remains, because a largest-potential mixed piece was selected. At

none is a descendant of another piece cut in the same     bucket.   Moreover
