  The potential is needed only to count cuts. It does not alter the two mea-
sures, the quota constraints, or the degree error in the connected assignment.
Corollary 8.3.3 (Paired two-measure assignment). Under the hypotheses
of Proposition 4.3.5, its coloring and separator may be chosen with the same
exact quotas and degree bounds, while the separator obeys (8.3.3) with d =
2(g−1) and N = |A|+|B|. If N ≤ n, its normalized size is bounded by (8.3.4).
The position tags may then be recovered with the bound of Lemma 4.3.6. The
same assertion holds after a permitted connected completion of a forest,
                                       221
provided the completion capacity and the added-edge cost are charged as in
Lemma 4.3.1.
Proof. In the proof of Proposition 4.3.5, linear-dependence elimination leaves
at most 2(g − 1) mixed pieces. Split a mixed nonsingleton of largest potential
using Lemma 8.3.1; the split vertex belongs to B, so it has zero mass for
both preserved measures. Each pure piece remains frozen, and each mixed
singleton on the A side is rounded by the same degree-sorted rule; a singleton
on the B side has no quota. Thus the original quota, edge-monochromaticity,
and degree-error proofs apply unchanged. Lemma 8.3.2 gives the improved
cut count. A connected completion adds no vertices and satisfies the same
A-degree hypothesis; the construction is applied to the completed tree, with
its additional edges included in all later cost estimates.                   □

8.4. The profile-block bridge through order 514999
   We now apply the same asymmetric Hall criterion to orders above 49999.
The parameters change in two ways. Small primes are placed in disjoint
blocks, and connected rows may use the paired cut bound of Section 8.3.
Both changes affect the numerical estimates: the blocks determine the actual
compatibility graph on both sides, while a completed forest is charged for its
added edges.
   The local probability estimate also retains its exact exponential coefficient.
The following rational enclosure states the particular calculation used by the
certificate.

type bounds. Thus endpoint estimates are not substituted for an unexamined
interior order.

then tests all three strict Hall budgets. The output records the exact
numerator and denominator of every decisive ratio; displayed decimals have

profiles, rectangle minimizer, transport, and local exponential coefficient. A
separate small-tree check tests 30525 paired cuts and 500 complete connected
assignments. These checks test the implementations; the preceding lemmas
establish the implications for arbitrary trees. The saved next-range report is
read only to verify that its first order is 515000.                          □
Theorem 8.4.3 (The initial interval through order 514999). Every tree of
order at most 514999 has a prime labeling.
Proof. Theorem 8.2.2 handles the orders through 49999. Take 50000 ≤
n ≤ 514999 and an arbitrary tree of order n. Prepare its residual forest
by Proposition 6.3.3; the case with no residual edges is completed there.
                                      226
Otherwise choose the unique certificate interval containing n and the row
for its residual degree. Proposition 8.4.2 supplies the exact quotas, positive
universal reserve, both type-law bounds, the local degree target, and the
strict Hall budgets. The local union bound and the global Markov bound
are simultaneous with positive probability. Corollary 8.1.3 therefore gives a
class permutation for which Theorem 8.1.2 supplies a perfect matching. The
matched odd labels are coprime to every assigned even neighbor, because
disjoint block types exclude all primes at most z and the deleted edges account
for every larger common prime. Proposition 6.3.3 restores the special labels
and the separator. The two proved intervals meet at 49999 + 1 = 50000. □

8.5. Refining a rare group of full types
   The next certificate reduces the separator dimension by treating several
infrequent even-label types as one color during quota rounding. The matching
problem still uses every full prime type. We therefore refine the group after
rounding and absorb all positions adjacent to it. The cost is controlled by
choosing this group as the preferred color in the degree rounding.
   Let G = (A, B) be the residual bipartite forest, or a permitted connected
completion, with M = |A| > 0, N = |B|, e = e(G) and ∆G (A) ≤ D. Index
its r full small-prime types by 0, . . . , r − 1, with 0 the empty type, and
prescribe integer quotas ai on A. Choose a set R ⊆ {1, . . . , r − 1} of k > 0
types. During the geometric    construction, replace these colors by one color
⋆, with quota a⋆ = i∈R ai . There are g = r − k + 1 geometric colors.
                    P

Lemma 8.5.1 (A preferred group in the original forest). In the original-
forest construction of Theorem 6.3.2, one prescribed group ⋆ may be required
to have degree error h⋆ ≤ 0. The exact color quotas, the edge property outside
the separator, the tag-count bound g/4, and either separator bound of that
theorem remain valid. The degree errors then satisfy
                                      1X
(8.5.1)            |hi | ≤ D,    H=         |hi | ≤ D⌊g/2⌋.
                                      2 i
A permitted donor exchange toward color 0 cannot increase the degree sum
of a nonzero preferred group.
Proof. Keep the fractional three-measure construction, its cuts, and its prun-
ing exactly as in Theorem 6.3.2. At termination the remaining color quotas
on the A-singletons are integers. Round those singletons with Lemma 4.3.3,
using their fixed degrees in [0, D] and selecting ⋆ as the preferred color. This
gives (8.5.1) and h⋆ ≤ 0. The separate rounding of B-singletons is unchanged,
so its g/4 variation bound and the edge property remain valid. A donor
exchange transfers degree into color 0 and out of a nonzero color: the two
vertices keep the same pair of colors, but the nonzero color moves to a vertex
of no larger degree. Hence a nonzero preferred group’s degree sum does
not rise. The error bound in (8.5.1) refers to the assignment before these
exchanges; their degree transfers are charged separately.                     □
                                      227
   The connected construction permits a preferred group by Proposition 4.3.5.
Its paired separator bound is Corollary 8.3.3. The next refinement applies
to both constructions.

8.6. A full-type bridge through order 559999
   We return to individual small primes. On each row let P = {3, 5, . . . , z}

independently with probability 1/p. The rare group of Section 8.5 is used
only while constructing the forest assignment. The label types, position tags,
compatibility graph, local tail profiles, and clipped shortfall are all evaluated
on the full 2|P | types.

Theorem 8.7.2 (The initial interval through order 1982759). Every tree of
order at most 1982759 has a prime labeling.
Proof. Theorem 8.6.3 handles the preceding orders. For 560000 ≤ n ≤
1982759, prepare any tree as in Proposition 6.3.3. A zero residual degree
is handled there. Otherwise select the unique interval containing n and its
row for the residual degree. The structural constructions give exact even-
type quotas, full odd-position tags, and the paid absorbed set. The exact
certificate bounds the two type laws, the local conflict probability, and the
global number of deleted matching edges. Their simultaneous success follows
as in Theorem 8.6.3. The asymmetric Hall criterion gives a perfect matching
of the remaining labels and positions. Finally restore the core and special

              9. Logarithmic bridges from 29/2 to 256
  This part proves that every tree of order n with 29/2 ≤ log n ≤ 256 has
a prime labeling. The interval is divided into nineteen closed intervals of
                                      235
log n, and each is covered by one bridge theorem. Every bridge prepares
the tree as in Section 3, sometimes with a variant proved in its subsection,
assigns the even labels with exact type quotas, chooses a permutation of the
even labels within exact classes, and places the odd labels by a Hall criterion.
Its finite certificate divides the interval into closed cells, either rectangles in
log n and a normalized residual degree or unit intervals of log n, and lists
rational inequalities that are checked on every cell and imply the hypotheses
of the construction.
   Table 30 lists the bridges. Each bridge after the first uses the construction
of an earlier one with the changes listed in the table; most bridge subsections
begin by stating which parts are unchanged. Section 9.3 proves the degree-
balanced refinement and the Hall criterion with empty positions on which the
third bridge and several later ones rely. The union of the nineteen intervals
is taken in Section 9.21 (Theorem 9.21.1).

9.1. The first logarithmic bridge
  The integer certificates stop immediately below log n = 29/2. For the
next range the number of possible full types is still at most 256, so we can
assign them all at once. The difficulty is geometric: an ordinary cut at a
high-degree vertex may cost too many positions. We first record a bound
that distinguishes the number of cuts from the number of neighbors they
expose.

With d = 3(r − 1), put ZC = (C ∩ B) ∪ NG (C ∩ A). Then every edge whose
B-end avoids ZC has equal endpoint color and tag, and
(9.1.3)    |C| ≤ C∗ (N, d),     |ZC | ≤ min{B∗ (N, d, D), C∗ (N, d) + RA }.
For r = 1 take C = ∅ and color both sides alike.
Proof. Use the three additive coordinates consisting of A-count, A-degree sum
in the original forest, and B-count. Begin with each component carrying the
vector of fractional color weights π. The pruning argument of Theorem 6.3.2
preserves all three coordinates and leaves at most d = 3(r − 1) mixed
connected objects. Cut a largest nonsingleton mixed object at a centroid,
giving its weight vector to the cut vertex and to every component left by its
deletion, and prune again. Frozen objects stay frozen. After finitely many
cuts, every mixed object is a singleton.
   An object in the bucket 2j < |U | ≤ 2j+1 has every child of size at most 2j .
It cannot return to that bucket after a cut. At most d mixed objects occur
there, and the objects cut in one bucket are disjoint, so at most ⌊N/(2j + 1)⌋
are cut. This proves the first inequality of (9.1.3). An A-centroid in that
bucket has at most min(D, 2j+1 − 1) neighbors; a B-centroid is charged
one. Summing gives the first bound on |ZC |. Alternatively each A-centroid
costs at most one plus (dG (a) − 1)+ , while each B-centroid costs one. The
A-centroids are distinct, proving the second bound.
   Next reduce the mixed A-singletons once more, now preserving only their
color counts and color degree sums. These are 2(r − 1) constraints, so the
same dimension argument leaves at most fA ≤ 2(r − 1) mixed A-singletons;
newly pure atoms are frozen. For each color, its target count minus its
number of frozen vertices is a nonnegative integer, and these integers sum to
fA . Assign the mixed A-singletons these remaining counts. The A-quotas are
then exact. Let ηv,i be the resulting color indicators and
                                                         P v,i
                                                              θ the fractional
weights just before this assignment. Exact counts give v (ηv,i − θv,i ) = 0 for
every color, and the degree sums were preserved until this step. Therefore
              X                       X           D
                   dG (a) − e(G)πi =     dG (v) −      (ηv,i − θv,i ),
              a∈A                     v            2
             c(a)=i

the sum being over the mixed A-singletons. For each such v, |dG (v) − D/2| ≤
         1P
D/2 and 2 i |ηv,i − θv,i | ≤ 1. Hence H ≤ DfA /2 ≤ D(r − 1). The centering
by D/2 uses the exact counts. Round the B-counts by giving the largest

remainders the necessary upward roundings. The same calculation as in
Theorem 6.3.2 gives r/4. An edge with neither endpoint a cut vertex remains
in one frozen monochromatic object. Thus the singleton roundings do not
change any edge whose B-end avoids ZC .                                  □
  The next exchange lets us keep the tags while reducing the geometric
boundary. Color 0 will be the empty small-prime type. Its compatibility with
every odd type makes the direction of the exchange significant. Figure 11
shows the cut vertex and its low-degree donor.
Lemma 9.1.2 (Empty-type exchanges at the separator). Let the assignment
of Lemma 9.1.1 have empty-type count a0 and empty-type degree sum E0 .
For an integer t ≥ 1 assume
                                E0
(9.1.4)                   a0 −     ≥ |C ∩ A|.
                               t+1

choose a set Z ⊆ B such that
                            |Z| ≤ min(t, D)|C|,
and every edge to B \ Z has A-color either 0 or equal to its B-tag. If Ei′
denotes the new degree sums, then
(9.1.5)
   E0′ = E0 + ∆,    Ei′ = Ei − ∆i (i ̸= 0),

Proof. Let S be the nonempty-colored vertices in C ∩ A of degree greater
than t, and let h be the number of empty-colored vertices in C ∩ A. At most
E0 /(t + 1) empty-colored vertices have degree greater than t. By (9.1.4), at
least a0 − h − E0 /(t + 1) ≥ |C ∩ A| − h ≥ |S| empty-colored vertices outside
C have degree at most t. Choose distinct such donors and exchange their
colors with the members of S. Every transfer of degree is strictly toward the
empty color, which proves (9.1.5).
   Let L be the nonempty-colored A-centroids of degree at most t, and let U
be the chosen donors. Take
                      Z = (C ∩ B) ∪ NG (L) ∪ NG (U ).
An edge outside Z from an unchanged noncentroid retains its equal tag.
A high-degree centroid now has empty color, and a low-degree nonempty
centroid or donor has no neighbor outside Z. These cases exhaust the edges.
Since |L| + |U | ≤ |C ∩ A|,
           |Z| ≤ |C ∩ B| + min(t, D)(|L| + |U |) ≤ min(t, D)|C|.
When D < t + 1, S is empty. This proves the last assertion.                □

Proof. For every ordinary prefix, the empty-coordinate discrepancy is at most
Co /2: the coordinate errors sum to zero and their total absolute value is at
most Co . Multiplication by a prime p > z preserves the P -type. Consequently
at most α⌊m/p⌋ + Co /2 empty-type half-labels are divisible by p. Removing
the special labels can only reduce this numerator, while a0 remains the
denominator. Summing over the large prime divisors of y proves (9.1.6).
Their product is at most y ≤ n, proving the second assertion there.
   For the sum over odd y, there are at most n/(2p) + 1 odd multiples of p

(9.1.7). Summing the discrepancy terms uses z<p≤m 1/p ≤ 1 + log n and

gives the second. For the remaining +1 term, average the number of large
prime divisors over the retained empty labels; each has at most log n/ log z
of them. This gives the last term.                                          □

Proposition 9.1.4 (The first logarithmic certificate). The parameter table
partitions [29/2, 16] into 30 consecutive closed logarithmic intervals. On
each, its closed degree intervals cover [0, 1]. Its 83 rectangles satisfy all 2905
rational conditions, including (9.1.8) and (9.1.11). Every deletion-budget
ratio and every absorbed-position-to-universal-supply ratio is strictly less than
995/1000.
Proof. The table and exact evaluator are supplied in the first_log_bridge
directory of the supplement. The evaluator verifies the interval joins and
all degree endpoints as rational numbers. It recomputes the eight complete
small-prime residue periods by integer enumeration; periodicity, not a sample
of orders, proves the prefix discrepancies for every n. It encloses exponentials,
logarithms, square roots, and prime tails in directed rational bounds, and
adds an infinite remainder to the dyadic separator sum. It verifies the prime-
count domains, preparation, donor supply, both type-law errors, and every
condition above on each whole rectangle. Reproducing the frozen report
gives the stated counts and strict ratios.                                    □
Theorem 9.1.5 (Orders in the first logarithmic interval). Every tree of
order n with 29/2 ≤ log n ≤ 16 has a prime labeling.
Proof. Choose the balanced independent side and the prime-labeled core as
in Sections 3.3 and 3.4. Put all special even labels on vertices of highest
original degree, and let G = (A, B) be the remaining forest. The checked
prime supply makes the preparation valid throughout this interval. Select a
certificate rectangle containing log n and x = 2w(D − 1)/n.
   Lemma 9.1.1 gives exact full-type quotas. Lemma 9.1.2, whose hypothesis
is (9.1.8), leaves every tag fixed while ensuring that an odd label disjoint
from a tag is coprime at all adjacent retained positions with respect to primes
at most z. Assign the absorbed positions distinct universal odd labels. Their
remaining number is at least g− n. The odd and slot type laws have variation
at most V from µ and νξ .
   Permute even labels independently inside their exact full types. Equa-
tions (9.1.6)– (9.1.9) bound the local and total conflicts from primes greater
than z. The local failure probability is at most 1/10000; Markov’s inequality
                                       243
gives a global failure probability at most 1000/1001. Their sum is less than
one, so one permutation satisfies both bounds. The comparisons (9.1.11)
and Theorem 8.1.2 give a perfect matching. Restore the absorbed positions,
special labels, and core, then apply the sparse parity-preserving repair to
the possible p, 2p pairs. Every edge of the resulting bijection has coprime
labels.                                                                    □

9.2. A simultaneous full-type bridge
   Above logarithm 16 we can use the full-type assignment of Lemma 9.1.1
without the donor exchanges. Its ordinary boundary becomes small enough
to be absorbed by the universal odd labels. We keep the alternative bound
through the excess degree because it is valid on every certificate rectangle,
even though the accepted rows choose the ordinary bound.

remaining even half-labels have exact quotas in every full P -type.

labels is the same as in the preceding section. A second consequence controls
the excess degree outside F .

Theorem 9.2.3 (Orders in the second logarithmic interval). Every tree of
order n with 16 ≤ log n ≤ 343/20 has a prime labeling.
Proof. Prepare the tree as above and choose a rectangle containing (log n, x).
The full-type assignment gives every exact even-label quota and bounds its
exposed positions by χn. Assign those positions distinct universal odd labels.
Every retained edge has equal A-type and B-tag, so matching an odd label
to a disjoint tag avoids all small-prime conflicts on that edge.
   Permute the even labels independently inside each exact type class. The
local and global bounds of Lemma 9.3.2 give respectively (9.2.8) and (9.2.7).
A local failure has probability at most 1/10000; by Markov, the global
failure probability is at most 1000/1001. Their sum is less than one. For a
permutation satisfying both, the checked row inequalities and Theorem 8.1.2
give a perfect matching on the residual labels and positions. Restore the
absorbed labels, special labels, and core. The sparse repair handles the
permitted p, 2p pairs, completing a prime labeling of the tree.            □

9.3. Adaptive refinement and one-sided Hall reserves
   The simultaneous assignment of Section 9.1 uses one coordinate for each
full type. When the cutoff prime grows, we instead refine types one prime at
a time and combine rare classes temporarily. The next lemma adds degree
balance to the quota construction of Section 7.1. All degrees are measured in
the fixed original forest, including after a separator vertex has been removed.
   For integers N, d, D, t ≥ 1 define

Lemma 9.3.1 (Quotas with degree balance). Let G = (A, B) be a bipar-
tite forest on N ≥ 1 vertices. Partition its sides into nonempty classes
A1 , . . . , AcA and B1 , . . . , BcB , allowing either list to be empty when its side
is empty. Put d = 2cA + cB and suppose d ≥ 1. Let D ≥ max{1, ∆G (A)},
0 ≤ ρ ≤ 1, and t ≥ 1 be an integer. Prescribe 0 ≤ ki ≤ ai = |Ai |, and write
                           X                        X
                    Ei =          dG (v),     δ=            (ki − ρai )+ .
                           v∈Ai                         i

There are sets W ⊆ A and T ⊆ B such that
(9.3.3)                        |W ∩ Ai | = ki ,            NG (W ) ⊆ T,

                                 ki           cA
             X     X                                         
(9.3.4)                  dG (v) − Ei ≤ D dt +    ,
              i   v∈W ∩A
                                 ai            2
                         i

(9.3.5)                         |T ∩ Bj | ≥ ρ|Bj |,
                  X                        
(9.3.6)                |T ∩ Bj | − ρ|Bj | ≤ Bt (N, d, D) + dt + Dδ.
                   j
If A is empty, the degree and quota claims are vacuous.
Proof. Step 1. Preserve all three kinds of coordinates. Give every connected
component of G selection weight ρ. Its vector records the counts in the
A-classes, the degree sums in those classes, and the counts in the B-classes.
There are d coordinates. If more than d objects have fractional weights, a
linear dependence among their vectors permits a nonzero change of weights
preserving all coordinates. Move until one weight reaches 0 or 1 and freeze
that object. The number of fractional objects decreases, so this pruning
terminates.
   If a fractional object has more than t vertices, choose one of largest order
and split it at a centroid. Keep the centroid as a singleton object, give it
and every remaining component the parent’s weight, and prune again. The
coordinates add under the split. Every new object is smaller, and no vertex
is cut twice; eventually at most d fractional objects remain, each of order at
most t.
   In the bucket t2j < |U | ≤ t2j+1 , a split produces only objects of order at
most t2j . At most d objects can be split in this bucket: at its first split no
larger fractional object remains, and no later split can enter the bucket. The
objects split in one bucket are pairwise disjoint, so their number is also at
most ⌊N/(t2j + 1)⌋. Thus the number of centroids is at most Ct (N, d). For
each A-centroid, charge only its neighbors outside the final separator; for
each B-centroid, charge one. Every charged neighbor belonged to the object
when it was split, so this external boundary is at most Bt (N, d, D).
   Step 2. Meet the exact quotas. Round every remaining fractional object
upward. Let U be the union of objects of weight one. Only at most d objects
of order at most t have changed, so, for Ai ,
                                                               X
            si := |U ∩ Ai | = ρai + xi ,         xi ≥ 0,              xi ≤ dt.
                                                                  i
If e0i is the degree sum on U ∩ Ai , the preserved degree coordinate gives
                                      si
(9.3.7)                          e0i − Ei ≤ Dxi .
                                      ai
Indeed, the difference is the sum, over newly rounded vertices, of the fractional
increase times dG (v) − Ei /ai , and each absolute summand is at most D times
that increase.
                                           248
   We use the following elementary selection fact. Among numbers in [0, D],
a k-subset can be chosen whose sum differs from k times the overall mean
by at most D/2. The target is between the sums of the k smallest and the
k largest numbers, because it is the average over all k-subsets. A sequence
of single-element exchanges from the smallest to the largest subset can be
chosen with nondecreasing sums and steps at most D; take the closer sum
at the first crossing. The empty and full subsets are exact.
   In Ai , if ki ≤ si , retain an appropriate ki -subset of U ∩ Ai ; otherwise retain
all of it and add an appropriate (ki − si )-subset from its complement. Call
the resulting union W . In the first case, the deviation in (9.3.7) is multiplied
by ki /si ≤ 1; in the second it is multiplied by (ai − ki )/(ai − si ) ≤ 1. The
selection adds at most D/2. Empty denominators occur only in the exact
endpoint cases and are omitted. Summing over i proves (9.3.4). Moreover,
the number of newly added vertices is at most δ, since si ≥ ρai .
   Step 3. Keep the opposite-side lower counts. Let C be the set of cut
centroids and put
                       T = (U ∩ B) ∪ (C ∩ B) ∪ NG (W ).
All fractional objects were rounded upward and all B-centroids are included,
so |T ∩ Bj | ≥ ρ|Bj |. The rounding itself contributes at most dt positions
above the target. Neighbors of selected A-vertices outside their selected
objects lie on the external boundary of C, of size at most Bt (N, d, D). The
at most δ newly added A-vertices contribute at most Dδ further neighbors.
Summing these three charges proves (9.3.6).                               □
   The same assignment of even labels must satisfy a bound at every odd
label and a bound on the total number of large-prime conflicts. The next
lemma gives both bounds for one permutation. It uses degrees in the original
residual forest, before any marked position is absorbed.

                                               250
  A union bound makes the probability of any failed local estimate at most
1/10000. Markov’s inequality makes the probability of exceeding 1001/1000
times the global expectation at most 1000/1001. The two probabilities sum
to less than one. If the expectation vanishes, the global cost is zero almost
surely. Thus the same permutation satisfies both bounds.                   □
  The residual matching has a larger stock of universal odd labels than

positions at all. We therefore state a Hall criterion for different model laws
on the two sides and a reserve on only the label side.

Theorem 9.3.3 (Asymmetric Hall criterion with empty positions). Let X
be odd labels and B positions, both of size R > 0. Their empirical type laws
are within total variation V of µ and νη , respectively. Start with all pairs of
disjoint types, and delete at most M edges. Assume every label retains more
than τ R neighbors and at least g labels remain adjacent to every position.
Choose g0 and s with
                                             1
     0 < g0 ≤ g, g0 ≤ τ R, 0 ≤ V < τ ≤ , 0 < s ≤ 1, s − V > τ.
                                             2
For the actual position tags set
                                     X
                          AB (s) =         (s − cs (J(v)))+ .
                                     v∈B
If M is strictly less than each of
(9.3.11)                       g0 ((s − V )R − g0 ) − RAB (s),
(9.3.12)                          R2 τ (s − V − τ ) − RAB (s),
                   R2 b (1 − θ)τ (1 − τ ) − V + (1 − θ)V 2 ,
                                                                    
(9.3.13)
then the remaining graph has a perfect matching.
Proof. Suppose Hall’s condition fails, and take nonempty U ⊆ X and W =
B \ N (U ) with |U | + |W | > R. Put u = |U |/R and v = |W |/R. A universal
label cannot lie in U , so |W | > g ≥ g0 . If u < τ , every label of U has fewer
than R − |W | < |U | < τ R remaining neighbors, a contradiction. Hence
u ≥ τ.
                                           251
   If v < τ , a position of tag J initially has at least R(cs (J) − V ) compatible
labels. At most R − |U | < |W | lie outside U . Using cs (J) ≥ s − (s − cs (J))+
and summing over W , the number of initially compatible pairs in U × W is
strictly greater than
                                                  
                         |W | (s − V )R − |W | − RAB (s).
The quadratic is concave for g0 ≤ |W | ≤ τ R; its minimum occurs at an
endpoint. The two endpoint values are (9.3.11) and (9.3.12), contrary to the
bound on deleted edges.
   It remains that u, v ≥ τ . Reduce the two selected type submeasures
fractionally until their masses sum to one. Truncate them at µ, νη , losing at
most V from each side, and reduce again to model submeasures of masses
x, y ≥ τ − V with x + y = 1 − 2V . Let J0 be the coordinate coupling giving
probabilities ai , ρi , 1 − ai − ρi to (1, 0), (0, 1), (0, 0). It is supported on disjoint
types. Its density relative to µν is at most 1/b, and its maximal correlation
is at most θ, by the orthonormal product expansion used in Lemma 7.2.1.
The mixture
                               Jη = (1 − η)J0 + η µ ⊗ δ∅
couples µ to νη and is still supported on disjoint types. Its density relative to
µνη is at most 1/b: for a nonempty second type this follows from the product
coupling; at the empty type, the additional ηµ term has density at most
1/b because b ≤ 1. For functions centered under their respective marginals,
covariance under Jη is (1 − η) times covariance under J0 . The variance of
the second function under νη is at least (1√− η) times its variance under ν.
Thus the maximal correlation is at most 1 − η θ ≤ θ. Consequently the
original rectangle contains at least
                                       q                     
                         R2 b xy − θ xy(1 − x)(1 − y)

compatible pairs. As x + y = 1 − 2V , (1 − x)(1 − y) = xy + 2V , whence the
square root is at most xy + V . Concavity also gives xy ≥ τ (1 − τ ) − V + V 2 .
Substitution yields (9.3.13); all these pairs would have to be deleted, again a
contradiction.                                                               □

9.4. A logarithmic bridge without reserved positions
   We next cover the interval 343/20 ≤ log n ≤ 20. Here the number of
primes in the cutoff can vary from one certificate rectangle to another.
Keeping all full types as separate coordinates would make the separator too
large. We therefore use the sequential refinement of Section 7.1, with the
degree coordinate added in Lemma 9.3.1. The ordinary tags are limited to a
prescribed support size; positions that exceed it receive universal odd labels.
   The clipped-shortfall term in the Hall inequalities must be calculated
before those positions are removed. We record the bound in the form used
by the certificate. Write P = {p1 < · · · < pk }, ai = 1/pi , and fi = 1 − ai , so

Lemma 9.4.1 (Shortfall of capped tags). Suppose B + has at most on
positions, each tagged by a subset of P of size at most q, or by †. Let its
tag counting measure be within en in total variation of |B + |(cq )∗ ν, where cq
sends sets of size greater than q to †. If B is obtained by deleting positions
of B + , including every position tagged †, then for 0 < t ≤ 1,
                  1 X
(9.4.1)                 (t − cs (J(v)))+ ≤ oΦq (t) + e(t − σq )+ .
                  n v∈B
In particular Φq (t) = 0 when t ≤ σq .
Proof. Define the nonnegative function F on capped tags by F (J) = (t −
cs (J))+ for ordinary J, and F (†) = 0. It vanishes at the empty tag. The
product of any at most q factors from the nondecreasing sequence fi is at
least σq . Hence 0 ≤ F ≤ (t − σq )+ . Total variation changes the integral of a
function in this interval by at most en(t − σq )+ . If |J| ≤ q, then F (cq (J))
is the summand in Φq (t). If |J| > q, it is zero and is still at most that
summand. Thus E(cq )∗ ν F ≤ Φq (t). Removing positions can only decrease a
nonnegative sum, which proves (9.4.1).                                       □
Lemma 9.4.2 (Variation after paying marked positions). Let Ξ be a counting
measure of mass s on ordinary types J of size at most q and the mark †, with
                 Ξ − s(cq )∗ ν TV ≤ E,           β = ν{J : |J| > q}.
Delete the c = Ξ(†) marked positions and adjoin h ≥ 0 positions of empty
type. The resulting counting measure Λ has mass ℓ = s − c + h. If ℓ > 0 and
η = h/ℓ, then
                     Λ                         E + sβ
(9.4.2)                − (1 − η)ν + ηδ∅      ≤         .
                     ℓ                    TV       ℓ
Proof. Write δ = Ξ − s(cq )∗ ν and d = δ(†) = c − sβ. The positive mass of
the signed measure δ is at most E. If d > 0, its positive mass on ordinary
types is at most E − d; if d ≤ 0, that mass is at most E.
  Let Ξord be Ξ restricted to ordinary types, extended by zero to all full
types. For an event A of full types,
                                  
             Ξord − (s − c)ν (A) = δ(A ∩ {|J| ≤ q})
                                         + cν(A) − sν(A ∩ {|J| > q}).
If d > 0, the right side is at most (E − d) + cν(A) ≤ E + sβ, because
c = sβ + d. If d ≤ 0, it is at most E + cν(A) ≤ E + sβ. The two measures
in the difference have the same mass s − c, so the supremum of its positive
                                          253
values is their total variation distance. Adding h empty positions to both
measures does not change that distance. Divide by ℓ to obtain (9.4.2). □
   The quantity Φq (t) is a finite product-law expectation. Its rational upper-
bound evaluator scans the primes in increasing order and retains at most q
selected factors. If the current product is already at most t, the remaining
conditional expectation follows from the corresponding truncated product
moment; if even the smallest possible remaining product exceeds t, the
branch contributes zero. All values are rational, with outward rounding at
the finite arithmetic resolution.
Upper parameters and arithmetic inputs. A row cannot use the actual selec-
tion probabilities ρi of the refinement, since they depend on the order. It
uses rational upper bounds ρ̄i ≥ ρi , and it bounds several arithmetic errors
by finite sums. We justify these substitutions here. The statements are used
in this section and in all later logarithmic bridges.
Lemma 9.4.3 (Evaluation at upper probabilities). Let 0 ≤ ρi ≤ ρ̄i ≤ 1
for 1 ≤ i ≤ k, and let ν, ν̄ be the laws of the random sets J ⊆ [k] with
independent coordinates of probabilities ρi and ρ̄i , respectively.
    (1) For every integer q ≥ 0, ν(|J| > q) ≤ ν̄(|J| > q).
    (2) Let 0 < f1 ≤ · · · ≤ fk ≤ 1, let q ≥ 0 be an integer and 0 ≤ t ≤ 1.
        Write J [q] for the first min(q, |J|) indices of J, cs (J) = i∈J fi , and
                                                                    Q
                                               Y          
                             ψ(J) = t −               fi           .
                                                               +
                                            i∈J [q]
        Then ψ is nondecreasing under inclusion,
                   (t − cs (J))+ 1|J|≤q ≤ ψ(J) ≤ (t − cs (J))+ ,
        and consequently Eν [(t − cs (J))+ 1|J|≤q ] ≤ Eν ψ ≤ Eν̄ ψ.
Proof. Take independent uniform variables Ui on [0, 1] and put Xi = 1Ui ≤ρi
and X̄i = 1Ui ≤ρ̄i . The random sets J = {i : Xi = 1} and J¯ = {i : X̄i = 1}
have laws ν, ν̄, and J ⊆ J¯ for every outcome. The event |J| > q is preserved
under enlargement, which proves the first assertion.
   If |J| ≤ q, then J [q] = J and the left comparison is an equality; otherwise
its left side is zero. Since the factors increase, J [q] retains the smallest factors
of J, and their product is at least cs (J); this is the right comparison. Adding
an index to J either multiplies the retained product by a further factor at
most one, replaces a retained factor by a smaller one, or leaves the retained
set unchanged. The product does not increase, so ψ does not decrease. With
J ⊆ J¯ this gives Eν ψ ≤ Eν̄ ψ.                                                    □
  The first part justifies computing the overflow probability β as the exact
Bernoulli tail with the parameters ρ̄i . The recursion keeps the probabilities



                                          254
v0 , . . . , vq of the states 0, . . . , q and v∗ of the absorbing state > q. A coordinate
of probability p maps them to
          (1 − p)v0 ,        (1 − p)vj + pvj−1 (1 ≤ j ≤ q),              v∗ + pvq .
All coefficients are nonnegative, so rounding every value upward preserves
an upper bound; no upper partial sum is subtracted from one. The function
Φq (t) of Lemma 9.4.1 is Eν ψ. By the second part it may be evaluated with
the parameters ρ̄i by the recursion described above, which conditions on
successive coordinates and uses the exact product c already selected.
   Replacing ρi by ρ̄i decreases b and every label compatibility i∈I (1 − ρi ),
                                                                Q

and increases θ. A label shortfall increases when its compatibility decreases.
A positive wide budget is nondecreasing in b and nonincreasing in θ, and
the narrow and middle budgets do not contain b. Hence each of these
substitutions weakens the certified inequalities.
   The number of coordinates in a refinement step can be bounded by the
classes that can actually occur.

by at most D. With q = qA and m ≤ n/2, the normalized restoration cost is
at most D+ rare, where rare = eqA +2 (P )/2.

The finite certificate reads the parameter table, checks these exact joins
and every degree join, recomputes the eight complete prime-prefix periods,
and evaluates the displayed inequalities with integers and directed rational

tail and overflow bounds, positive universal supply, local label degree, and
all three strict Hall budgets. The 64-term boundary sum has the explicit tail
in (9.4.12); the large-prime profile includes the first impossible additional

ratio is less than 0.98524, and the largest displayed universal-supply ratio

9.6. A bridge by degree-balanced sorting
   The preparation with protected positions from Section 9.5 remains useful
above logarithm 128/5. We now choose the even labels within each exact
type by degree. This gives a deterministic bound on all large-prime deletions.
It does not require the pointwise estimate used in the preceding bridge, so
both directions of Hall’s condition must be handled by shortfall bounds.

Proof. Suppose Hall’s condition fails. Choose U ⊆ X and W = B \ N (U )
with |U | + |W | > R. Both sets are nonempty. A label adjacent to every
position is not in U , for then W would be empty; a position adjacent to every
label is not in W , since U ̸= ∅. Hence |U |, |W | ≤ R − h, and so |U |, |W | > h.
Put q = min{|U |, |W |}.
   If q < τ R, count type-compatible pairs from the smaller set into the larger.
For a label of type I, the model compatibility probability on the position
side is at least cX (I); for a position of type J, it is cB (J). Total variation
costs at most V in either case. Fewer than q vertices lie outside the larger
set. Thus the rectangle originally contained more than
                           q((s − V )R − q) − RA(s)
compatible pairs. The quadratic is concave on [h, τ R]; its endpoint values
are the first two expressions in (9.6.2). Every such pair would have been
deleted, a contradiction.

and empty-position mixture in the proof of Theorem 9.3.3 give at least

9.7. Degree balance after fixed centroid rounds
   The boundary estimate in Lemma 9.3.1 stops when every fractional object
has at most a prescribed number of vertices. For the next range, it is enough
to make a fixed number of centroid splits at each prime. We keep the degree
coordinates, so the sorting bound of Lemma 9.6.1 continues to apply.

Proof. Give every connected component weight ρ. Its vector records counts
in the A- and B-classes and degree sums in the A-classes. It has d coordinates.
Prune fractional weights by the linear-dependence argument of Lemma 9.3.1.
At most d fractional objects remain; every object reaching weight zero or
one is fixed.
   Perform r rounds. In one round, split each fractional object of order
greater than one at a centroid, retain the centroid as a singleton with its
parent’s weight, and give the resulting components that same weight. Then
prune back to at most d fractional objects. The coordinate vector is additive
under a split, so every coordinate total is preserved. At most d centroids are
cut in one round. Every resulting nonsingleton has at most half its parent’s
vertices; objects fixed at an earlier step never return. Thus after r rounds
                                           272
each fractional object has at most tr vertices, and the separator C has at
most dr vertices.

9.8. Centroid rounds measured on the position side
  We continue from logarithm 143/5. The degree coordinates used in the
preceding section make the rounding dimension larger. In the present range
we keep only the class counts. This reduces the cost of a centroid round,
but requires a separate estimate for large-prime deletions. We give both
arguments before stating the finite certificate.
                                      274
Lemma 9.8.1 (Quotas after weighted centroid rounds). Let G = (A, B) be
a bipartite forest. Partition its two sides into d ≥ 1 nonempty classes Ai , Bj ,
with either list possibly empty. Suppose
              D ≥ max{1, ∆G (A)}, 0 ≤ ρ ≤ 1, 0 ≤ ki ≤ |Ai |,
                                   X
                              δ=       (ki − ρ|Ai |)+ .
                                   i

For every integer r ≥ 0, there are W ⊆ A and Tb ⊆ B satisfying
           |W ∩ Ai | = ki ,     NG (W ) ⊆ Tb,         |Tb ∩ Bj | ≥ ρ|Bj |,
and
              X
                   |Tb ∩ Bj | − ρ|Bj | ≤ d (Dr + max{1, |B|/2r }) + Dδ.
                                   
(9.8.1)
               j
If B = ∅, the left side is zero.
Proof. The empty-B case follows by choosing each A-quota arbitrarily. Oth-
erwise represent every component of G by its vector of d class counts and give
it weight ρ. Along a linear dependence among more than d fractional vectors,
move their weights until one reaches 0 or 1, preserving every coordinate total.
Freeze integral objects. At most d fractional objects remain.
   We perform r rounds. For a fractional connected object U with at least
two B-vertices, choose a vertex c such that every component of U − c has at
most |U ∩ B|/2 vertices in B. To find it, start at any vertex and move into
a component carrying more than half the B-weight while such a component
exists. The move cannot immediately reverse, so the process terminates.
Retain {c} as an object, and give it and every component of U − c the old
weight of U . Thus the objects still partition the vertices and every coordinate
sum remains ρ times its class size. Prune the fractional weights again. There
were at most d cuts in the round.
   After r rounds, every fractional object has at most tr = max{1, |B|/2r }
vertices in B. Indeed, a split halves this count, while an unsplit object has
at most one B-vertex; pruning never joins objects. Let C be the set of cut
vertices, so |C| ≤ dr. Round the remaining fractional weights upward and
let U1 be the union of the objects of weight one. Every class count in U1 is
at least its original weighted count. The total excess on B is at most dtr .
   Meet each Ai -quota by deleting excess vertices from U1 ∩ Ai and adding
missing vertices outside it. At most δ additions are needed. Call the resulting
set W , and put
                      Tb = (U1 ∩ B) ∪ (C ∩ B) ∪ NG (W ).
For an originally chosen vertex outside C, every neighbor outside its object
lies in C. A chosen vertex in C can add at most D further marks. Newly
added vertices contribute at most Dδ marks; deletions contribute none. The
excess on B is consequently at most dtr + D|C| + Dδ, as claimed.              □

9.11. The type-profile bridge through logarithm 32
   Above logarithm 31 the certificate of Section 9.10 is changed in two places.
The protected reserve is taken from the second-order lower bound for the
number of special even labels, with a smaller fraction of these labels spent
on the vertices of largest degree. The three arithmetic errors are bounded by
grouping the Boolean expansion by the size of the product of its primes, with
a finite prime sum up to 10000 and an analytic remainder. No periodic block
is used. The type assignment, the geometric error, the deletion estimate and
the Hall budgets are those of Section 9.10. We cover 31 ≤ log n ≤ 32.

Theorem 9.11.4 (The tenth logarithmic bridge). Every tree of order n with
31 ≤ log n ≤ 32 has a prime labeling.
Proof. Prepare the tree by Lemma 9.11.1 and choose a certified rectangle
containing the logarithm and the normalized residual degree. At each
prime, Lemma 9.8.2 gives exact even-label quotas with all true neighbors
marked. Restore the rare exact classes, pay the marked positions with
distinct universal odd labels, and retain the protected positions. Sort the
even labels within their full prime types by Lemma 9.8.3; by Lemma 9.11.2
the centered arithmetic bound controls every deletion caused by a prime
above the cutoff. The row conditions bound both empirical type laws and
both integrated shortfalls, and verify the three strict budgets of Lemma 9.6.2.
Its perfect matching places the remaining odd labels. Restore the reserved
labels and the core, then perform the sparse prime-pair repair.             □

estimate and the Hall budgets are those of Section 9.11.

9.15. Symmetric moment bounds through logarithm 42
   On 39 ≤ log n ≤ 42 the construction of Section 9.14 is used with three
changes. The rounding at each prime uses a two-sided square-root bound
instead of the one-side bound of Lemma 9.8.2. The three arithmetic errors
are bounded by elementary symmetric moments of the reciprocal primes
and of their square roots, without product bins. The reserve estimate uses
slightly weaker logarithmic constants.
                                      296
A two-sided square-root bound.
Lemma 9.15.1 (A two-sided weighted rounding bound). In the setting of
Lemma 7.1.2, put Kρ = max{1 + ρ, Dρ}. There are sets W ⊆ A and T ⊆ B
such that |W ∩ Ai | = ki for every i, NG (W ) ⊆ T , and
                   X                                 q

ρ|X \ C| − |V∗ ∩ X|. Both are nonnegative. Each discarded fractional
component is counted once in LA + LB , with its weight times its size, so
LA + LB ≤ dt.

9.16. Truncated moment bounds through logarithm 48
   On 42 ≤ log n ≤ 48 the construction of Section 9.15 is used with the
Boolean expansions truncated at a prescribed degree r. The remainder of
a truncated expansion has a sharp bound, which we prove first. The rare
restoration is paid by the populations of the combined rare classes. The
preparation, the rounding bound of Lemma 9.15.1, the overflow bound and
the Hall conditions at the minimum compatibility are unchanged.
Truncated expansions. For f : 2P → [0, 1] and I ⊆ P let ∆I f =

of the tree.

9.18. Uniform upper probabilities through logarithm 72
   On 60 ≤ log n ≤ 72 the margins are large enough for three simplifications
of the construction of Section 9.17. Only a tenth of the special labels is
spent on vertices of large degree. All selection probabilities are bounded
by one common relative inflation of 1/p. The Hall criterion uses the global
compatibility bound b as its threshold. The interval is again cut into twelve
unit intervals with fixed parameters.

Theorem 9.18.4 (The seventeenth logarithmic bridge). Every tree of order
n with 60 ≤ log n ≤ 72 has a prime labeling.
Proof. Let a = min{⌊log n⌋, 71} and prepare the tree by Lemma 9.18.1.
At each prime, Lemma 9.15.1 gives exact even-label quotas with all true
neighbors marked, and Lemma 9.18.2 controls the probabilities. Restore
the rare classes, pay the marked positions with distinct universal odd labels,
and sort the even labels within their full types by Lemma 9.8.3. The row
conditions verify the Hall conditions above, so Lemma 9.6.2 gives a perfect
matching of the remaining odd labels. Restore the reserved labels and the
core, then perform the sparse prime-pair repair.                           □

degree enters only as a factor. The arithmetic bounds, the common inflation
and the Hall conditions at the global compatibility are those of Sections 9.16
and 9.18.

9.21. The logarithmic range
  We collect the nineteen bridges. Put
   (a0 , . . . , a9 ) = (29/2, 16, 343/20, 20, 128/5, 53/2, 143/5, 144/5, 29, 31),
 (a10 , . . . , a19 ) = (32, 35, 37, 39, 42, 48, 60, 72, 96, 256).
For 1 ≤ i ≤ 19, the ith bridge theorem of Table 30 proves that every tree of
order n with ai−1 ≤ log n ≤ ai has a prime labeling.
Theorem 9.21.1 (Orders with 29/2 ≤ log n ≤ 256). Every tree of order n
with 29/2 ≤ log n ≤ 256 has a prime labeling.
Proof. The numbers a0 < a1 < · · · < a19 increase, so the closed intervals
[ai−1 , ai ], 1 ≤ i ≤ 19, cover [a0 , a19 ] = [29/2, 256]. If 29/2 ≤ log n ≤ 256,
choose i with ai−1 ≤ log n ≤ ai and apply the ith bridge theorem.              □
Remark 9.21.2. For 0 ≤ i ≤ 18 let Ni be the integer in Table 31. Directed
rational logarithms give
                             log Ni < ai < log(Ni + 1).
Hence the orders covered by the ith bridge and by no earlier bridge are
exactly the integers Ni−1 < n ≤ Ni for 1 ≤ i ≤ 18, and together with
Theorem 8.7.2 the bridges through the ith one cover every order at most Ni .
The proof of Theorem 1.1 uses only the case i = 0, which shows that every
order after 1982759 has logarithm greater than 29/2.

                      10. Proof of Theorem 1.1
   The orders through 1982759 are covered by a chain of cumulative theorems,
listed in Table 32. Each of them states the assertion for all orders up to
its endpoint. The first, Theorem 5.8.4, is proved by the finite certificates
of Section 5. The proof of each later one applies the direct results for
the new orders, listed in Table 1, and the preceding cumulative result to
the remaining orders. Each new range begins at the integer following the
preceding endpoint, so the chain omits no order.
   The larger orders are covered by the logarithmic bridges, Theorem 9.21.1,
and by the analytic argument, Theorem 7.7.2.
Proof of Theorem 1.1. Let T be a tree of order n ≥ 1. If n ≤ 1982759,
Theorem 8.7.2 gives a prime labeling of T . Otherwise n ≥ 1982760, and
Remark 9.21.2 gives log n ≥ log 1982760 > 29/2. If moreover log n ≤ 256,
Theorem 9.21.1 gives a prime labeling of T . If log n > 256, Theorem 7.7.2
gives one.                                                              □
  The results used in this proof depend on finite computations in the way
described in Section 11.

         Table 32. The cumulative results for the integer orders.

            1 ≤ n ≤ 1753       Theorem 6.9.2      Section 6.9
            1 ≤ n ≤ 2209       Theorem 6.10.4     Section 6.10

            1 ≤ n ≤ 559999     Theorem 8.6.3      Sections 8.5–8.6
            1 ≤ n ≤ 1982759    Theorem 8.7.2      Section 8.7

                    11. Computational verification
  The proof of Theorem 1.1 is computer-assisted. This section states which
parts are proved in the text and which are computed, explains why finitely
many computed conditions suffice, and describes how the computations are
reproduced.

11.1. Proved and computed statements
   The structural reductions and the soundness of the certificate checks are
proved in the text. The finite certificate conditions are verified by exact
computation. The proved part includes the reductions of Section 3; the
assignment, matching, and probability estimates; the criteria which state
that certain finite conditions imply prime labelings for all trees of all orders
in a range; and the analytic argument for log n ≥ 256.
   The computed statements are the certificate propositions and theorems
in Table 33. Each of them asserts that explicit finite data satisfy explicit
inequalities or case lists, or follows from such data by rules proved in the
text. Finitely many conditions suffice for the following reasons, each proved
in the text.
    (1) Proof graphs. A finite proof graph for P([n]) has finitely many nodes,
        each obtained from its successors by a rule that is proved sound in
        Theorem 5.1.11 or in Sections 5.2–5.8. The checker verifies every rule
        application and the completeness of every case list.
    (2) Integer intervals. On an interval of orders the residual degree takes
        finitely many values (Lemma 6.6.2), the local failure events are
        covered by finitely many events (for the certificates of Section 6, the
        maximal-support events at the endpoint of Lemma 6.7.1), and every
        other quantity is bounded uniformly over the interval. The finitely
        many rational comparisons of a row therefore imply the hypotheses
                                      313
        of Theorem 6.6.6 or of its variants in Sections 6.9–6.12, or of the
        corresponding integer bridge, at every order of the interval.
    (3) Logarithmic bridges. The quantities of a bridge are bounded mono-
        tonically on closed cells in log n and, for most bridges, a normalized
        residual degree. The rational inequalities of a cell imply the hypothe-
        ses of the bridge at every order in the cell, and the cells cover the
        interval of the bridge.
The argument for log n ≥ 256 uses no certificate. Its numerical steps are
elementary comparisons at finitely many rational points, mostly at log n =
256, stated in Sections 7.4–7.7 and extended to larger orders by monotonicity.

11.2. Search, verification, and additional tests
   Three kinds of computation should be distinguished.
   Search. The parameters of the certificates, such as interval covers, prime
blocks, construction choices, and rational parameters, were found by separate
search programs. These programs are not part of the proof and are not
needed to check it. Their results enter the supplement as fixed input files;
hashes of the research files are recorded only as provenance.
   Verification. For each computed statement, a checker reads the fixed
inputs and recomputes every hypothesis from them with integer and rational
arithmetic. Logarithms, exponentials, and probability bounds are replaced
by directed rational enclosures (Section 6.5), so that each comparison is
decided exactly. Floating-point values occur only in displayed summaries.
Where expected outputs are stored, they are compared with the recomputed
values and are not used as bounds. The interval checkers accept a cover of
orders and degrees only if it is complete; a collection of successful rows is not
enough. For the integer and logarithmic bridges, a wrapper also regenerates
the validation reports and compares them byte for byte with frozen copies.
   Additional tests. The supplement also contains independent recompu-
tations, such as sieves, gcd-based type counts, and second minimization
algorithms, exhaustive tests on small cases, randomized tests with fixed
seeds, and the rejection of malformed inputs. The following checks consist
only of such tests of the helper routines:
     local_probability, height_assignments, donor_assignments,
  finite_arithmetic, directed_probability, rare_type_assignment.
These tests support the correctness of the implementations; the proof does
not use them.
   The programs are not formally verified. Their correctness rests on their
source code, on the description of every checked condition in the text and in
the supplement, and on the independent recomputations.

11.3. Reproduction
   The supplement contains the checkers for the finite proof graphs and the
integer intervals through order 34999 in its top directory, and one directory

for each integer and logarithmic bridge. Its README.md describes each check,
its inputs and its report, and lists the former section numbers that still occur
in file names and reports together with the present subsections. All programs

                                      315
use only the standard library of Python 3. The integer bridges and the first
nine logarithmic bridges also compile a small C++17 program, with clang++
or c++, which enumerates a full period of residues modulo products of small
primes in integer arithmetic.
   The command
                       python3 run_all_checks.py
in the supplement directory copies the supplement to a temporary directory
and runs every documented check there. It prints one line PASS or FAIL for
each check and exits with status 0 exactly when every check passes; a check
passes when its command exits with status 0. The option –list lists the
checks and the option –only selects some of them.

                        Appendix A. Notation
  The following lists give the symbols used in more than one section, with
their meaning, the part of the paper in which that meaning applies, and the
place of definition. Symbols used only inside one section are defined there
and are not listed. Numbers in parentheses refer to displayed equations.

General notation
[n], π(x)           {1, . . . , n}; number of primes at most x. Scope: the
                    whole paper; defined in §1.4.
e(G), NG , dG ,     edge count, neighborhood, degree, largest degree on X
∆G (X)              (zero if X is empty). Scope: the whole paper; defined
                    in §1.4.
P(L), Pd (L)        every forest on |L| vertices (of maximum degree at most
                    d) has a coprime labeling by L. Scope: the whole paper;
                    defined in §1.4.
universal in L      coprime to every other element of L. Scope: the whole
                    paper; defined in §1.4.

Labels and the residual problem

Letters with several meanings
  Several letters have different meanings in different parts of the paper; in
each part the meaning is fixed where it is introduced. The most frequent
cases are the following.


                                       318
     • P is the set of small primes defining the types; in Section 6 it also
       denotes a set of primes above the cutoff, such as P (y), the primes
       above z dividing y. The separator labels are Psep .
     • W is the reserve of positions in Section 3.4, the number minn wn /n
       in Section 6, and a function of log n in the logarithmic bridges.
     • ρ is maxj qj /(1 − qj ) in Section 4.2, (D − 1)/D in Sections 4.3 and 8.3,
       and 1/2 − κ in Section 6.
     • N is the size of a vertex set or label side, defined in each section; Ni
       in Section 9.21 are integer endpoints.
     • w− is the integer minn wn in Section 6 and a lower bound for the
       fraction of special even labels in Sections 8 and 9.
     • Br and Rr are the quantities of Proposition 4.1.3, except in Sec-
       tions 9.16, 9.18, and 9.19, where they are redefined in Section 9.16.
     • D bounds the residual degree, except in Proposition 3.4.3, where
       D ⊆ Z is a set of positions; F is the forest T − K in Section 3.4, the
       full type from Section 4.5 on, and, in Sections 9.2 and 9.4, the set of
       vertices that receive the special even labels; Z0 is the residual position
       set in Section 3.4 and a set of absorbed positions in Section 8.5.

Appendix B. Degree covers of the retained-position certificates
  The following tables give the interval covers of the seven certificates of
Proposition 6.12.2. Each interval carries one row for every degree 1, . . . , D∗ ,
and the intervals of each cover are adjacent. The columns are read downwards.
The rows themselves, with their constructions, prime blocks, and rational
parameters, are part of the supplement (Section 11).

                            321
                             Use of generative AI
   Generative AI systems, principally GPT Astra and Claude Opus, were used
extensively in the development of this work, including mathematical proof
development, computational verification, literature and research suggestions,
drafting, restructuring, and technical consistency review, under the author’s
direction. The author reviewed the mathematical arguments and computa-
tional results presented in the manuscript and takes full responsibility for
the accuracy and integrity of the work.

                               Acknowledgments
  The author acknowledges the extensive assistance of GPT Astra and
Claude Opus in the development and preparation of this manuscript. The
AI systems are not authors.

                         Code and data availability
    The proof of Theorem 1.1 depends on the exact finite computations
described in Section 11. The code, certificate data, fixed inputs, and ver-
ification reports used for these computations are archived in the frozen
computational supplement at doi:10.5281/zenodo.23057222. The supple-
ment includes instructions for reproducing the verification and a wrapper,
run_all_checks.py, which runs the documented checks and reports their
exit status; it is not a separate verifier. Section 11 identifies which mathe-
matical statements depend on these computations and why the finite checks
suffice. Search programs used to discover certificate parameters are not part
of the proof unless explicitly identified otherwise in the supplement.
